<a id="beginner-orientation"></a>
## 🌱 Absolute Beginner Orientation

This curriculum is broad by design, but it should still feel safe to start from zero. Before tools, commands, or labs, hold these simple ideas:

### What Cybersecurity Means

Cybersecurity is the practice of protecting computers, networks, software, data, and people from harm. It is not only about "hackers." It is also about mistakes, weak passwords, unsafe defaults, missing updates, poor design, lost devices, scam messages, and systems nobody is watching.

The basic promise of security is:

- **Confidentiality:** private things stay private.
- **Integrity:** information stays accurate and unmodified unless authorized.
- **Availability:** systems and data are usable when needed.

### What You Are Learning to Protect

You will gradually learn how to protect:

- Your own laptop and phone.
- Accounts, passwords, and MFA.
- Home and public networks.
- Websites and APIs.
- Servers and cloud systems.
- Logs and evidence after something suspicious happens.
- Other people, by practicing ethically and only in legal labs.

### What a Cybersecurity Lab Is

A lab is a safe practice space. In a lab, you can break vulnerable systems legally because they are intentionally made for learning. A lab might be:

- A local vulnerable web app.
- A virtual machine you own.
- A TryHackMe or HackTheBox room.
- A PortSwigger Web Security Academy exercise.
- A small app you built yourself.

Do not practice on random websites, school systems, company systems, or public IPs unless you have written permission.

### How to Learn Without Getting Overwhelmed

Use this rhythm:

1. Learn the normal behavior.
2. Observe it with a tool.
3. Break it safely in a lab.
4. Fix or defend it.
5. Write what happened in your journal.

Cybersecurity rewards patient repetition. If a topic feels familiar, revisit it anyway and ask: "How could this fail, and how would I prove it?"

### Beginner Vocabulary

| Term | Plain meaning |
|---|---|
| Asset | Something valuable, like data, accounts, code, or systems. |
| Threat | Something that could cause harm. |
| Vulnerability | A weakness that can be abused. |
| Exploit | A method used to abuse a weakness. |
| Risk | Likelihood plus impact. |
| Control | A protection that reduces risk. |
| Payload | Input crafted to trigger a behavior. |
| Scope | The exact systems you are allowed to test. |
| Responsible disclosure | Privately reporting a bug to the owner before public discussion. |

<a id="philosophy"></a>
## 🧭 Course Philosophy & How to Use This Curriculum

### The Three Pillars

1. **Theory You Actually Understand** — We don't memorize definitions. We build mental models. If you can't explain it to a 10-year-old, you don't understand it yet.
2. **Hands On, Every Single Module** — There is no theory-only module. You will type commands, break things (in safe labs), and fix them.
3. **Ethics as a Reflex** — Security skills without ethics are a liability. Every module reinforces legal, ethical, and responsible behavior.

### How Each Module is Structured

Every module follows this exact structure so you always know what to expect:

1. 🎯 **Mission Briefing** — Objectives and why this matters in the real world
2. 📘 **Theory** — Explained with analogies, then deeper, then expert-level
3. 🛠️ **Lab: Hands-On Practice** — Mac-first, Windows/Linux notes
4. 🌍 **Case Study** — Real-world incident or scenario
5. 🧩 **Try It Yourself (hidden solutions)** — Challenges you solve first, then reveal
6. ❓ **Self-Assessment Quiz**
7. ✅ **Progress Tracker + Reflection**
8. 🚀 **Next Steps + Optional Bonus Challenges**

### The "Try It Yourself" Format

Throughout the curriculum you'll see challenges like this:

> **Challenge:** Something is asked of you.
>
> <details>
> <summary><strong>🔓 Click to reveal the solution (attempt first!)</strong></summary>
>
> Solution explanation here.
>
> </details>

**If you're reading this in an app that doesn't render collapsible sections**, just cover the "Solution" block with your hand or a sticky note until you've attempted the problem. The discipline of *trying first* is what builds real skill.

<a id="ethics"></a>
## ⚖️ The Code of the Cyber Guardian (Read This Before Anything Else)

Before you learn a single command, you sign (mentally) the Cyber Guardian's Code:

1. **I will only test systems I own or have written permission to test.** Period. No exceptions. No "I was just curious." Unauthorized access to computer systems is a crime in nearly every country (US: Computer Fraud and Abuse Act; UK: Computer Misuse Act 1990; EU: Directive 2013/40; Nigeria: Cybercrime Act 2015; Kenya: Computer Misuse and Cybercrimes Act 2018; and many more).
2. **I will practice in legal sandboxes only** — my own virtual machines, TryHackMe, HackTheBox, OverTheWire, PortSwigger Academy, PicoCTF, etc.
3. **I will use what I learn to defend** — my family, my school, my future employer, my community.
4. **I will disclose responsibly.** If I find a vulnerability in real software, I report it privately to the owner *before* going public.
5. **I will never sell skills to harm others.** The moment a "job" requires hurting a person or organization that didn't consent, I walk away.

> 💭 **Reflection Question:** Think of a time when you had access to something you weren't supposed to use (maybe a sibling's phone, a parent's password). What did you do? Why? Write your answer in your Cyber Journal before continuing.

<a id="tool-download-map"></a>
## 📦 Full Tool Download Map

The setup section below gives the first install commands. This map explains what each tool is for so you do not install a mysterious pile of software.

### Beginner Essentials

| Tool | macOS Apple Silicon | Windows | Why you need it |
|---|---|---|---|
| Package manager | Homebrew | Winget | Installs tools from the terminal. |
| Code editor | VS Code | VS Code | Write notes, scripts, configs, and reports. |
| Browser | Firefox Developer Edition | Firefox Developer Edition | Best browser for web security labs and proxy setup. |
| Password manager | Bitwarden | Bitwarden | Store strong unique passwords safely. |
| Terminal | Terminal/iTerm2 | Windows Terminal | Most security tools are command-line first. |
| Git | `brew install git` | `winget install Git.Git` | Track code, notes, reports, and portfolio projects. |
| Python | `brew install python@3.12` | `winget install Python.Python.3.12` | Scripting and many security tools. |

### Core Security Tools

| Category | Tools | First use |
|---|---|---|
| Network discovery | Nmap | Learn ports, services, and host discovery in legal labs. |
| Packet analysis | Wireshark | See what network traffic looks like. |
| Web testing | Burp Suite Community, OWASP ZAP | Inspect and modify your own web requests. |
| Local labs | Docker Desktop, VMware Fusion Pro 13+, UTM | Run vulnerable apps and VMs safely. |
| Linux practice | Kali Linux ARM64, Ubuntu Server ARM64 | Practice attacker/defender workflows. |
| Password/hash labs | John the Ripper, hashcat | Understand password cracking defensively and legally. |
| Vulnerability checks | Nuclei, Nikto | Learn scanner output and false positives. |
| Code/security scanning | Semgrep, Gitleaks, Trivy | Find insecure code, secrets, dependencies, and container risks. |

### Mac M-Series Notes

For Apple Silicon Macs like M1/M2/M3:

- Prefer ARM64-native downloads.
- Prefer Docker images that support `linux/arm64`.
- Use VMware Fusion Pro 13+ as the polished VM option for Apple Silicon, with UTM as the free/open-source fallback.
- Use Docker Desktop first for vulnerable web apps when a container is available.
- Some classic old vulnerable VMs were built for Intel/x86 and may be slow or incompatible.
- If a lab fights your chip for hours, switch to Juice Shop, WebGoat, PortSwigger, or TryHackMe instead of losing momentum.

### Recommended Mac Install Bundle

```bash
brew install git gh tree jq yq wget curl httpie ripgrep fd bat fzf htop tmux
brew install node pnpm python@3.12 pipx go
brew install nmap nikto sqlmap ffuf nuclei semgrep trivy gitleaks
brew install --cask visual-studio-code iterm2 firefox@developer-edition bitwarden wireshark burp-suite owasp-zap docker utm
```

Install VMware Fusion Pro manually from Broadcom/VMware's official download portal if you want the most polished VM platform on Mac M-series. Use Fusion Pro 13 or newer, and choose ARM64/Apple Silicon Linux images such as Kali Apple Silicon or Ubuntu Server ARM64.

### Recommended Windows Install Bundle

```powershell
winget install Microsoft.VisualStudioCode Mozilla.Firefox Git.Git Python.Python.3.12
winget install WiresharkFoundation.Wireshark PortSwigger.BurpSuite.Community OWASP.ZAP
winget install Docker.DockerDesktop Bitwarden.Bitwarden
```

### Install Slowly, Learn Immediately

Do not install every advanced tool and then never open them. Install in waves:

1. First week: VS Code, Firefox, Bitwarden, Git, Python, Docker.
2. Web security week: Burp Suite, OWASP ZAP, Juice Shop.
3. Network week: Nmap, Wireshark.
4. Code security week: Semgrep, Gitleaks, Trivy.
5. Advanced practice: Kali/Ubuntu VMs, Nuclei, Nikto, password tools.

<a id="setup"></a>
## 🛠️ Environment Setup: Your Digital Lab

Before Level 1, you'll set up a workbench. This takes ~2–3 hours and is the one and only "boring" section of the course. Do it carefully; every future module depends on it.

### Setup Comparison Table

| Tool | macOS (Primary) | Windows | Linux (Ubuntu) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Package Manager** | Homebrew (`brew`) | Winget / Chocolatey | `apt` (built-in) |
| **Terminal** | Built-in Terminal or iTerm2 | PowerShell / Windows Terminal | Built-in Terminal |
| **Virtualization** | VMware Fusion Pro 13+ or UTM (Apple Silicon); VirtualBox mainly for Intel | VirtualBox or VMware Workstation Pro/Player | VirtualBox or KVM |
| **Code Editor** | VS Code | VS Code | VS Code |
| **Browser for Security** | Firefox Developer Edition | Firefox Developer Edition | Firefox Developer Edition |
| **Password Manager** | Bitwarden | Bitwarden | Bitwarden |
| **Network Analyzer** | Wireshark | Wireshark | Wireshark |

### Step 1: Install Homebrew (macOS) / Package Manager

**macOS:**
```bash
/bin/bash -c "$(curl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Homebrew/install/HEAD/install.sh)"
```

After it finishes, add Homebrew to your PATH (the installer will tell you the exact command — follow it).

**Windows (PowerShell as Admin):**
```powershell
winget install --id Git.Git -e
```

**Linux (Ubuntu/Debian):**
```bash
sudo apt update && sudo apt upgrade -y
```

### Step 2: Install the Core Toolkit

**macOS (one command):**
```bash
brew install --cask visual-studio-code firefox wireshark utm bitwarden
brew install nmap git python@3.12 tree htop
```

**Windows:**
```powershell
winget install Microsoft.VisualStudioCode Mozilla.Firefox WiresharkFoundation.Wireshark Oracle.VirtualBox Bitwarden.Bitwarden Insecure.Nmap Git.Git Python.Python.3.12
```

**Linux:**
```bash
sudo apt install -y code firefox wireshark virtualbox nmap git python3 python3-pip
sudo snap install bitwarden
```

### Step 3: Create Your Cyber Journal

Open VS Code. Create a folder called `cyber-guardians-journal/`. Inside it, create `README.md`. You'll use this to track everything — notes, commands learned, reflections, CTF writeups. This becomes your portfolio.

### Step 4: Download Your First Victim VM (For Later)

Bookmark these — you'll download VMs in Level 2:

- Kali Linux — https://www.kali.org/get-kali/ (your attacker machine)
- Metasploitable 2 — intentionally vulnerable target (for lab practice ONLY)
- DVWA (Damn Vulnerable Web App) — web security practice

### ✅ Setup Checklist

- [ ] Package manager installed and working
- [ ] VS Code opens and runs
- [ ] Terminal runs a command and shows output
- [ ] Firefox installed with at least one extension (uBlock Origin)
- [ ] Password manager set up with a strong master password you actually remember
- [ ] Cyber Journal folder created in VS Code
- [ ] You can answer: "What's the difference between a package manager and an app store?"

> <details>
> <summary><strong>🔓 Answer</strong></summary>
>
> An app store is a curated, often paid, consumer-friendly marketplace. A package manager is a command-line tool that installs software + all its dependencies (other software it needs) automatically, usually from open-source repositories, and is designed for developers and power users who want reproducibility and automation.
>
> </details>

## Module 0.5 — Command-Line Field Guide

### 🎯 Mission Briefing
Modules 1 and 2 have you run `openssl`, `shasum`, `dig`, `traceroute`, `nc`, and `openssl s_client` — powerful commands that feel like magic if the first time you meet them is *inside* a security lab. This module front-loads exactly those commands, one at a time, in a **what → run it → exact output** shape, so nothing in the next two modules is a first encounter. Module 7 (Command Line Fundamentals) later dissects the shell in depth; this is the just-enough field guide to get you there without fear.

### 📘 Theory

#### Why this module exists

Here's a problem hiding in most beginner security courses: the very first labs make you type `openssl s_client`, `dig`, and `traceroute` — powerful commands — *six modules before* the one that actually explains the shell. So the commands feel like spells. This module fixes that. It's a **field guide**: every command Modules 1 and 2 use, taught once here in isolation, so that when you meet it in a lab you're *reading*, not decoding.

You do **not** need to memorize this. You need to have *seen each piece once*. Every entry below follows the same shape: **what it does → a command you can run → the exact output you'll see.**

> **Cross-platform note.** These commands are written for a **bash/zsh** shell — macOS Terminal, any Linux terminal, or **WSL2** on Windows. If you're on Windows, run them inside WSL2 (set up in Module 0) and everything below works unchanged.

#### The three pieces of shell grammar you need first

**Redirect `>` and append `>>`.** `>` sends a command's output *into a file* (replacing it); `>>` adds to the end.

```bash
echo "hello" > note.txt      # write "hello" into note.txt (overwrites)
echo "world" >> note.txt     # add "world" on a new line
cat note.txt                 # cat = show a file's contents
```

**Pipe `|`.** Sends the output of the command on the left *into* the command on the right, so small tools chain together — the heart of the shell.

```bash
ls /usr/bin | wc -l          # list programs, then COUNT the lines -> e.g. 1043
```

Read it as: "list the files, and feed that list into `wc -l`, which counts lines." You'll build every investigation this way — each stage narrows the data.

#### 🔐 openssl enc — encrypt a file (Module 1 uses this)

`openssl` is a Swiss-army knife for cryptography; `enc` means "encrypt/decrypt." Give it a password and it scrambles a file with AES-256:

```bash
echo "my secret grade: A+" > secret.txt
openssl enc -aes-256-cbc -pbkdf2 -salt -in secret.txt -out secret.enc   # prompts for a password
cat secret.enc            # <- unreadable gibberish: confidentiality holds
openssl enc -d -aes-256-cbc -pbkdf2 -in secret.enc -out back.txt && cat back.txt   # -d decrypts
```

#### 🔎 shasum / sha256sum & openssl dgst — fingerprint a file (Modules 1 & 3)

A **hash** is a fixed-length fingerprint of a file. Change one byte and the fingerprint changes completely — that's how you *prove integrity*.

```bash
echo "important data" > data.txt
shasum -a 256 data.txt          # macOS spelling; Linux/WSL2: sha256sum data.txt
```

> **Same tool, two names.** `shasum -a 256` (macOS) and `sha256sum` (Linux/WSL2) give identical output; `openssl dgst -sha256 data.txt` works everywhere. Change one character in the file, re-run, and watch the entire hash change — the tamper-detection you'll build in Module 1.

#### 🌐 dig — turn a name into an IP address (Module 2)

`dig` asks DNS "what IP is behind this name?" — the phone-book lookup the internet runs on constantly.

```bash
dig +short example.com          # -> 93.184.216.34   (+short trims it to just the answer)
```

#### 🛰️ traceroute — see the path your packets take (Module 2)

`traceroute` shows every router ("hop") between you and a destination — proof your data crosses many machines you don't control. (On Windows the command is `tracert`.)

```bash
traceroute -m 8 example.com     # -m 8 = stop after 8 hops so it returns fast
```

Each numbered line is one router; `* * *` just means a hop didn't reply — normal.

#### 🔌 nc (netcat) — open a raw connection (Module 2)

`nc` opens a raw TCP connection so you can *see* a server talk. Here it fetches a page by hand, proving HTTP is just text:

```bash
printf 'GET / HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: example.com\r\nConnection: close\r\n\r\n' | nc example.com 80
```

Because this is port 80 (plain HTTP), everything travels as readable text — the exact point Module 2 drives home.

#### 🔒 openssl s_client — inspect a TLS certificate (Module 2)

Where `nc` shows plaintext, `openssl s_client` opens an *encrypted* TLS connection and shows the server's certificate — its cryptographic ID card:

```bash
echo | openssl s_client -connect example.com:443 -servername example.com 2>/dev/null \
  | openssl x509 -noout -subject -issuer -dates
```

That block says *who* the certificate is for, *who vouched for it*, and *how long it's valid* — the trust chain behind the padlock. Module 2 walks through reading it.

#### You're ready

Every command in the next two modules now has a home. When Module 1 says `openssl enc` or Module 2 says `openssl s_client`, you'll recognize it instead of meeting it cold. Keep this page as a reference — and know that Module 7 will take the whole shell apart in depth once you're fluent enough to appreciate it.

### 🛠️ Lab: Field-Guide Warm-Up (M2 / Apple Silicon)

> ⚖️ **Ethics box.** Everything here touches only files you create and public sites that exist for exactly this purpose (`example.com`, `neverssl.com`). Nothing you don't own, no network you're not invited onto.

```bash
mkdir -p ~/cyber-journal/m0-5 && cd ~/cyber-journal/m0-5

# 1) Redirects and a pipe
echo "hello" > note.txt && echo "world" >> note.txt
cat note.txt
ls /usr/bin | wc -l                       # count the programs on your machine

# 2) Encrypt and decrypt a file (Module 1 preview)
echo "my secret grade: A+" > secret.txt
openssl enc -aes-256-cbc -pbkdf2 -salt -in secret.txt -out secret.enc   # choose a password
cat secret.enc                            # gibberish = confidentiality
openssl enc -d -aes-256-cbc -pbkdf2 -in secret.enc -out back.txt && cat back.txt

# 3) Fingerprint, tamper, re-fingerprint (Module 1 preview)
echo "important data" > data.txt
shasum -a 256 data.txt                    # Linux/WSL2: sha256sum data.txt
echo "important datax" > data.txt         # change ONE character
shasum -a 256 data.txt                     # the whole hash changes = tamper detected

# 4) Name -> IP, and the path there (Module 2 preview)
dig +short example.com
traceroute -m 8 example.com

# 5) Speak HTTP by hand, then inspect a TLS cert (Module 2 preview)
printf 'GET / HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: example.com\r\nConnection: close\r\n\r\n' | nc example.com 80 | head
echo | openssl s_client -connect example.com:443 -servername example.com 2>/dev/null \
  | openssl x509 -noout -subject -issuer -dates
```

**Windows (WSL2):** run the identical commands inside your WSL2 Ubuntu shell, using `sha256sum` in place of `shasum -a 256`. On native PowerShell the closest equivalents are `Get-FileHash` (for hashing) and `Resolve-DnsName` (for `dig`), but WSL2 keeps everything above working unchanged.

### 🌍 Real-World Case Study — Why the commands feel like magic (and why that's dangerous)

Ask ten self-taught beginners how TLS works and many will confidently paste an `openssl s_client` command they copied from a tutorial — but they can't tell you what a single line of the output means, or why the `issuer` field matters. That's the trap of learning tools before concepts: you can run the spell but you can't reason about it, so you can't tell a real certificate warning from a fake one, or notice when a lab's output is wrong. This tiny field-guide module exists to break that pattern early. By running each command once, in isolation, with the output explained, you build the one thing copy-paste never gives you: the ability to *read* what the tool is telling you.

### 🧪 Try It Yourself

1. **You run `shasum -a 256 report.pdf` today and write down the result. A week later you get a completely different value on the same file. What are the two likely explanations, and which is a security concern?**
   <details><summary>Answer</summary>Either (1) the file genuinely changed — someone edited or replaced it, which if unexpected is an integrity/tampering concern worth investigating — or (2) you're hashing a different file than you think (wrong directory, a re-downloaded copy). A hash only changes when the input bytes change, so an unexplained change on a file you believed was static is exactly the tamper signal Module 1 teaches you to trust.</details>
2. **Both `nc example.com 80` and `openssl s_client -connect example.com:443` reach the same site. Why does only one let someone on your Wi-Fi read your traffic?**
   <details><summary>Answer</summary>Port 80 is plain HTTP — `nc` sends and receives readable text, so anyone on the network path can see it. Port 443 with `openssl s_client` negotiates TLS first, encrypting everything after the handshake, so an eavesdropper sees only scrambled bytes. That plaintext-vs-encrypted difference is the whole reason Module 2 insists you never send anything sensitive over http://.</details>

### ✅ Quiz
1. What does the pipe `|` do, in one sentence, and what does `ls /usr/bin | wc -l` count?
2. What is the difference between `>` and `>>`?
3. What is a hash, and why does changing one character of a file change the whole hash?
4. Which command turns a domain name into an IP address, and which shows the routers your packets pass through?
5. Why can someone on your Wi-Fi read a plain `nc ... 80` HTTP request but not an `openssl s_client ... :443` TLS session?

### 📝 Progress Tracker
- [ ] Ran the redirect/append/pipe examples and can read `ls | wc -l` in plain English
- [ ] Encrypted and decrypted a file I own with `openssl enc`
- [ ] Fingerprinted a file with `shasum`/`sha256sum`, tampered with it, and watched the hash change
- [ ] Used `dig` to resolve a name to an IP and `traceroute` to see the hops
- [ ] Spoke HTTP by hand with `nc` and inspected a TLS certificate with `openssl s_client`
- [ ] Understand that every command here reappears in Modules 1–2 — and is dissected fully in Module 7

### ➡️ Next Step
Module 1 — **The CIA Triad**. You now recognize every command the first labs use, so you can focus on the *ideas* — confidentiality, integrity, availability — instead of fighting the terminal.

# 🎖️ LEVEL 1 — RECRUIT (Foundation)
### Weeks 1–8 | Goal: Build rock-solid mental models

You finish Level 1 able to explain how the internet works, why passwords fail, what malware is, and how to secure your own devices. Think of this as "digital literacy, but with security x-ray vision."

> ### 🗺️ Level 1 Roadmap — Recruit · Weeks 1–8
> **The promise:** Level 1 builds the mental models everything else hangs on — the CIA triad, how the internet really works, passwords and malware, and how to harden your own machine — so no later tool ever feels like magic.
>
> **By the end you can —**
> - Map any tool, attack, or defense to the CIA property it protects or breaks
> - Explain how the internet moves a request from your browser to a server and back
> - Reason about why passwords fail and how hashing, salting, and MFA fix it
> - Harden your own OS and work fluently at the command line
>
> **You'll build:** a personal security audit of your own devices and accounts (Level 1 capstone, Module 8).
> **Prerequisite:** basic computer use — no security background needed.

## Module 1 — Welcome to the Cyber World: The CIA Triad

### 🎯 Mission Briefing
Every single concept in cybersecurity ladders up to three goals: **Confidentiality, Integrity, Availability** — the CIA Triad. If you can't map a concept back to one of these, you don't understand it yet. By the end of this module you will be able to look at any tool, attack, or defense and instantly say *which property it protects or breaks, and why*. That single reflex is the spine of everything that follows.

### 📘 Theory

#### First, define the words

Before the triad, define the words it is built from, because most beginners use them loosely and then get confused later.

- **Asset** — anything worth protecting: data, an account, a device, a service, even a person's reputation.
- **Security property** — a *promise* you want to keep about an asset. "Only the right people can read this" is a promise. The CIA Triad is just the three most fundamental promises.
- **Threat** — a thing that could break a promise (a person, a piece of malware, a flood, a fat-fingered admin).
- **Vulnerability** — a weakness that lets a threat actually break the promise.
- **Control** — anything you do to keep the promise (a password, encryption, a backup, a lock on a door).

The CIA Triad is the answer to the question: *"What promises are we even trying to keep?"* Everything else — passwords, firewalls, encryption, logging — exists to keep one of these three promises.

#### Confidentiality, Integrity, Availability

- **Confidentiality** — only authorized parties can *read* the asset. Broken when a secret is exposed.
- **Integrity** — the asset is *accurate and unaltered* unless an authorized party changed it. Broken when data is tampered with (even if nobody reads it).
- **Availability** — the asset is *usable when needed*. Broken when the asset is destroyed, locked, or made too slow to use.

#### Analogy — your bedroom diary

- **Confidentiality** — the diary is locked; only you can read it.
- **Integrity** — no one sneaks in and rewrites a page while you sleep.
- **Availability** — the diary is on your desk when you want it, not buried under laundry or thrown in a river.

Now scale that up to Google, your bank, a hospital:

- Confidentiality breach → your medical records leak online.
- Integrity breach → someone silently changes your bank balance.
- Availability breach → the hospital is locked by ransomware during surgery.

#### Deriving the triad from first principles — why exactly three?

You might ask: *why these three and not five, or one?* Here is the derivation. Imagine a single piece of data — say, your exam grade — and ask what an adversary could do to harm you with it. There are exactly three fundamental verbs:

1. They could **read** it when they shouldn't → that is the *confidentiality* failure.
2. They could **change** it when they shouldn't → that is the *integrity* failure.
3. They could **stop you from using** it → that is the *availability* failure.

Reading, changing, denying. Every attack on a piece of data reduces to one (or a combination) of these three verbs. That is *why* the triad has exactly three legs — it is not a list someone made up, it is the complete set of bad outcomes for data. This is your first taste of *first-principles* thinking: instead of memorizing a list, you derive the list from "what could go wrong?"

#### The naive mental model — and why it fails

> **The naive model:** "Security means stopping hackers from stealing data." (Confidentiality only.)

This fails because it ignores two-thirds of the actual risk:

- It misses **integrity**: the 2010 Stuxnet worm did not steal a single file — it *changed* control signals so Iranian centrifuges tore themselves apart while the dashboards showed "normal." Nothing was leaked. Enormous damage.
- It misses **availability**: ransomware usually does not read your files — it *encrypts* them so *you* cannot read them, then sells you the key. The 2021 Colonial Pipeline attack shut down half the US East Coast's fuel supply without exposing a single customer record.

A defender who only thinks "don't let them steal it" will leave the door wide open to tampering and denial-of-service. That is why we replace the naive one-legged model with the three-legged triad on day one.

#### The hidden fourth concept: non-repudiation

**Non-repudiation** means a party *cannot credibly deny* having performed an action. If you sign a contract digitally, you cannot later claim "that wasn't me." This is built from cryptographic **digital signatures** and tamper-evident **logs**. It is not part of the classic triad, but it matters enormously in legal, financial, and forensic contexts — and we will build it for real in Module 10 (Cryptography). For now, just know it exists and that it is what turns "the system thinks you did X" into "we can *prove* you did X."

### 🛠️ Lab: Prove the Triad With Your Own Files (M2 / Apple Silicon)

> ⚖️ **Ethics box — read every time.** This lab runs **only against a file you create in your own home folder**. You are not touching anyone else's data, any network, or any system you do not own. That is the whole point: you can safely *break* each CIA property when the asset is yours. Never run integrity/availability "attacks" on files you do not own.

You are going to *experience* each property breaking, on a file you own, using ARM64-native tools that ship with macOS or install cleanly via Homebrew on your M2 Pro.

**Step 0 — make a sandbox and a victim file:**

```bash
mkdir -p ~/cyber-journal/m1-cia && cd ~/cyber-journal/m1-cia
echo "Final grade: A+. Account balance: 100." > secret.txt
cat secret.txt
```

**Confidentiality — lock it, then prove the lock works.** macOS Apple Silicon includes the LibreSSL `openssl` binary, which is native ARM64 (verify with `file $(which openssl)` → it reports `arm64`).

```bash
# Encrypt the file with a password (AES-256). This is a CONFIDENTIALITY control.
openssl enc -aes-256-cbc -pbkdf2 -salt -in secret.txt -out secret.enc
# Now prove the plaintext is unreadable in the encrypted blob:
strings secret.enc | grep -i "grade"   # <- returns NOTHING. The secret is not readable.
cat secret.enc                          # <- gibberise. Confidentiality holds.
# Decrypt to recover it (the authorized action):
openssl enc -d -aes-256-cbc -pbkdf2 -in secret.enc -out recovered.txt && cat recovered.txt
```

*What you observed:* before encryption, `cat` reveals the secret (confidentiality is absent). After encryption, the secret cannot be read without the password (confidentiality is enforced by a control). You just built a confidentiality control with your own hands.

**Integrity — detect tampering with a hash.** A **cryptographic hash** is a fixed-length fingerprint of a file; change one byte and the fingerprint changes completely (the "avalanche effect").

```bash
# Record the fingerprint of the good file (INTEGRITY baseline):
shasum -a 256 secret.txt | tee good.sha256

# Now an "attacker" (you) tampers with one character:
sed -i '' 's/A+/F-/' secret.txt   # macOS BSD sed needs the '' after -i
cat secret.txt                     # the grade changed!

# Detect the tampering by re-checking the fingerprint:
shasum -a 256 -c good.sha256       # <- prints "secret.txt: FAILED". Integrity violation DETECTED.
```

*What you observed:* nobody had to *see* the change for you to *prove* it happened. That is integrity: not "nobody altered it" but "if they did, we will know." Defenders hash critical files and re-check them; this is exactly how tools like AIDE and Tripwire catch tampering.

**Availability — break it, then see why backups exist.**

```bash
# Simulate ransomware/loss by destroying the only copy (AVAILABILITY attack):
rm secret.txt
cat secret.txt   # <- "No such file or directory". The asset is GONE. Availability = 0.

# This is why availability is a separate promise: the data was never read or altered.
# It was simply made unusable. Recover from the encrypted copy you made earlier:
openssl enc -d -aes-256-cbc -pbkdf2 -in secret.enc -out secret.txt && cat secret.txt
```

*What you observed:* a backup (even your earlier encrypted copy) *is* an availability control. No backup, no recovery. This is the entire lesson of ransomware in one command.

**Windows (PowerShell) equivalents:**

```powershell
# Confidentiality: hash + EFS/Bitlocker conceptually; quick hash for integrity:
"Final grade: A+. Balance 100" | Out-File secret.txt
Get-FileHash secret.txt -Algorithm SHA256   # baseline
# tamper:
(Get-Content secret.txt) -replace "A\+","F-" | Set-Content secret.txt
Get-FileHash secret.txt -Algorithm SHA256   # different hash = integrity broken
Remove-Item secret.txt                        # availability broken
```

**Linux equivalents:** identical to macOS but use GNU `sed -i 's/A+/F-/' secret.txt` (no `''`) and `sha256sum` instead of `shasum -a 256`.

### 🌍 Case Study — The 2021 Colonial Pipeline Attack

Ransomware (deployed by the DarkSide group) hit Colonial Pipeline, the largest refined-fuel pipeline on the US East Coast. The attackers did **not** destroy or steal the operational data in the attack that caused the outage — they *encrypted* business systems, and Colonial proactively shut down pipeline operations to contain the spread. **Availability** collapsed: fuel shortages across 17 states, panic buying, and a $4.4M ransom paid (about half was later recovered by the FBI). The entry point? **One leaked VPN password, on an account with no multi-factor authentication.** One credential. That is the whole story.

Map it to the triad: the *primary* damage was availability. But notice the **chain** — a confidentiality failure (a leaked credential) led to an availability failure (the shutdown). Real incidents almost always cross legs of the triad. Training your eye to trace that chain is the skill this module builds.

### 🧩 Try It Yourself

**Challenge 1:** Name one real-world event that was primarily an *Integrity* attack — not Confidentiality or Availability.

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal example answer</strong></summary>

**Stuxnet (2010)** is the textbook case. The worm altered the control signals sent to Iranian uranium centrifuges so they spun themselves to destruction, while *simultaneously* replaying earlier "normal" readings to the operators' monitors. No data was stolen (confidentiality intact) and the systems stayed up (availability intact) — the harm was entirely from *silently changing trusted data*. Other valid answers: deepfake videos presented as real, tampered scientific datasets, or banking malware that changes the destination account number on a wire transfer.

</details>

**Challenge 2:** You hashed `secret.txt` and stored the hash in `good.sha256` *in the same folder*. An attacker who can edit `secret.txt` can probably also edit `good.sha256`. What does that tell you about where integrity baselines must live?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

It tells you a control is only as trustworthy as its *weakest dependency*. If the attacker can rewrite both the file and its fingerprint, the fingerprint proves nothing — they just regenerate it after tampering. Real integrity systems store baselines somewhere the attacker cannot reach: a read-only volume, a separate logging server, a signed manifest, or a hardware security module. This is your first **false-confidence trap**: a security check that the attacker also controls is theater, not security.

</details>

### 🛡️ Pitfalls & False-Confidence Traps

- **"We encrypt everything, so we're secure."** Encryption is a *confidentiality* control. It does nothing for availability (ransomware *uses* encryption against you) and little for integrity unless you specifically use authenticated encryption. One leg is not three.
- **"Nobody read the data, so there was no breach."** Tampering (integrity) and denial (availability) are breaches too. Regulators and customers care about all three.
- **The self-validating check.** As in Challenge 2 — a hash, log, or alert that the attacker can also modify gives *false confidence*. Always ask: "Who controls the thing that's supposed to catch the attacker?"
- **Confusing the verb.** People say "they hacked us" without naming which property failed. Force yourself to say read / changed / denied. The verb dictates the defense.

### ❓ Quiz

1. Which CIA property is violated when a website is knocked offline by a DDoS?
2. A hospital's patient records are leaked online. Which property failed — and could a *second* property fail in the same incident?
3. Can a single attack violate all three at once? Give a concrete example.
4. You hash a file and store the hash next to it. Why is that weak, and where should the hash live instead?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Answers</strong></summary>

1. **Availability** — the asset is intact and unread, but unusable.
2. **Confidentiality** failed (records were read by unauthorized parties). A second property can also fail: if the attacker *also altered* records, that is an integrity failure; if they deleted them, availability. Real incidents cross legs.
3. **Yes.** Example: an attacker breaks into a database, copies it (confidentiality), changes some records to cover their tracks (integrity), then deletes the originals and locks you out (availability). Stuxnet and many ransomware-with-exfiltration ("double extortion") campaigns hit multiple legs.
4. It is weak because anyone who can alter the file can alter the adjacent hash and regenerate it — the check validates itself. Store the baseline somewhere the attacker cannot reach: a read-only/append-only store, a separate server, or a signed manifest.

</details>

### ✅ Progress Tracker
- [ ] I can explain Confidentiality, Integrity, and Availability to a 10-year-old
- [ ] I derived *why* there are exactly three legs (read / change / deny)
- [ ] I ran the lab and personally broke each property on my own file
- [ ] I can trace the cross-property *chain* in a real incident (e.g., Colonial Pipeline)
- [ ] I can spot a self-validating "false confidence" check

### ➡️ Next Step
Module 2 — **How the Internet Actually Works**. Now that you know *what* you are protecting (the three promises), you need to understand the *medium* every attack and defense travels through: the network. You cannot protect confidentiality in transit until you know what "in transit" even means.

## Module 2 — How the Internet Actually Works (Without the Hand-Waving)

### 🎯 Mission Briefing
Most security professionals cannot clearly explain what happens when you type `google.com`. You will, by the end of this module — and you will *watch it happen* with real tools on your own machine. Networking ignorance is the single biggest ceiling on a security career, because **every attack and every defense travels over a network**. You cannot protect data "in transit" until you understand what "in transit" actually means.

### 📘 Theory

#### Define the words first

- **IP address** — a numeric address for a machine on a network, e.g. `142.250.80.46` (IPv4) or `2607:f8b0::200e` (IPv6). Like a street address: it says *where*, not *who*.
- **Domain name** — a human-friendly label, e.g. `google.com`, that *maps to* an IP address. Like a contact name that resolves to a phone number.
- **DNS (Domain Name System)** — the global, distributed directory that translates names to IP addresses.
- **Port** — a numbered "door" on a machine (0–65535). The IP gets you to the building; the port gets you to the right office (web = 443, email = 25, etc.). Covered in depth in Module 9.
- **Packet** — a small chunk of data with a "from" address, "to" address, and a piece of the message — like a postcard. Big messages are split into many packets.
- **Protocol** — an agreed-upon set of rules for how two machines talk (HTTP, TCP, TLS, DNS are all protocols).
- **Client / Server** — the client *initiates* a request (your browser); the server *responds* (Google's machine).

#### What really happens when you type `google.com` and hit Enter

1. **DNS resolution.** Your computer asks a DNS resolver, "What is the IP for `google.com`?" The resolver may ask several servers (root → `.com` → Google's nameservers) and returns an IP like `142.250.80.46`. *Naive assumption: this is one quick lookup.* In reality it is a recursive chain, it is cached at many layers, and — critically for security — **classic DNS is unencrypted**, so anyone on your network can see (and historically, forge) which sites you visit.

2. **TCP handshake.** Your computer and the server perform a **three-way handshake**: `SYN` ("can we talk?") → `SYN-ACK` ("yes, can you hear me?") → `ACK` ("yes, talking now"). This establishes a reliable, ordered connection. **TCP (Transmission Control Protocol)** guarantees delivery and order; **UDP (User Datagram Protocol)** does not (it is fire-and-forget, used for DNS, video, games).

3. **TLS handshake (the padlock).** **TLS (Transport Layer Security)** negotiates an encrypted tunnel: the server presents a **certificate** proving its identity, both sides agree on keys, and from here on everything is encrypted. Without TLS, every byte — passwords, cookies, messages — is readable by anyone between you and the server.

4. **HTTP request.** Inside the encrypted tunnel, your browser sends an **HTTP (HyperText Transfer Protocol)** request: `GET /` ("give me the homepage"), plus headers (your browser type, cookies, etc.).

5. **Response and render.** The server returns HTML, CSS, and JavaScript; your browser renders the page, often firing dozens more requests for images, fonts, and scripts — each its own mini-version of steps 1–5.

#### Why the naive "it's just one connection to Google" model fails

Beginners picture a single wire from their laptop to Google. The reality matters for security:

- Your packets cross **many** intermediate routers (your home router → ISP → peering points → Google's edge → Google's data center). **Any** of them can see unencrypted traffic. This is *why* TLS exists.
- The **name lookup (DNS) and the data transfer (TCP/TLS) are separate steps with separate weaknesses.** You can have perfect HTTPS and still leak which sites you visit via plaintext DNS. You can also be sent to the *wrong* IP entirely if DNS is poisoned. Treating it as "one connection" hides both attack surfaces.
- Packets can take **different routes** and arrive **out of order**; TCP reassembles them. An attacker who can inject or reorder packets (a "man-in-the-middle") attacks at this layer.

#### The OSI model (preview — deep dive in Module 9)

Networks are described as 7 stacked layers. Mnemonic: *"Please Do Not Throw Sausage Pizza Away"* — Physical, Data Link, Network, Transport, Session, Presentation, Application. The value of layers: each attack and each defense lives at a specific layer, and naming the layer tells you which tool to reach for. A DDoS can hit layer 3/4 (flood the network) or layer 7 (flood the app); the defense differs.

### 🛠️ Lab: Watch the Internet Work (M2 / Apple Silicon)

> ⚖️ **Ethics box.** Every command here observes *your own* traffic and queries *public* services that explicitly allow it (`google.com`, your own DNS). You are not attacking anyone. Do not run captures on networks you do not own or have permission to monitor.

All of these tools are native ARM64 on macOS (`dig`, `traceroute`, `nc`, `openssl` ship with the OS or LibreSSL).

```bash
# 1. Your own IP on the local network
ipconfig getifaddr en0      # Wi-Fi address on most M-series Macs
ifconfig | grep "inet "      # all interfaces

# 2. Who are you talking to RIGHT NOW (established connections)?
netstat -an | grep ESTABLISHED | head

# 3. DNS — resolve a name to an IP, and SEE the resolution path
dig google.com +short                 # just the answer IPs
dig google.com +trace | head -40      # the FULL chain: root -> .com -> google NS
scutil --dns | grep "nameserver\[0\]"  # which DNS server you're using

# 4. Trace the physical path your packets take (count the hops!)
traceroute google.com

# 5. Watch the TCP handshake + TLS cert with one command
echo | openssl s_client -connect google.com:443 -servername google.com 2>/dev/null \
  | openssl x509 -noout -subject -issuer -dates
# You just saw: a TCP connect, a TLS handshake, and the server's certificate identity.

# 6. PROVE plaintext is readable without TLS (your own request to a deliberately-HTTP site)
#    neverssl.com exists precisely so you can do this safely.
printf 'GET / HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: neverssl.com\r\nConnection: close\r\n\r\n' | nc neverssl.com 80 | head -20
# Everything you sent and received is plaintext. On a hostile network, so are your cookies.
```

**Windows (PowerShell):**
```powershell
ipconfig
netstat -an | findstr ESTABLISHED
Resolve-DnsName google.com
tracert google.com
Test-NetConnection google.com -Port 443
```

**Linux:**
```bash
ip addr
ss -tn state established
dig google.com +short
dig google.com +trace | head -40
traceroute google.com
```

*What to record in your journal:* (a) how many hops to Google, (b) the certificate's issuer, (c) whether you could read the `neverssl.com` response in plaintext. These three observations make TLS feel real instead of abstract.

### 🌍 Case Study — The 2016 Dyn DNS Attack
The Mirai botnet — hundreds of thousands of hijacked IoT devices (cameras, DVRs) using **default passwords** — flooded Dyn, a major DNS provider, with junk traffic. Because DNS is *phase 1* of loading any site, Twitter, Spotify, Netflix, Reddit, and others were **up** but **unreachable** — nobody could resolve their names to IPs. Half the US internet felt broken for hours.

Map it to what you just learned: this was an **availability** attack (Module 1) at the **DNS layer**. The lesson: critical infrastructure has **invisible dependencies**. Your beautifully hardened HTTPS site is useless if the name lookup in front of it goes down — and the weapon was built from devices whose owners never changed the default password (foreshadowing Module 3).

### 🧩 Try It Yourself

**Challenge 1:** Run `traceroute google.com`. Count the hops. Where is hop 1? Where is the last hop?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 What you're looking at</strong></summary>

- Hop 1 is almost always your home router (`192.168.x.1` or `10.0.0.1`).
- The next few hops are your ISP.
- Then you cross "peering points" where ISPs exchange traffic.
- Eventually you reach Google's network; the final hop's IP belongs to Google.

Hops showing `* * *` are routers configured not to answer traceroute — totally normal, not an error.

</details>

**Challenge 2:** You ran step 6 and read the `neverssl.com` response in plaintext. Now imagine that was a login form on coffee-shop Wi-Fi. List exactly what an attacker on the same Wi-Fi could capture, and the *one* change that defeats them.

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

Over plaintext HTTP, anyone on the same network (or any router in between) can read the **full URL, all headers, cookies/session tokens, and the POSTed username and password** — and can even *modify* the page in flight (inject scripts). The one change that defeats them: **HTTPS (TLS)**, which encrypts the entire request/response so intermediaries see only *that* you talked to the host, not *what* you said. This is why "there's no login, I don't need HTTPS" is wrong — see the quiz.

</details>

### 🛡️ Pitfalls & False-Confidence Traps

- **"I have HTTPS, so I'm private."** HTTPS hides the *content*, but classic **DNS still leaks which sites you visit** to your network/ISP. Encrypted DNS (DoH/DoT) closes that specific gap; a VPN moves the trust to the VPN provider. Know what each does and does not hide.
- **"The padlock means the site is safe/legit."** The padlock means the connection is *encrypted to whoever holds that certificate* — which can be a phishing site that got a free certificate. TLS proves *encryption*, not *honesty*. Check the domain name, not just the padlock.
- **"It's one connection to the server."** It is many hops and several separate protocols (DNS, TCP, TLS, HTTP), each with its own weakness. Naming the protocol/layer is half the battle in diagnosing an attack.
- **`*  *  *` in traceroute means it's broken.** No — many routers simply don't reply to traceroute. Absence of a reply is not evidence of a problem.

### ❓ Quiz
1. What is the difference between an IP address and a domain name, and which system maps one to the other?
2. Why does TLS matter? Name two concrete things an attacker can do on plaintext HTTP that TLS prevents.
3. Your friend says "I don't need HTTPS on my blog, there's no login." Give the strongest argument for HTTPS anyway.
4. You have perfect HTTPS but use default plaintext DNS on public Wi-Fi. What can a snooping observer still learn?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Answers</strong></summary>

1. An **IP address** is the numeric location of a machine; a **domain name** is a human-friendly label that *maps to* an IP. **DNS** performs the mapping.
2. TLS encrypts the connection so intermediaries cannot **read** content (passwords, cookies, page data) or **modify** it in flight (inject malicious scripts, swap downloads). On plaintext HTTP both are trivial for anyone on the path.
3. Even with no login: (a) without HTTPS, an attacker can *inject* content into your page (malware, fake forms, crypto-miners) for every visitor; (b) ISPs and networks can inject ads or track visitors; (c) browsers increasingly flag/penalize HTTP sites; (d) integrity of what readers see is itself worth protecting (Module 1's integrity leg). HTTPS protects *integrity and authenticity*, not just confidentiality.
4. The observer can still see the **domain names you look up** (via plaintext DNS) and the **destination IPs** of your connections — i.e., *which* sites you visit, even though the *contents* are encrypted.

</details>

### ✅ Progress Tracker
- [ ] I ran traceroute on 3 different sites and counted hops
- [ ] I explained the DNS → TCP → TLS → HTTP sequence in my own words
- [ ] I inspected a real TLS certificate's issuer and dates
- [ ] I read a plaintext HTTP response and understood exactly what an attacker would capture
- [ ] I can explain why the padlock proves encryption, not honesty

### ➡️ Next Step
Module 3 — **Digital Identity & Password Security**. You now know how data travels and how credentials cross the network. Next: how the *credentials themselves* are stored, why they leak, and the cryptography that is supposed to protect them (and how it fails).

## Module 3 — Digital Identity & Password Security

### 🎯 Mission Briefing
Your identity — username + password + second factors — is the front door to your entire digital life, and most breaches start here. This module goes past "pick a good password" (boring, and partly wrong). You will understand the cryptography that protects passwords, *crack real hashes you generate yourself* to feel why some are weak, and learn the defense that actually wins: stop relying on a single secret.

### 📘 Theory

#### Define the words first

- **Authentication** — proving *who you are*. (Different from **authorization** = what you are *allowed* to do; Module 14.)
- **Credential** — the thing you present to authenticate (a password, a code, a key).
- **Hash function** — a one-way function that turns any input into a fixed-length output (a "digest"/"fingerprint"). One-way means: easy to compute forward, computationally infeasible to reverse.
- **Encryption** — a *two-way*, reversible transformation with a key. *This is the most common beginner confusion:* hashing is one-way (no "decrypt"); encryption is two-way.
- **Salt** — a unique random value added to a password *before* hashing, stored alongside the hash.
- **Pepper** — a secret value (not stored with the hash, often in code/HSM) mixed in for extra defense.
- **Rainbow table** — a precomputed lookup from hash → input, used to reverse *unsalted* fast hashes instantly.
- **MFA / 2FA** — multi-/two-factor authentication: requiring more than one *category* of evidence.

#### How passwords are *supposed* to be stored

A well-designed site **never stores your password**. At signup it computes `hash(salt + password)` and stores the *salt and the hash*. At login it recomputes the hash from what you typed and compares. So even if the database leaks, the attacker gets fingerprints, not passwords — *if* the hashing was done right.

#### The naive solutions and exactly why each fails

> **Naive #1: "Store the password directly."**
> Fails because any database leak, backup theft, rogue admin, or SQL injection (Module 14) instantly exposes every user's actual password — which they have reused on their email and bank.

> **Naive #2: "OK, store a hash with a fast algorithm like MD5 or SHA-256."**
> Fails because fast hashes are the *point* — a modern GPU computes **billions of SHA-256 hashes per second**. An attacker with the leaked hashes runs a dictionary/brute-force offline and recovers most passwords in hours. Speed is a feature for file integrity but a catastrophe for passwords.

> **Naive #3: "Add a fast hash but no salt."**
> Fails because identical passwords produce identical hashes, so an attacker uses a **rainbow table** (precomputed once, reused forever) to reverse millions of hashes instantly, and can spot every user who chose the same password.

> **The correct solution:** a **slow, salted, memory-hard** password hash — **bcrypt**, **scrypt**, or **Argon2id**. "Slow" (a tunable work factor) means each guess costs the attacker real time/memory; "salted" defeats rainbow tables and reveals nothing about reuse; "memory-hard" (scrypt/Argon2) blunts the GPU/ASIC advantage. This does not make weak passwords safe — it makes *cracking expensive enough* that strong passwords stay uncracked and weak ones cost the attacker dearly.

#### Why length beats complexity

Cracking cost grows with the **search space** = (alphabet size) ^ (length). Adding one character *multiplies* the space; adding a symbol to a short password only widens the alphabet a little. A 4-word passphrase like `correct-horse-battery-staple` (~44 bits+ of entropy, easy to remember) beats `P@ss1!` (short, predictable substitutions attackers' rules already try). The professional standard: a password manager generating **20+ random characters per site**, so you never reuse and never remember them.

#### Multi-factor authentication — the real win

Three *categories* of evidence:

- Something you **know** (password)
- Something you **have** (phone with an authenticator app, a hardware key)
- Something you **are** (fingerprint, Face ID)

MFA matters because it breaks the **offline-crack-then-reuse** chain: even with your password, the attacker lacks the second factor. Microsoft's telemetry: MFA blocks **over 99%** of automated account-takeover attempts. But not all MFA is equal — **SMS codes can be SIM-swapped** (attacker convinces the carrier to move your number) and phished; an **authenticator app (TOTP)** is better; a **phishing-resistant hardware key / passkey (FIDO2/WebAuthn)** is best because the browser cryptographically checks the site's domain before responding.

### 🛠️ Lab: Hash It, Then Crack It (M2 / Apple Silicon)

> ⚖️ **Ethics box.** You will crack **only hashes you generate yourself** in this lab, plus check **your own** email against Have I Been Pwned. Cracking hashes you do not own — from a breach dump, a friend's account, anything — is illegal and against the Cyber Guardian's Code. The cracking tools below are legal to install and use *on your own data*.

**Part A — feel the avalanche effect and the speed problem (built-in tools):**

```bash
mkdir -p ~/cyber-journal/m3-pw && cd ~/cyber-journal/m3-pw

# SHA-256 of "hello", then change ONE character
echo -n "hello"  | shasum -a 256
echo -n "hello!" | shasum -a 256
# ~50% of output bits flip from a 1-char change = the avalanche effect

# Same password, NO salt -> identical hash every time (the rainbow-table problem)
echo -n "password" | shasum -a 256
echo -n "password" | shasum -a 256   # same output -> attacker reverses it once, reuses forever
```

**Part B — crack your OWN fast-hash, to feel why MD5/SHA are wrong for passwords.** Install the ARM64-native cracker `john` (John the Ripper) via Homebrew:

```bash
brew install john   # native arm64 build

# Create a tiny "leaked" file of MD5 hashes for passwords WE choose (owned data):
printf '%s\n' \
  "$(echo -n 'password' | md5)" \
  "$(echo -n 'iloveyou' | md5)" \
  "$(echo -n 'Tr0ub4dor&3' | md5)" > myhashes.txt
cat myhashes.txt

# Crack with a wordlist (John ships rockyou-style logic; use a small wordlist you own)
printf '%s\n' password iloveyou letmein dragon monkey > words.txt
john --format=raw-md5 --wordlist=words.txt myhashes.txt
john --format=raw-md5 --show myhashes.txt   # shows which cracked
```

*What you observe:* the common-word passwords fall instantly; `Tr0ub4dor&3` survives the tiny wordlist (it would still fall to a large list + rules — which is the point about length/entropy). On MD5, the *hashing* gives the attacker zero slowdown.

**Part C — prove slow hashing changes the economics.** Generate a bcrypt hash and time it:

```bash
brew install httpd 2>/dev/null  # provides htpasswd; or use python bcrypt
python3 -m pip install --user bcrypt
python3 - <<'PY'
import bcrypt, time
pw = b"password"
t=time.time(); h=bcrypt.hashpw(pw, bcrypt.gensalt(rounds=12)); print("hash:", h.decode())
print("one bcrypt hash took %.3f s (work factor 12)" % (time.time()-t))
PY
# Compare: SHA-256 = ~nanoseconds/guess; bcrypt = ~hundreds of ms/guess.
# At ~0.25s/guess, a billion guesses take ~8 years on one core. THAT is the defense.
```

**Part D — operational hygiene (do these for real):**
1. Check your email at `https://haveibeenpwned.com` (Troy Hunt's reputable service). Note every breach you appear in.
2. In Bitwarden: add 5 accounts, generate one 20-char password, run the **Vault health report**.
3. Enable MFA on email, bank, and school — prefer an **authenticator app** (2FAS/Aegis/Raivo) or a passkey over SMS.

**Windows (PowerShell) hashing:**
```powershell
$s=[IO.MemoryStream]::new([byte[]][char[]]"hello"); Get-FileHash -InputStream $s -Algorithm SHA256
# For cracking practice on Windows, install John the Ripper or hashcat (own hashes only).
```

### 💻 Vulnerable vs. Secure Code (password storage)

```javascript
// ❌ VULNERABLE — Node/Express signup
const crypto = require("crypto");
function storePassword(user, password) {
  // WHY WRONG: SHA-256 is FAST (billions/sec on GPU) and UNSALTED here.
  // A DB leak -> rainbow tables + offline brute force crack most users in hours.
  const hash = crypto.createHash("sha256").update(password).digest("hex");
  db.save({ user, hash });
}
```

```javascript
// ✅ SECURE — slow, salted, memory-hard hash with a tuned work factor
const argon2 = require("argon2");            // npm i argon2  (Argon2id default)
async function storePassword(user, password) {
  // WHY IT WORKS: Argon2id is deliberately SLOW and MEMORY-HARD, defeating
  // GPU/ASIC speedups. A per-user salt is generated and embedded automatically,
  // so identical passwords get different hashes (no rainbow tables, no reuse leak).
  const hash = await argon2.hash(password, { type: argon2.argon2id, memoryCost: 19456, timeCost: 2, parallelism: 1 });
  db.save({ user, hash });                    // store the single self-describing hash string
}
async function verify(user, password) {
  const row = db.find(user);
  return row && await argon2.verify(row.hash, password);  // constant-time compare inside
}
```

### 🌍 Case Study — The 2012 LinkedIn Breach
~6.5 million password hashes leaked publicly; LinkedIn used **unsalted SHA-1** (a fast, salt-free hash). Within days, ~90% were cracked — `123456`, `linkedin`, `password` came tumbling out. A 2016 follow-up revealed the real scope was **117 million** accounts. Two lessons map directly to this module: (1) *unsalted fast hashes are barely better than plaintext under a leak* — use slow, salted, memory-hard hashing; (2) *reuse multiplies the blast radius* — those cracked passwords were immediately tried (credential stuffing) against victims' other accounts, which is exactly why a password manager with unique passwords per site is non-negotiable.

### 🔑 Beyond Passwords: Passkeys & WebAuthn

The step beyond "manager + MFA" is **no password at all**. **Passkeys** (the consumer name for WebAuthn/FIDO2 credentials) replace the shared secret with a **key pair** on your device. Signing in:

1. The website sends a random **challenge**.
2. Your device prompts Touch ID / Face ID.
3. Your device signs the challenge with a **private key that never leaves the device**.
4. The website verifies the signature against the **public key** you registered.

Why it is a leap forward: there is **no secret in the database to steal**; it is **un-phishable** because the browser checks the real domain before signing (a fake `app1e.com` simply won't match); and it is one tap instead of password + code. Try it now:

- **GitHub:** Settings → Password and authentication → Passkeys → Add a passkey (use Touch ID).
- **Google:** `g.co/passkeys`.
- **Apple ID:** supported on macOS Ventura+/iOS 16+.

Register, then **sign out and back in with the passkey** — no password typed. We cover the **developer side of WebAuthn** in Module 14 and Level 3.

> Passkeys are not yet a 100% replacement; many sites still require passwords. Use a manager for the rest and migrate site-by-site.

### 🧩 Try It Yourself

**Challenge 1:** Hash `Password1` and `Password2` with SHA-256. Are the outputs similar or completely different? Why does that matter?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

Completely different — the **avalanche effect**: a one-bit input change flips ~50% of output bits. That is exactly what makes a hash usable as a fingerprint (you can't infer the input from a near-match), and it is why integrity checks (Module 1) work. It does *not*, by itself, make the hash safe for passwords — speed and salting still matter.

</details>

**Challenge 2:** A website emails you your *current* password after you click "Forgot Password." What does that prove, and what should you do?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

It proves they store your password **reversibly** — plaintext or two-way encryption — because a site that hashes correctly *cannot* recover your password to send it. That is a serious red flag. Change that password everywhere you reused it, and consider closing the account. A secure site can only send a time-limited **reset link**, never your existing password.

</details>

### 🛡️ Pitfalls & False-Confidence Traps

- **"We hash passwords, so we're safe."** *Which* hash? SHA-256/MD5 without a slow KDF is near-plaintext under a leak. Only slow, salted, memory-hard hashing (bcrypt/scrypt/Argon2id) counts.
- **Complexity theater.** Forcing `P@ssw0rd!` patterns pushes users to predictable substitutions attackers' rule-sets already include. Favor length/passphrases and screen against known-breached passwords.
- **"We have MFA" but it's SMS-only.** SIM-swap and SMS phishing defeat it. Treat SMS as the weakest MFA, fine as a fallback, not the primary factor for high-value accounts.
- **The reused master mistake.** A password manager is great until its master password is weak or reused. The master must be a long, unique passphrase plus MFA on the vault.
- **Comparing hashes with `==`.** Naive string comparison can leak timing. Use the library's constant-time `verify` (Argon2/bcrypt do this for you).

### ❓ Quiz
1. What is the precise difference between encryption and hashing?
2. Why is plain SHA-256 a poor choice for password storage, and what should you use instead?
3. Why is SMS-based MFA weaker than an authenticator app or a passkey?
4. Your 14-character password looks strong, but an attacker can try 10 billion guesses/second *if the site used a fast hash*. Reason about orders of magnitude: roughly how does a slow hash (≈4 guesses/second/core) change that?
5. Why can a correctly built site never email you your existing password?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Answers</strong></summary>

1. **Encryption** is reversible with a key (you can decrypt). **Hashing** is one-way — there is no key and no "un-hash"; you can only recompute and compare.
2. Plain SHA-256 is **fast and (as typically used) unsalted**, so leaked hashes fall to GPU brute force and rainbow tables. Use a **slow, salted, memory-hard** password hash: Argon2id (preferred), scrypt, or bcrypt.
3. SMS can be **SIM-swapped** (carrier social-engineered) and **phished/intercepted**, and SS7 weaknesses can divert it. Authenticator apps keep the secret on-device; passkeys add domain-checking that resists phishing entirely.
4. With a fast hash, 10¹⁰/s makes large search spaces fall quickly. A slow hash at ~4/s is ~2.5 *billion* times slower per guess — the same brute force that took hours now takes geologic time, which is the entire defensive point. (Memory-hardness blunts the attacker's hardware advantage on top of that.)
5. Because it does not *have* your password — only a one-way hash. It can only send a time-limited reset link. Emailing your password proves reversible storage.

</details>

### ✅ Progress Tracker
- [ ] I can explain hashing vs. encryption without hesitating
- [ ] I cracked my own MD5 hashes with John and watched weak ones fall instantly
- [ ] I timed a bcrypt hash and understand why "slow" is the defense
- [ ] MFA enabled on email, primary social, and any financial account (app or passkey, not SMS)
- [ ] 20+ unique passwords stored in a password manager; ran a Vault health report
- [ ] At least one account migrated from password to passkey (GitHub or Google)

Module 4 — **Malware, Phishing & Social Engineering**. You just hardened the *technical* front door. But the most common breach skips the math entirely and attacks the *human*: it convinces you to hand over the credential. Next we study how that works and how to resist it.
