## Module 4 — Malware, Phishing & Social Engineering

### 🎯 Mission Briefing
The single most common cyberattack is not a Hollywood-style technical hack — it is psychological. In Module 3 you hardened the *technical* front door (slow hashes, MFA, passkeys). This module covers the attack that skips the math entirely and convinces *you* to open the door. You will learn the taxonomy of malware, the human-exploitation playbook attackers use, and — critically for a developer — you will safely build and analyze a phishing-email header on your own machine, then write the server-side validation that an application uses to refuse spoofed mail.

### 📘 Theory

#### Define the words first

- **Malware** — *mal*icious soft*ware*: any program written to act against the interest of the device's owner. The categories below are not exclusive; real samples combine them.
- **Payload** — the part of malware that does the harmful work (encrypt files, steal data), as opposed to the part that spreads or hides it.
- **Social engineering** — manipulating a *person* into performing an action or revealing information, instead of attacking a machine. The "vulnerability" is human psychology.
- **Phishing** — social engineering delivered by message (email/SMS/voice), tricking the target into clicking, paying, or revealing a credential.
- **Spoofing** — forging the *source* of a message so it appears to come from someone trusted. Email's original design trusts the sender's claimed address with no proof — which is the root cause we defend against below.
- **SPF / DKIM / DMARC** — three layered email-authentication standards (defined in detail in the Defense section) that let a *receiving* server detect spoofing.

#### The malware family tree

| Type | What it does | Famous example |
|---|---|---|
| Virus | Attaches to a file; spreads when the file is executed | ILOVEYOU (2000) |
| Worm | Self-propagates over networks with **no user action** | WannaCry (2017) |
| Trojan | Looks legitimate, carries a hidden payload | Zeus banking trojan |
| Ransomware | Encrypts files, demands payment for the key | WannaCry, LockBit |
| Spyware | Silently monitors the user | Pegasus |
| Rootkit | Hides deep in the OS to maintain persistent access | Stuxnet |
| Adware | Unwanted ads and tracking | Superfish (Lenovo, 2015) |
| Botnet node | Your device enslaved into an attacker's remote-controlled army | Mirai |
| Fileless | Lives only in memory; leaves no file on disk to scan | Poweliks |

The single most important distinction: a **worm needs no human** (it exploits a network vulnerability and spreads itself — which is why WannaCry crossed the planet in hours), whereas a **virus needs a human** to run the infected file. This is why patching (Module 6) stops worms and *user training* stops viruses and phishing.

#### Why the "naive" defenses fail

> **Naive #1: "Antivirus will catch it."**
> Fails because signature-based antivirus recognizes *known* malware by its fingerprint. Fileless malware leaves no file, polymorphic malware rewrites itself each run, and a brand-new ("zero-day") sample has no signature yet. AV is one layer, not a guarantee.

> **Naive #2: "I can tell a fake email by looking at the sender name."**
> Fails because the display name and even the visible `From:` address are *attacker-controlled text*. SMTP, the 1982 mail protocol, never required proof of identity. Anyone can claim to be `security@apple.com`. Believing the `From:` line is the root cause of virtually all phishing success.

> **Naive #3: "I'm too smart / too small a target to be phished."**
> Fails because modern phishing is personalized (spear phishing), well-written (now LLM-generated, no grammar tells), and exploits *universal* psychology — urgency and authority work on experts under time pressure. Twitter's own employees fell for it (case study below).

> **The correct posture:** *defense in depth on both the human and the machine.* For humans: verify through an independent channel, never act on inbound urgency. For machines: cryptographically authenticate mail (SPF/DKIM/DMARC), patch relentlessly to deny worms, and treat all inbound data as hostile (the developer's version of "don't trust the From line").

#### Social engineering — the six levers (Cialdini's principles, weaponized)

1. **Authority** — "This is IT, we need your password to fix a problem."
2. **Urgency** — "Your account will be closed in 1 hour." (Time pressure suppresses careful thinking.)
3. **Scarcity** — "Only 3 seats left." (Fear of missing out.)
4. **Reciprocity** — "I sent you a gift card, can you just verify your info?"
5. **Social proof** — "Your colleague Sarah already signed."
6. **Liking / familiarity** — cloned sender names, hijacked real email threads.

#### Phishing variants

- **Phishing** — mass email; **Spear phishing** — targeted with personal detail; **Whaling** — aimed at executives; **Smishing** — SMS; **Vishing** — voice calls (now AI-voice-cloned); **Quishing** — malicious QR codes that move the lure off-screen where URL inspection is hard.

### 🛠️ Lab: Dissect a Phishing Email & Verify Mail Auth (M2 / Apple Silicon)

> ⚖️ **Ethics box.** You will analyze **only email already sitting in your own inbox/spam**, and you will generate a **fake header file yourself** to practice parsing. Do **not** click links, open attachments, or visit attacker URLs in your real browser — use the sandboxed analyzers. Sending spoofed mail to others, even as a "test," can be a crime. Everything here is read-only analysis of data you already own.

**Part A — read a real header and find the spoof signals (built-in tools).** Save a suspicious email's raw source to a file, then parse it. In Gmail: three-dot menu → **Show original** → Download. In Apple Mail: View → Message → **Raw Source**. Save as `~/cyber-journal/m4/sample.eml`.

```bash
mkdir -p ~/cyber-journal/m4 && cd ~/cyber-journal/m4
# Pull the authentication verdicts the RECEIVING server already computed for you:
grep -iE "Authentication-Results|Received-SPF|dkim=|dmarc=|spf=" sample.eml
# The lines that matter: spf=fail / dkim=fail / dmarc=fail  => the message was spoofed.
# Compare the visible sender against the envelope sender:
grep -iE "^From:|Return-Path:|^Reply-To:" sample.eml
# A From: you trust but a Return-Path: at a random domain is a classic spoof tell.
```

**Part B — generate your OWN sample header to practice parsing (owned data).**

```bash
cat > evil.eml <<'EOF'
From: Apple Support <security@app1e-support.co>
Return-Path: <bounce@xq7-mailer.ru>
Reply-To: <collect@apple-verification.xyz>
Authentication-Results: mx.example.com; spf=fail; dkim=fail; dmarc=fail
Subject: URGENT: Your Apple ID has been suspended
EOF
# Now re-run the Part A greps against evil.eml and confirm you can read the spoof.
grep -iE "From:|Return-Path:|Reply-To:|spf=|dkim=|dmarc=" evil.eml
```

**Part C — check a real domain's published SPF/DMARC policy (ARM64-native `dig`, ships with macOS).**

```bash
dig +short TXT apple.com   | grep -i spf     # the v=spf1 record that lists who may send for apple.com
dig +short TXT _dmarc.apple.com              # the DMARC policy: p=reject means "drop spoofed mail"
# A domain with p=reject is hard to spoof to a modern inbox. p=none = monitoring only (weak).
```

**Part D — safe URL analysis (never open the link directly):** paste a suspicious URL into `https://urlscan.io` (sandboxed render) or `https://www.virustotal.com`. Do **not** submit URLs that contain your private tokens.

**Windows (PowerShell) header view:** Outlook → File → Properties → Internet Headers, or Outlook Web → three-dot → View → View message source. Same red flags apply.

### 💻 Vulnerable vs. Secure Code — refusing spoofed inbound mail

The developer's version of "don't trust the From line": an app that processes inbound email (support tickets, "reply to approve" flows) must verify authentication results, not the visible sender.

```javascript
// ❌ VULNERABLE — Node.js: trusting the From header
function handleInboundEmail(msg) {
  // WHY WRONG: msg.from is attacker-controlled text. SMTP never proved it.
  // Anyone can send mail claiming from = "ceo@yourcompany.com".
  if (msg.from === "ceo@yourcompany.com") {
    approvePayment(msg.body);   // a spoofed email just authorized a wire transfer
  }
}
```

```javascript
// ✅ SECURE — Node.js: verify SPF/DKIM/DMARC before trusting sender
const { authenticate } = require("mailauth");   // npm i mailauth
async function handleInboundEmail(rawMime, clientIp) {
  const res = await authenticate(rawMime, { ip: clientIp });
  // WHY IT WORKS: DKIM proves the body was signed by the domain's private key;
  // SPF proves the sending IP is authorized; DMARC ties them to the visible From.
  const ok = res.dmarc?.status?.result === "pass" && res.dkim?.results?.[0]?.status?.result === "pass";
  if (!ok) return reject("unauthenticated sender");
  // Even then: high-value actions need an independent confirmation channel, never email alone.
}
```

```python
# ❌ VULNERABLE — Python: same mistake
def handle_inbound_email(msg):
    # WHY WRONG: msg["From"] is forgeable header text, not proof of identity.
    if msg["From"] == "ceo@yourcompany.com":
        approve_payment(msg.get_payload())

# ✅ SECURE — Python: check the receiver's authentication verdict + independent confirmation
import email
def handle_inbound_email(raw_bytes):
    msg = email.message_from_bytes(raw_bytes)
    auth = (msg["Authentication-Results"] or "").lower()
    # WHY IT WORKS: we rely on the cryptographic verdicts the MX already computed,
    # not the spoofable From line. All three must pass.
    if not ("spf=pass" in auth and "dkim=pass" in auth and "dmarc=pass" in auth):
        raise ValueError("unauthenticated sender — refusing")
    # And never let an email alone authorize money/credentials — confirm out-of-band.
```

### 🛡️ Defense — root cause and layers

- **Root cause of phishing success:** email trusts an unauthenticated `From:`. **Fix it at the protocol layer** with all three of SPF (which IPs may send for your domain), DKIM (a cryptographic signature over the message), and DMARC (`p=reject` to actually drop failures and tie SPF/DKIM to the visible From).
- **Deny worms by patching** (Module 6): WannaCry only spread because unpatched machines exposed a known SMB hole. Auto-update is a security control.
- **Human layer:** independent-channel verification ("I'll call the number on my card, not the one in the email"), no action on inbound urgency, and reporting — not engaging.
- **Developer layer:** treat *all* inbound data as hostile; never let an unverified message trigger a privileged action; require out-of-band confirmation for money or credentials.

### 🌍 Case Study — The 2020 Twitter Bitcoin Hack
Teenagers used *phone-based vishing* (voice phishing) to trick Twitter employees into surrendering internal-tool access. They hijacked the accounts of Obama, Biden, Musk, Apple, and Uber, tweeted a cryptocurrency scam, and stole ~$118,000 in minutes. **Zero malware. 100% social engineering.** The 17-year-old mastermind was arrested within weeks and received real prison time. Two lessons: (1) the strongest technical controls fail when a human with access is manipulated — which is why least privilege and out-of-band confirmation (Module 14, Module 25) matter; (2) social engineering is a *crime*, not a prank — remember Rule #1 of the Cyber Guardian's Code.

### 🧩 Try It Yourself

**Challenge 1:** Here's a fake email excerpt. List every red flag you can spot.

> From: Apple Support <security@app1e-support.co>
> Subject: URGENT: Your Apple ID has been suspended — Action required within 24 hours
>
> Dear Customer,
>
> We detected unusual activity on your accoun. Please kindly verify your identity by click the link below immediately or your account will be closed permanently.
>
> [Verify My Account Now](http://apple-verification.xyz/login)

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

1. Sender domain is `app1e-support.co` — a "1" replaces the "l", and the TLD is wrong (not `apple.com`).
2. "Dear Customer" — Apple knows your name (generic greeting).
3. "URGENT" + "24 hours" — the **urgency** lever.
4. Typo: "accoun" (though modern LLM-written phishing won't have these — don't rely on typos).
5. Awkward grammar: "kindly verify your identity by click".
6. Link text says Apple but the URL is `apple-verification.xyz` — a different domain entirely (hover, don't click).
7. Threat of permanent account closure — fear/loss pressure (the **authority** + **urgency** combo).
8. The real tell a defender checks: the header's `dmarc=fail` — the visible `apple.com`-style identity cannot be cryptographically proven.

</details>

**Challenge 2:** Why is "the grammar was perfect, so it must be real" now a dangerous heuristic?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

Because LLMs generate fluent, error-free, well-targeted text instantly. The classic "spot the typo" advice is obsolete. Shift your trust to *structural* signals you can verify: the sending domain, the link's real destination, the mail-authentication verdict, and above all **independent-channel confirmation** for anything consequential. Never let the *quality* of a message substitute for *verification* of it.

</details>

### ❓ Quiz
1. What is the key *structural* difference between a worm and a virus, and why does it change which defense stops each?
2. Why does urgency work so effectively on humans, even on smart ones under no normal cognitive deficit?
3. What is the single safest way to verify whether an email from "your bank" is real?
4. The visible `From:` says `security@apple.com`. What three mechanisms let a receiving server tell whether that claim is genuine, and which DMARC policy actually *drops* spoofed mail?
5. As a developer, why must an "approve by replying to this email" workflow never authorize a payment on its own?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Answers</strong></summary>

1. A **worm self-propagates over the network with no user action** (it exploits a vulnerability), so **patching** stops it; a **virus needs a human to run an infected file**, so **user training/awareness** and execution controls stop it.
2. Urgency triggers fast, emotional ("System 1") thinking and suppresses slow, analytical scrutiny. Under time pressure even experts skip the verification step — which is exactly the attacker's goal.
3. Contact the institution through an **independent, known-good channel** — the phone number printed on your physical card or typed from the official site — never a number, link, or button supplied by the suspicious message.
4. **SPF** (authorized sending IPs), **DKIM** (cryptographic signature over the message), and **DMARC** (ties SPF/DKIM to the visible From and sets policy). **`p=reject`** is the DMARC policy that instructs receivers to drop messages that fail.
5. Because the `From:` line is spoofable and email is asynchronous and unauthenticated by default; a privileged action must require out-of-band confirmation (a signed request, an authenticated dashboard action, a callback), or a single spoofed message authorizes the transfer.

</details>

### 🛡️ Pitfalls & False-Confidence Traps
- **"We have antivirus, so we're protected."** AV misses fileless, polymorphic, and zero-day malware. It is one layer atop patching, least privilege, and training.
- **Trusting the display name or `From:`.** Both are attacker-controlled text. Only SPF/DKIM/DMARC verdicts are evidence.
- **`p=none` DMARC theater.** Publishing DMARC in monitor-only mode (`p=none`) feels like protection but drops nothing. You must reach `p=reject` to actually block spoofing.
- **Typo-spotting as a defense.** LLM-written phishing is grammatically perfect. Shift to verifying domains, links, and authentication.
- **Engaging the scammer "to waste their time."** It can expose you, confirm your address is live, and escalate. Report to your provider and to anti-phishing services (APWG) instead.

### ✅ Progress Tracker
- [ ] I saved a real email's raw source and read its Authentication-Results line
- [ ] I generated my own sample header and parsed the spoof signals with `grep`
- [ ] I checked a real domain's SPF and DMARC policy with `dig`
- [ ] I can name all six Cialdini levers
- [ ] I can explain SPF vs. DKIM vs. DMARC and why `p=reject` matters
- [ ] I taught one family member to verify through an independent channel

Module 5 — **Safe Browsing, Privacy & Digital Hygiene**. You can now spot the lure in your inbox. Next we move to where most lures *land* — the browser — and learn how tracking, malicious sites, and weak privacy hygiene expand your attack surface, plus the defenses that shrink it.

## Module 5 — Safe Browsing, Privacy & Digital Hygiene

### 🎯 Mission Briefing
You leak data every second you're online — which Wi-Fi networks you've joined, what you click, which apps tracked you across sites. This module is precise, not paranoid. You'll learn exactly what your browser reveals, why the obvious privacy tools (incognito, VPN) protect *less* than people assume, how to harden your own browser, and — as a developer — how the cookie flags and consent logic *you* write determine whether your users can be tracked.

### 📘 Theory

#### Define the words first

- **Browser fingerprint** — the combination of observable browser/device traits (User-Agent, installed fonts, screen resolution, time zone, WebGL/canvas rendering quirks) that together identify you *without* a cookie.
- **Cookie** — a small key/value the server asks the browser to store and send back on later requests. **First-party** = set by the site you're visiting (e.g., your login session). **Third-party** = set by a different domain embedded in the page (e.g., an ad network) so it can recognize you across many sites.
- **Tracking** — linking your activity over time/sites into a profile. Done via third-party cookies (declining) and increasingly via fingerprinting (rising, because it survives cookie deletion).
- **VPN (Virtual Private Network)** — an encrypted tunnel from your device to a provider's server, which then talks to the internet on your behalf. It moves trust *from your local network to the VPN provider*.
- **Tor** — onion routing: your traffic is relayed through three volunteer nodes so no single node sees both who you are and what you're visiting.
- **Cookie flags** — server-set attributes (`Secure`, `HttpOnly`, `SameSite`) that constrain how a cookie may be used; the developer's primary privacy/security lever.

#### What your browser reveals without asking

User-Agent, IP address, installed fonts, screen resolution, time zone, language, and a **canvas/WebGL fingerprint** (how your exact GPU+drivers render a hidden image). Combined, these are often unique enough to single you out across sites *even with cookies cleared and incognito on*. This is why deleting cookies is not the whole answer.

#### Why the naive privacy tools under-deliver

> **Naive #1: "Incognito/Private mode makes me invisible."**
> Fails because incognito only stops your *local* browser from saving history/cookies *after the window closes*. The websites, your ISP, your employer, and your school still see every request in real time. It hides your activity from *the next person on your laptop*, nothing more.

> **Naive #2: "A VPN makes me anonymous."**
> Fails because a VPN only moves the trust: it hides your IP from the website and your traffic from the local network/ISP, but the **VPN provider now sees everything**, and if you're logged into Google/Facebook the site identifies you by your *account*, not your IP. A VPN is a confidentiality tool for untrusted networks, not an anonymity cloak.

> **Naive #3: "I block third-party cookies, so I can't be tracked."**
> Fails because **fingerprinting needs no cookie**. As browsers kill third-party cookies, trackers pivot to canvas/WebGL/font fingerprints, which survive cookie clearing and private mode. Defense requires *fingerprint resistance* (uniform browser profiles, anti-fingerprinting extensions), not just cookie hygiene.

> **The correct posture:** match the tool to the threat. Use HTTPS-everywhere + a VPN on untrusted Wi-Fi (confidentiality), tracker/fingerprint blocking for advertising surveillance, and Tor + compartmentalization for true anonymity needs. There is no single switch.

#### VPN — what it actually does

| ✅ Does | ❌ Does not |
|---|---|
| Hides your IP from websites | Make you anonymous (the provider sees all) |
| Hides activity from ISP / school / coffee-shop Wi-Fi | Protect against malware or phishing |
| Lets you appear in another region | Matter much if you're logged into Google anyway |

#### Tor — what it actually does

Bounces your traffic through three random relays. The **entry** node knows you but not the destination; the **exit** node knows the destination but not you; no single node sees both. Used by journalists, dissidents, researchers — and criminals. Legal in most countries, restricted in a few. Slower by design (three hops).

#### The developer's role in privacy

When *you* build a site, your cookie flags decide whether a user's session can be stolen or tracked: `Secure` (only sent over HTTPS, so it can't leak on plain HTTP), `HttpOnly` (JavaScript can't read it, blunting XSS theft — Module 14), and `SameSite` (controls whether the cookie is sent on cross-site requests, the front-line CSRF and cross-site-tracking control). We harden these properly in Module 14; here we establish the principle: **privacy and security are set by the defaults you ship.**

### 🛠️ Lab: Harden Your Browser & Inspect Real Tracking (M2 / Apple Silicon)

> ⚖️ **Ethics box.** Everything here inspects **your own browser and your own machine's settings**. The fingerprint test (EFF Cover Your Tracks) tests *your* browser only. Do not run fingerprinting or tracking against other people.

**Part A — harden Firefox:**
1. Settings → Privacy & Security → **Strict** Enhanced Tracking Protection.
2. Enable **HTTPS-Only Mode**.
3. Disable telemetry (Settings → search "telemetry").
4. Install **uBlock Origin** (ad/tracker blocker), **Privacy Badger** (learning tracker blocker), **Bitwarden** (manager).

**Part B — measure your own fingerprint:** visit `https://coveryourtracks.eff.org`. Run it once with uBlock disabled, once enabled. Note how "unique" your fingerprint is (often 1-in-hundreds-of-thousands) — proof that cookies aren't the whole story.

**Part C — see cookie flags on the wire (built-in `curl`, ARM64-native):**
```bash
mkdir -p ~/cyber-journal/m5 && cd ~/cyber-journal/m5
# Inspect the Set-Cookie flags a real site sends YOU (your own request, read-only):
curl -sI https://github.com | grep -i "set-cookie"
# Look for: Secure; HttpOnly; SameSite=Lax/Strict — the three flags you'll set as a dev.
```

**Part D — macOS privacy sweep:** walk System Settings → Privacy & Security → each category (Location, Camera, Microphone, Screen Recording, Full Disk Access) and revoke apps that don't need it.

```bash
# List which apps requested location access (read-only check of your own machine):
defaults read /var/db/locationd/clients.plist 2>/dev/null | grep -i Bundle || \
  echo "Check: System Settings → Privacy & Security → Location Services"
```

**Windows:** Settings → Privacy & Security → walk every section. **Linux:** `gnome-control-center privacy` on GNOME.

### 💻 Vulnerable vs. Secure Code — cookie flags that protect users

```javascript
// ❌ VULNERABLE — Node/Express: a session cookie with no protective flags
app.use(session({
  secret: "s3cr3t",
  cookie: {}   // WHY WRONG: no Secure -> leaks over plain HTTP; no HttpOnly ->
               // JavaScript (and any XSS) can read it; no SameSite -> sent on
               // cross-site requests, enabling CSRF and cross-site tracking.
}));
```

```javascript
// ✅ SECURE — Node/Express: lock the cookie down
app.use(session({
  secret: process.env.SESSION_SECRET,           // not hard-coded
  cookie: {
    secure: true,        // WHY: only sent over HTTPS, can't leak on plain HTTP
    httpOnly: true,      // WHY: JS can't read it, so XSS can't steal the session
    sameSite: "lax"      // WHY: not sent on cross-site POSTs -> blunts CSRF/tracking
  }
}));
```

```python
# ❌ VULNERABLE — Python/Flask: cookie with no flags
resp.set_cookie("session", token)   # WHY WRONG: defaults to no Secure/HttpOnly/SameSite

# ✅ SECURE — Python/Flask: explicit protective flags
resp.set_cookie(
    "session", token,
    secure=True,        # only over HTTPS
    httponly=True,      # not readable by JavaScript
    samesite="Lax",     # not sent on cross-site requests
)
# WHY IT WORKS: the same three flags as Node — they are an HTTP standard, not a framework feature.
```

### 🌍 Case Study — Cambridge Analytica (2016–2018)
A personality-quiz app on Facebook collected data on **87 million** users — not just the ~270,000 who took the quiz, but all of *their friends* too, via an over-permissive platform API. The data fed political micro-targeting. Nobody was "hacked" in a technical sense; the data was handed out *by design*. The lesson maps to this module: privacy isn't only about attackers breaking in — it's about the **defaults and permissions** of the platforms you use (as a user) and the platforms you build (as a developer). Over-broad data access is a vulnerability even when no exploit is involved.

### 🧩 Try It Yourself

**Challenge 1:** Your friend says "incognito mode makes me invisible online." Correct them precisely.

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

Incognito only prevents the *local* browser from persisting history, cookies, and form data after the window closes. It does **nothing** to hide you from the websites you visit, your ISP, your employer/school network, or DNS resolvers — all of which see your traffic in real time. It also doesn't stop fingerprinting. It protects you from the *next person using your device*, not from the network or the sites.

</details>

**Challenge 2:** You set `HttpOnly` on a session cookie. Which attack does that blunt, and which does it *not* help with at all?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

`HttpOnly` stops **JavaScript** from reading the cookie, so a cross-site scripting (XSS, Module 14) payload can't exfiltrate the session token via `document.cookie`. It does **not** help against **CSRF** — the browser still *attaches* the cookie to cross-site requests automatically; that's what `SameSite` (and CSRF tokens) are for. Different flags defend different attacks — this is defense in depth.

</details>

### 🛡️ Pitfalls & False-Confidence Traps
- **"VPN = anonymous."** It relocates trust to the provider and does nothing if you're logged into accounts. Confidentiality ≠ anonymity.
- **"Cleared cookies = untracked."** Fingerprinting survives cookie clearing and incognito. Cookie hygiene is necessary, not sufficient.
- **Trusting a "no-logs" VPN claim.** It's unverifiable marketing for most providers; pick audited ones and assume the provider *can* see your traffic.
- **Shipping cookies without flags.** Framework defaults are often permissive. Set `Secure; HttpOnly; SameSite` explicitly — silence is a vulnerability.
- **Over-collecting as a developer.** Cambridge Analytica was a permissions-and-defaults failure, not an exploit. Collect the minimum; least data is least liability.

### ❓ Quiz
1. A VPN encrypts your traffic. Does it make you anonymous? Why or why not?
2. Your friend says incognito mode makes them invisible online. Correct them precisely.
3. What is browser fingerprinting, and why is it hard to defend against by clearing cookies?
4. As a developer, what do the `Secure`, `HttpOnly`, and `SameSite` cookie flags each defend against?
5. Why is Cambridge Analytica described as a privacy failure even though "no one was hacked"?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Answers</strong></summary>

1. No. It hides your IP from sites and your traffic from the local network/ISP, but the VPN provider sees everything and logged-in accounts identify you regardless. It's confidentiality on untrusted networks, not anonymity.
2. Incognito only stops the local browser from *saving* history/cookies after the window closes. Websites, ISP, employer/school, and DNS still see all traffic live; fingerprinting still works.
3. Fingerprinting identifies you by stable browser/device traits (canvas/WebGL render, fonts, resolution, time zone) that need no stored cookie, so clearing cookies doesn't remove it. Defense needs fingerprint *resistance*, not just cookie deletion.
4. `Secure` = only sent over HTTPS (no plaintext leak); `HttpOnly` = JavaScript can't read it (blunts XSS token theft); `SameSite` = not sent on cross-site requests (blunts CSRF and cross-site tracking).
5. Because the harm came from over-broad data access granted *by design* and accepted via terms of service — a defaults/permissions failure. Privacy is about who can access data and why, not only about break-ins.

</details>

### ✅ Progress Tracker
- [ ] Firefox hardened with Strict ETP, HTTPS-Only, and 3+ privacy extensions
- [ ] Ran the EFF Cover Your Tracks test with and without blocking
- [ ] Inspected a real site's `Set-Cookie` flags with `curl`
- [ ] Audited and trimmed app permissions on my Mac and phone
- [ ] I can explain what `Secure`, `HttpOnly`, and `SameSite` each defend against

### ➡️ Next Step
Module 6 — **Operating System Hardening (Mac-First)**. You've secured the browser, the layer where most attacks arrive. Next we harden the layer *underneath* it — the operating system itself: the firewall, disk encryption, automatic patching (which, recall from Module 4, is what actually denies worms), and least-privilege accounts.

## Module 6 — Operating System Hardening (Mac-First)

### 🎯 Mission Briefing
Your laptop is where you live and where you build. This module turns it from a default consumer device into a hardened workstation without breaking usability — and connects each OS control to its server-side analogue, because the same principles (least privilege, patch fast, encrypt at rest, minimize attack surface) protect the production hosts you'll deploy to.

### 📘 Theory

#### Define the words first

- **Hardening** — systematically reducing **attack surface** while preserving needed function. Every disabled service, removed app, and applied patch is one fewer way in.
- **Attack surface** — the sum of all points where an attacker could try to enter or extract data (open ports, running services, installed apps, granted permissions, exposed APIs).
- **Least privilege** — every user, process, and service runs with the *minimum* rights it needs and no more. The single most important systems-security principle.
- **Full-disk encryption (FDE)** — encrypting the entire drive at rest so a stolen/lost device's data is unreadable without the key (FileVault on macOS, BitLocker on Windows, LUKS on Linux).
- **Patch** — a vendor fix for a known vulnerability. **Auto-update** turns patching into a default, which is what denies *worms* (recall Module 4: worms spread only through unpatched holes).

#### Why the naive posture fails

> **Naive #1: "It's a Mac, it doesn't get viruses."**
> Fails because platform reputation is not a control. macOS has had real in-the-wild malware, supply-chain attacks, and zero-click spyware (Pegasus, below). Safety comes from *enabled defenses* (Gatekeeper, XProtect, FileVault, updates), not from the logo.

> **Naive #2: "I'll patch when I get around to it."**
> Fails because the window between a patch's release and mass exploitation is now *hours*. Attackers reverse-engineer the patch to find the bug it fixes, then hunt for unpatched machines. Deferring updates is choosing to stay exploitable. Auto-update is a security control.

> **Naive #3: "I work as admin/root because it's convenient."**
> Fails because then *every* program you run — including malware you trip over — inherits full power to read all files, install kernel extensions, and disable defenses. Least privilege means a compromised app can only damage what that app could already touch.

> **The correct posture:** enable the platform's defenses, encrypt at rest, auto-update, run as a standard (non-admin) user for daily work, and remove what you don't use. The same four ideas — least privilege, patch, encrypt, minimize — are exactly how you'll harden a production server.

#### macOS built-in defenses (learn them by name)
- **XProtect** — Apple's anti-malware signatures, auto-updated.
- **Gatekeeper** — blocks unsigned/unnotarized apps by default.
- **SIP (System Integrity Protection)** — blocks even `root` from modifying system files. **Never disable it.**
- **TCC (Transparency, Consent, Control)** — the per-app permission prompts (camera, mic, files).
- **FileVault** — full-disk encryption.
- **Secure Enclave** — hardware chip that isolates biometric data and keys.
- **Lockdown Mode** — extreme attack-surface reduction for high-risk users.

**Windows equivalents:** Windows Defender, SmartScreen, Credential Guard, BitLocker, Windows Hello, TPM 2.0. **Linux equivalents:** AppArmor/SELinux, UFW firewall, LUKS encryption, PAM.

#### The developer parallel: least privilege for services

Hardening your laptop and hardening a server are the same discipline. A web app must **not** run as root: if it's compromised (e.g., via a code-execution bug), least privilege limits the blast radius to what the service account can touch — not the whole host. You drop privileges after binding the port, and you never run the app process as an administrator.

### 🛠️ Lab: Harden macOS & Verify the Controls (M2 / Apple Silicon)

> ⚖️ **Ethics box.** Every command here inspects or configures **your own machine**. Read-only status checks are safe; the toggles change *your* settings only.

```bash
mkdir -p ~/cyber-journal/m6 && cd ~/cyber-journal/m6

# 1. FileVault: System Settings > Privacy & Security > FileVault > Turn On.
#    Save the recovery key OFFLINE (paper/safe). Verify:
fdesetup status                      # "FileVault is On."

# 2. Firewall state (0=off, 1=on for specific, 2=essential-only):
sudo defaults read /Library/Preferences/com.apple.alf globalstate
#    Turn on at System Settings > Network > Firewall.

# 3. Require password immediately after sleep:
#    System Settings > Lock Screen > Require password = Immediately

# 4. Automatic updates (the most important setting):
sudo softwareupdate --schedule on

# 5. Verify core defenses are enabled:
spctl --status            # Gatekeeper: "assessments enabled"
csrutil status            # SIP: "enabled"  (NEVER disable)
sw_vers                   # macOS version - still supported?
```

**Windows (PowerShell, as Administrator):**
```powershell
Get-MpComputerStatus | Select-Object AntivirusEnabled, RealTimeProtectionEnabled
Enable-BitLocker -MountPoint "C:" -EncryptionMethod XtsAes256 -UsedSpaceOnly -TpmProtector
Get-NetFirewallProfile | Select-Object Name, Enabled
```

**Linux (Ubuntu):**
```bash
sudo ufw enable && sudo ufw status verbose
sudo apt update && sudo apt upgrade -y
sudo dpkg-reconfigure -plow unattended-upgrades   # enable automatic security updates
```

### 💻 Vulnerable vs. Secure Code — least privilege for a service

```javascript
// ❌ VULNERABLE — Node service started and left running as root
// $ sudo node server.js   (listening on :80)
const http = require("http");
http.createServer(handler).listen(80);
// WHY WRONG: a code-execution bug in `handler` now runs AS ROOT — it can read
// every file, install persistence, and disable defenses. Max blast radius.
```

```javascript
// ✅ SECURE — bind the privileged port, then DROP to a low-privilege user
const http = require("http");
const server = http.createServer(handler);
server.listen(80, () => {
  // WHY IT WORKS: only the brief bind needs privilege. After binding we shed it,
  // so any later compromise is confined to the unprivileged 'appuser'.
  process.setgid("appuser");
  process.setuid("appuser");   // irreversible drop; cannot regain root
});
// Better still: don't run as root at all — listen on :3000 and let nginx/systemd
// (with AmbientCapabilities=CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE) handle the privileged port.
```

```python
# ❌ VULNERABLE — Python service run as root
# $ sudo python3 server.py   (binds :80, stays root)
# WHY WRONG: identical problem — any RCE in a handler executes with full root power.

# ✅ SECURE — drop privileges after binding (POSIX, same idea as Node)
import os, socket
sock = socket.socket(); sock.bind(("0.0.0.0", 80)); sock.listen()
# Drop group first, then user (order matters: you lose the right to setgid after setuid)
os.setgid(__import__("grp").getgrnam("appuser").gr_gid)
os.setuid(__import__("pwd").getpwnam("appuser").pw_uid)
# WHY IT WORKS: the process can no longer act as root; a later exploit is contained.
# Or: run as a non-root user under systemd/gunicorn and reverse-proxy the low port.
```

### 🌍 Case Study — The Pegasus Spyware
NSO Group's Pegasus infected journalists' and activists' iPhones via **zero-click** iMessage exploits — no tap required. Apple's response was **Lockdown Mode**, which *intentionally removes features* (some message types, some web technologies, some connection types) to shrink attack surface for the small fraction of users genuinely targeted. The lesson is the core theme of this module: **security is attack-surface management and always trades against convenience.** Different users (and different servers) need different levels — a kiosk should be locked far harder than a developer's daily laptop.

### 🧩 Try It Yourself

**Challenge 1:** Find out what version of macOS you're running and whether it still receives security updates.

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

```bash
sw_vers
```
Apple typically ships security updates for the current macOS plus the previous two major versions. If you're three or more versions behind, you're exposed to known, unpatched vulnerabilities — upgrade or, on unsupported hardware, isolate the machine.

</details>

**Challenge 2:** Why does dropping from root to a low-privilege user *after* binding port 80 give you the best of both worlds?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

Binding to a port below 1024 requires privilege, so you start with it just long enough to grab the port. Immediately dropping to an unprivileged account means that for the entire *lifetime* of request handling — where the bugs and exploits live — the process has no root power. A successful exploit is then confined to what `appuser` can touch, not the whole system. It's least privilege applied across time: privileged for a moment, unprivileged forever after.

</details>

### 🛡️ Pitfalls & False-Confidence Traps
- **"Macs don't get malware."** Reputation isn't a control. Enabled defenses are.
- **Disabling SIP "to fix something."** SIP protects system files from even root. Disabling it removes a foundational defense; almost no legitimate task requires it.
- **Deferring updates.** The patch-to-exploit window is hours. Auto-update or stay exploitable.
- **Daily-driving an admin account.** Every app you run inherits admin power, including malware. Use a standard account for daily work.
- **Running services as root in production.** The laptop lesson scales: drop privileges, reverse-proxy privileged ports, and let a compromise stay small.

### ❓ Quiz
1. What does FileVault protect you from, and what does it *not* protect you from?
2. If an app requests "Full Disk Access," what should you consider before granting it?
3. Why is automatic updating arguably the single most important security setting?
4. What is least privilege, and how does "bind port 80 as root, then drop to a low-privilege user" apply it?
5. Why should a production web app process never run as root?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Answers</strong></summary>

1. FileVault protects **data at rest** — a lost or stolen *powered-off* device is unreadable without the key. It does **not** protect a *running, unlocked* machine, nor against malware, phishing, or network attacks while you're logged in.
2. Full Disk Access lets the app read *everything*, including other apps' private data, Mail, Messages, and backups. Grant it only to trusted tools that genuinely need it (e.g., backup software), and revoke it when no longer needed.
3. Because the time between a patch's release and mass exploitation is now hours — attackers reverse the patch to find the bug. Auto-updating closes known holes before they're weaponized against you, and is what denies worms.
4. Least privilege = every actor runs with the minimum rights it needs. Binding port 80 needs privilege only momentarily; dropping to a low-privilege user immediately after means request handling (where exploits occur) has no root power, confining any compromise.
5. Because a code-execution bug in a root process executes *as root* — full access to all files, the ability to install persistence and disable defenses. Running unprivileged (and reverse-proxying the low port) limits the blast radius of any compromise.

</details>

### ✅ Progress Tracker
- [ ] FileVault on, recovery key stored offline; verified with `fdesetup status`
- [ ] Firewall enabled; Gatekeeper and SIP confirmed enabled
- [ ] Automatic updates on
- [ ] Daily work happens in a standard (non-admin) account
- [ ] Reviewed installed apps and revoked unneeded permissions
- [ ] I can explain how dropping privileges after binding a port applies least privilege

Module 7 — **Command Line Fundamentals**. Your machine is hardened. Now we make you fluent in the environment where real security work happens: the shell. Every lab from here on lives at the command line, so this module is the gateway to Level 2.

## Module 7 — Command Line Fundamentals

### 🎯 Mission Briefing
The command line is where real security work lives — every lab from here on runs in a shell. This is also a security topic in its own right: the shell is where you read logs, inspect processes, and *set file permissions*, the oldest access-control mechanism on the machine. You'll go from zero to fluent, and you'll connect Unix permissions to the code that creates secret files in any language.

### 📘 Theory

#### Define the words first

- **Shell** — a program that reads typed text, interprets it, and runs other programs. `zsh` (macOS default), `bash`, `fish`, and `pwsh` (PowerShell) are shells.
- **Pipe (`|`)** — connects the output of one command to the input of the next, so small tools compose into pipelines. The heart of the **Unix philosophy**: small tools that each do one thing well, chained together.
- **stdin / stdout / stderr** — the three standard streams: input, normal output, and error output. Redirection (`>`, `>>`, `2>`) and pipes route them.
- **File permissions** — Unix access control. Each file has read/write/execute bits for **owner / group / others**, written as three octal digits (e.g., `600` = owner read+write, nobody else; `644` = owner read+write, everyone read).
- **`chmod` / `chown`** — change a file's permission bits / change its owner.

#### Why permissions are a security control (and the naive mistake)

> **Naive: "It's my laptop, permissions don't matter."**
> Fails because the moment a file holds a secret — an SSH private key, an API token, a `.env` file — its permission bits decide who (which user *or process*) can read it. On a shared host or after a partial compromise, a world-readable secret (`644`) is readable by every account and many services. SSH itself **refuses to use a private key** that is group/world-readable, precisely because loose permissions are an exposure. The correct posture: secrets are `600` (owner-only), and code that writes them sets that mode *at creation*, not after.

#### The pipe model in one line

`command A | command B` means "run A, feed its output as B's input." `ps aux | grep node | wc -l` = list processes → keep the node ones → count them. You build investigations this way: each stage narrows the data.

### 🛠️ Lab: Essential Commands & Permission Hygiene (M2 / Apple Silicon)

> ⚖️ **Ethics box.** Everything here runs against **your own files and processes**. Inspecting your own `~` directory and your own running processes is completely safe and is exactly what defenders do daily.

```bash
mkdir -p ~/cyber-journal/m7 && cd ~/cyber-journal/m7

# Navigation
pwd; ls -la; cd ~ ; cd -        # where am I / list (with hidden) / home / back

# Files & streams
echo "hi"  > f.txt              # write (overwrite via stdout redirect)
echo "bye" >> f.txt             # append
cat f.txt; head -n1 f.txt; tail -f /var/log/system.log   # Ctrl+C to stop following

# Searching
grep -ri "password" ~/cyber-journal   # recursive, case-insensitive
find ~ -name "*.pem" 2>/dev/null      # locate key files (suppress permission errors)

# Piping - the investigation pattern
ps aux | grep -i node | grep -v grep | wc -l   # how many node processes?
history | tail -20

# Permissions - the security part
printf 'SECRET_API_KEY=abc123\n' > .env
ls -l .env                      # likely -rw-r--r-- (644): readable by EVERYONE
chmod 600 .env                  # owner-only; SSH-grade hygiene for secrets
ls -l .env                      # now -rw------- (600)
chmod +x somescript.sh          # make a script executable
```

**Windows PowerShell equivalents:**

| Unix | PowerShell |
|---|---|
| `ls` | `Get-ChildItem` / `dir` |
| `cat` | `Get-Content` |
| `grep` | `Select-String` |
| `ps` | `Get-Process` |
| `find` | `Get-ChildItem -Recurse -Filter "*.pem"` |
| `chmod 600` | `icacls file /inheritance:r /grant:r "$env:USERNAME:F"` |

### 💻 Vulnerable vs. Secure Code — creating a secrets file with correct permissions

```javascript
// ❌ VULNERABLE — Node: write a secret with default (world-readable) permissions
const fs = require("fs");
fs.writeFileSync(".env", "API_KEY=sk_live_123");
// WHY WRONG: default mode is typically 0644 -> every user/process on the host can
// READ the secret. On a shared or partially-compromised box, that's an exposure.
```

```javascript
// ✅ SECURE — Node: create owner-only (0600) atomically at write time
const fs = require("fs");
fs.writeFileSync(".env", "API_KEY=sk_live_123", { mode: 0o600 });
// WHY IT WORKS: the file is created with read/write for the OWNER ONLY, so the
// secret is never momentarily world-readable. Setting mode AT creation avoids the
// race where chmod-after-write briefly leaves it exposed.
```

```python
# ❌ VULNERABLE — Python: default permissions on a secrets file
with open(".env", "w") as f:
    f.write("API_KEY=sk_live_123")
# WHY WRONG: created ~0644 (minus umask) -> readable by other users/processes.

# ✅ SECURE — Python: open with an owner-only mode, created restricted from the start
import os
fd = os.open(".env", os.O_WRONLY | os.O_CREAT | os.O_TRUNC, 0o600)
with os.fdopen(fd, "w") as f:
    f.write("API_KEY=sk_live_123")
# WHY IT WORKS: os.open applies mode 0o600 at creation (subject to umask), so the
# file is owner-only from the first instant - same 600 hygiene as the shell lab.
```

### 🌍 Case Study — World-Readable Secrets in the Wild
A recurring class of real incidents: services that wrote `config.php`, `.env`, or cloud-credential files with default permissions, then a second low-privilege account or a Local File Inclusion bug (Module 14) read them and harvested the keys. SSH's hard refusal to load a `644` private key (`UNPROTECTED PRIVATE KEY FILE!`) is a designed-in defense against exactly this. The lesson: permission bits are not bureaucracy — they are the boundary between "one bug" and "full credential theft."

### 🧩 Try It Yourself

**Challenge 1:** Find the 5 largest files in your home directory using a pipeline.

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

```bash
du -ah ~ 2>/dev/null | sort -hr | head -n 5
```
`du -ah` = disk usage for all files (human-readable); `sort -hr` = sort human-readable, reverse (biggest first); `head -n 5` = top 5. Each stage of the pipe transforms the previous stage's output.

</details>

**Challenge 2:** Your teammate's deploy script does `open(path, "w")` then `os.chmod(path, 0o600)` on the next line. Why is creating the file with mode `0o600` directly still safer?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

There's a **race window** between the `open` (which creates the file world-readable) and the `chmod`. In that gap — even microseconds — another process can open and read the secret. Creating the file restricted from the first instant (via `os.open(..., 0o600)` or `writeFileSync(..., {mode:0o600})`) eliminates the window entirely. Security bugs love the gap between "do the thing" and "secure the thing."

</details>

### 🛡️ Pitfalls & False-Confidence Traps
- **`chmod 777` "to make it work."** That grants everyone read/write/execute — the opposite of least privilege. If something needs broader access, find the *specific* bit it needs.
- **`chmod` after create instead of at create.** Leaves a race window where the secret is exposed. Set the mode atomically.
- **`grep -r` from `/`.** Powerful but can crawl the whole disk for minutes and surface sensitive paths; scope your searches.
- **Trusting `sudo` muscle memory.** Running every command with `sudo` reintroduces the root-everywhere problem from Module 6. Use it only when a command genuinely needs it.

### ❓ Quiz
1. What does the `|` character do, and why does it embody the Unix philosophy?
2. What does `chmod 600` mean, bit by bit, and why is it the right mode for a secrets file?
3. Why is `grep -r` both powerful and risky on a large drive?
4. Why is creating a file with mode `0o600` safer than creating it default and `chmod`-ing it afterward?
5. Why does SSH refuse to use a private key that is group- or world-readable?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Answers</strong></summary>

1. `|` pipes one command's stdout into the next command's stdin, so small single-purpose tools compose into pipelines — the essence of "do one thing well and chain them."
2. `600` = owner: read(4)+write(2)=6; group: 0; others: 0. Only the owner can read or write; no one else can even read it — correct for a file holding a secret.
3. It recursively searches every file under a path, which is great for hunting but can take a long time and traverse sensitive areas if pointed at `/` or a huge tree. Scope it.
4. Creating default-then-`chmod` leaves a race window in which the file is world-readable and another process can read the secret. Creating it `0o600` makes it owner-only from the first instant, closing the window.
5. Because a key readable by others is effectively exposed — any user or process with that access can copy and use it. SSH treats loose key permissions as an unacceptable exposure and refuses to proceed.

</details>

### ✅ Progress Tracker
- [ ] I can navigate the filesystem with no GUI
- [ ] I built a 3+ stage pipeline (e.g., `ps | grep | wc -l`)
- [ ] I can read `-rw-r--r--` and translate it to/from octal
- [ ] I set a secrets file to `600` and understand why
- [ ] I can write code (Node or Python) that creates a `600` file atomically

### ➡️ Next Step
Module 8 — **Level 1 Capstone: Secure Yourself**. You now have the foundations — CIA, networking, identity, malware, privacy, OS hardening, and the shell. The capstone makes you *apply* all of it: a full personal security audit, written like a professional assessment.

## Module 8 — Level 1 Capstone: Secure Yourself

### 🎯 The Mission
Build a **Personal Security Audit Report** on yourself — as if a client hired you to assess their digital life — and *remediate* the findings. This capstone integrates every Level 1 module (CIA, networking, identity/hashing/MFA, malware/phishing, privacy, OS hardening, the shell) into one deliverable: a professional-style assessment report in your Cyber Journal, ~2–4 pages, plus the fixes you actually applied.

> ⚖️ **Ethics & scope box.** The *only* system in scope is **your own** — your devices, your accounts, your networks. This is the safest possible audit target and the same discipline you'll use on authorized engagements later: define scope first, document, then remediate. Do not audit anyone else's accounts or devices, even family, without explicit permission.

#### Why this format

Real penetration-test and security-assessment reports follow a fixed skeleton: scope → methodology → findings (each with severity, evidence, and remediation) → summary. Practicing that skeleton on yourself — where you have full authorization and full context — builds the habit before it ever matters professionally. A finding without a remediation is just anxiety; this capstone requires you to *fix*, not just *find*.

### Required Sections

1. **Scope & Inventory** — every device you own, every online account (use your password manager's vault as the source of truth; you'll be shocked at the count), and every network you regularly join.
2. **CIA Analysis** — for your 5 most important accounts, which CIA property (Module 1) matters most and why (e.g., bank = integrity + availability; medical = confidentiality).
3. **Authentication Audit** — how many accounts have unique passwords? MFA enabled (app/passkey, not SMS — Module 3)? Which appear on Have I Been Pwned?
4. **Endpoint Audit** — OS version and patch status, disk encryption (FileVault), firewall, automatic updates, daily-driver account is non-admin (Module 6).
5. **Privacy Audit** — browser hardened (Module 5), social-media privacy reviewed, app permissions trimmed.
6. **Findings Table** — each finding with a **severity** (Critical/High/Medium/Low), the **evidence** (a command output or screenshot), and a **remediation** (what you did, with the date).
7. **Three Biggest Risks** — ranked, each with a concrete mitigation plan.
8. **Reflection** — one thing you learned that genuinely surprised you.

#### Worked example of one finding (use this format)

> **Finding F-01 — Reused password on 7 accounts.** *Severity: High.*
> **Evidence:** Bitwarden Vault Health report flagged 7 reused credentials, 2 of which appear in a known breach (HIBP).
> **Impact:** Credential stuffing (Module 3/4) — one leaked password unlocks 7 accounts.
> **Remediation (applied 2026-06-17):** Generated unique 20-char passwords for all 7; enabled an authenticator app on the 3 highest-value ones. Re-ran the report: 0 reused.

### Scoring Rubric
| Area | Points |
|---|---|
| Scope defined + inventory complete | 15 |
| CIA mapping accurate | 15 |
| All critical accounts MFA'd (app/passkey) | 20 |
| OS hardened (encryption, firewall, updates, non-admin) | 15 |
| Browser & privacy hardened | 10 |
| Findings written with severity + evidence + remediation | 15 |
| Reflection | 10 |
| **Total** | **100** |

Score yourself honestly, and **keep this document** — you'll re-run and re-score it at the end of Level 3 to measure how far you've come.

> 🎖️ **Recruit Badge Earned** — congratulations. You've built the foundation 80% of computer users never do, and you've practiced the find-and-fix report discipline that defines professional security work.

### ➡️ Next Step
Level 2 — **Operator**, beginning with Module 9 — **Networking Deep Dive**. You secured yourself as an end user; now you build the technical depth to understand, capture, and analyze the networks every attack traverses and every defense monitors.

# 🎖️ LEVEL 2 — OPERATOR (Intermediate)
### Weeks 9–20 | Goal: Build technical depth in networks, crypto, web, and defense

You finish Level 2 able to spin up a virtual lab, capture and analyze network traffic, understand the OWASP Top 10, write basic scripts, and think in terms of both offense and defense.

> ### 🗺️ Level 2 Roadmap — Operator · Weeks 9–20
> **The promise:** Level 2 turns knowledge into capability: you scan networks, capture and read packets, exploit the OWASP Top 10, and stand up your own isolated lab — then defend it like a blue-teamer.
>
> **By the end you can —**
> - Scan and enumerate a network with Nmap, and write your own scanner
> - Capture and analyze real traffic in Wireshark
> - Exploit and then defend the OWASP Top 10 on a lab target you own
> - Build an isolated pentest lab and monitor it for attacks
>
> **You'll build:** an attack-and-defend exercise against your own virtual lab (Level 2 capstone, Module 17).
> **Prerequisite:** Level 1.

> 🖥️ **Platform note for the rest of the course (read once).** From Level 2 on, labs assume a **Unix shell** — macOS, Linux, or **WSL2** on Windows. Windows learners have two equally good options: (1) run every lab inside **WSL2** (set up in Module 0), where the commands work unchanged, or (2) run them inside the **Kali/pentest VM you build in Module 15**, which is the same for Mac and Windows users. Either way, the "M2 / Apple Silicon" label on each lab is about the *host you install on*, not a restriction — the commands themselves are the same across macOS, Linux, and WSL2. Where an OS difference is a genuine *security concept* (for example file permissions — `chmod` vs Windows `icacls`, first shown in Module 7), it's called out inline.

## Module 9 — Networking Deep Dive: OSI, TCP/IP, Ports

### 🎯 Mission Briefing
Every attack traverses a network and every defense monitors one, so without deep networking you will plateau. This module makes the OSI model, TCP/UDP, and ports *muscle memory* — then connects them to the single most common accidental exposure a developer ships: binding a service to the whole network instead of localhost. You'll scan your own machine and learn to explain every open port.

### 📘 Theory

#### Define the words first

- **Protocol** — an agreed set of rules for how two parties exchange data (HTTP, TCP, DNS are protocols).
- **OSI model** — a 7-layer conceptual map of networking, from physical bits up to application data. It lets you reason about *where* a problem or control lives.
- **Port** — a numbered endpoint on a host (0–65535) that identifies *which service* a connection is for. An IP address finds the machine; the port finds the program.
- **Listening / bound** — a service is "listening" on a port when it's accepting connections there. The **bind address** decides *who* can reach it: `127.0.0.1` (localhost only) vs `0.0.0.0` (every network interface — i.e., the whole network).
- **Firewall** — a control that allows/denies traffic based on rules (typically by port/IP at L3/L4; modern "next-gen" firewalls also inspect L7 application content).

#### The OSI 7-layer model (mnemonic: "Please Do Not Throw Sausage Pizza Away")

| # | Layer | What it does | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| 7 | Application | User-facing protocols | HTTP, DNS, SSH |
| 6 | Presentation | Format, encrypt | TLS, JPEG |
| 5 | Session | Keep conversations alive | Login sessions |
| 4 | Transport | Reliable/unreliable delivery | TCP, UDP |
| 3 | Network | Routing between networks | IP, ICMP |
| 2 | Data Link | Local network delivery | Ethernet, MAC |
| 1 | Physical | Actual bits on wire/radio | Cables, Wi-Fi |

Why it matters for security: every control and every attack lives at a layer. A firewall filters mostly at L3/L4 (IP/port); TLS protects L6; SQL injection is an L7 problem no firewall port-rule can stop. Knowing the layer tells you which defense applies.

#### TCP vs. UDP

- **TCP** — reliable, ordered, acknowledged. The "phone call." Web, email, SSH.
- **UDP** — fast, fire-and-forget, no delivery guarantee. The "postcard." DNS, video calls, games.

#### Ports you must know cold

| Port | Service | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| 22 | SSH | Remote secure login |
| 23 | Telnet | Remote login, **no encryption — dead** |
| 25 | SMTP | Email send |
| 53 | DNS | Name resolution (mostly UDP) |
| 80 | HTTP | Web, **unencrypted** |
| 443 | HTTPS | Web, encrypted |
| 445 | SMB | Windows file sharing (WannaCry/ransomware vector) |
| 3306 | MySQL | Database — **never expose to the internet** |
| 3389 | RDP | Windows Remote Desktop — heavily attacked |
| 5432 | PostgreSQL | Database — **never expose to the internet** |
| 8080 | HTTP-alt | Common dev/proxy port |

#### Why the naive posture fails

> **Naive #1: "I closed all my ports, so I'm safe."**
> Fails because a *useful* server must keep some ports open (you can't serve a website with port 443 closed). Security isn't "zero open ports" — it's "every open port is *intended*, *patched*, *authenticated*, and *exposed only to who needs it*." The goal is a known, minimal, hardened surface, not zero.

> **Naive #2: "My dev database is only on my laptop, so binding it to 0.0.0.0 is fine."**
> Fails because `0.0.0.0` means *every* network interface — including the coffee-shop Wi-Fi you're on. A database bound to `0.0.0.0` with a weak/no password is reachable by everyone on that network. Countless breaches are exposed dev databases. Bind internal services to `127.0.0.1`.

> **The correct posture:** minimize the listening surface, bind internal services to localhost (or a private network), authenticate everything, and put a firewall in front. Knowing *which* ports are open and *why* is the first step — which is exactly the lab.

### 🛠️ Lab: Scan Your Own Machine & See Bind Addresses (M2 / Apple Silicon)

> ⚖️ **Ethics box.** You will scan **only your own machine** (`localhost` / `127.0.0.1`). Port-scanning machines you don't own or have written permission to test is hostile activity and can be illegal. `nmap` against your own host is completely fine and is what every defender does to inventory their own surface.

```bash
mkdir -p ~/cyber-journal/m9 && cd ~/cyber-journal/m9

# What's listening on YOUR machine, and on WHICH address?
sudo lsof -iTCP -sTCP:LISTEN -P -n
# Read the NAME column: 127.0.0.1:5432 = localhost-only (good for a DB)
#                       *:5432 or 0.0.0.0:5432 = ALL interfaces (exposed!)

# ARM64-native nmap via Homebrew - scan your own loopback:
brew install nmap
nmap -sT -p- 127.0.0.1        # full TCP port sweep of your own host
nmap -sV -p 5432,3306 127.0.0.1   # service/version detection on DB ports

# Identify the process behind a mystery port:
sudo lsof -i :5000
```

Note every open port and write *why* each is open in your journal.

**Linux:** `sudo ss -tulpn`. **Windows:** `Get-NetTCPConnection -State Listen | Format-Table LocalAddress, LocalPort, OwningProcess` (LocalAddress `0.0.0.0` = exposed).

### 💻 Vulnerable vs. Secure Code — bind address (the accidental-exposure bug)

```javascript
// ❌ VULNERABLE — Node: binds to ALL interfaces by default
const app = require("express")();
app.listen(5432);                 // or app.listen(5432, "0.0.0.0")
// WHY WRONG: with no host argument, it listens on 0.0.0.0 — every interface,
// including the public/Wi-Fi network. An internal service is now internet/LAN-reachable.
```

```javascript
// ✅ SECURE — Node: bind internal services to localhost only
const app = require("express")();
app.listen(5432, "127.0.0.1");
// WHY IT WORKS: 127.0.0.1 is the loopback interface — reachable only from THIS
// machine. Other hosts on the network cannot connect at all. Expose externally
// only through an authenticated reverse proxy when you truly need to.
```

```python
# ❌ VULNERABLE — Python/Flask: 0.0.0.0 exposes the dev server to the whole network
app.run(host="0.0.0.0", port=5000)
# WHY WRONG: same problem — anyone on your LAN/Wi-Fi can reach the dev server.

# ✅ SECURE — Python/Flask: localhost-only bind
app.run(host="127.0.0.1", port=5000)
# WHY IT WORKS: loopback-only; unreachable from other machines. Same principle as Node —
# the bind address is an HTTP/socket concept, not a framework feature.
```

### 🌍 Case Study — Exposed Databases (MongoDB/Elasticsearch ransom waves)
Throughout the late 2010s, automated scanners swept the internet for databases bound to `0.0.0.0` with default or no authentication. Tens of thousands of MongoDB and Elasticsearch instances were found, their data copied, deleted, and held for ransom — purely because the service was *reachable* and *unauthenticated*. No exploit of a code bug was needed; the "vulnerability" was the bind address plus missing auth. This is the single most preventable breach class and maps directly to this module's lab: know what's listening, and on which interface.

### 🧩 Try It Yourself

**Challenge 1:** Your laptop shows port 5000 open. What's a likely reason, and how do you investigate?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

Common causes: a Flask or Node dev server, Apple's AirPlay Receiver (macOS used 5000), or a Docker container. Investigate with `sudo lsof -i :5000` to see the owning process, then decide whether it should be running — and critically, whether it's bound to `127.0.0.1` (fine) or `0.0.0.0` (exposed to your network).

</details>

**Challenge 2:** Why is "I closed all my ports, I'm safe" a flawed statement?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

A functioning server *must* keep needed ports open (you can't serve HTTPS with 443 closed). Security is not "zero open ports" — it's that every open port is *intended*, *patched*, *authenticated*, and *exposed only to those who need it*. The objective is a known, minimal, hardened surface, not an empty one. Also, "closed" only describes inbound listeners; outbound connections and application-layer (L7) attacks are unaffected.

</details>

### 🛡️ Pitfalls & False-Confidence Traps
- **Binding internal services to `0.0.0.0`.** Default in many frameworks; exposes the service to the whole network. Use `127.0.0.1` unless you deliberately need external reach (then authenticate + firewall).
- **"It's behind a firewall, so auth doesn't matter."** Firewalls fail open, get misconfigured, and don't stop insiders or pivoting attackers. Authenticate every service regardless.
- **Confusing IP-found with port-found.** The IP locates the host; the port selects the service. A scan that shows a port "open" means a service is *listening and reachable from where you scanned*.
- **Telnet/FTP/HTTP for anything sensitive.** They're plaintext (L7 over no encryption). Use SSH/SFTP/HTTPS.
- **Trusting a firewall to stop SQL injection.** That's an L7 application bug; a port rule can't see it. Different layers need different controls.

### ❓ Quiz
1. Which OSI layer does a traditional firewall usually filter at, and what do modern "next-gen" firewalls additionally inspect?
2. Why does DNS mostly use UDP rather than TCP?
3. Your friend says "I closed all my ports, I'm safe." What's wrong with that?
4. What is the difference between binding a service to `127.0.0.1` versus `0.0.0.0`, and why does it matter for security?
5. Tens of thousands of databases were ransomed without any code exploit. What two configuration facts made them vulnerable?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Answers</strong></summary>

1. Traditionally L3/L4 (IP addresses and TCP/UDP ports). Next-gen firewalls also inspect L7 application content (the actual HTTP, the app protocol) to make smarter allow/deny decisions.
2. DNS lookups are tiny, frequent, and latency-sensitive; UDP's connectionless, no-handshake model is faster and the application can simply retry on loss. (DNS falls back to TCP for large responses and zone transfers.)
3. A useful server must keep some ports open. Security is "every open port is intended, patched, authenticated, and minimally exposed," not "zero open ports." It also ignores outbound traffic and L7 attacks.
4. `127.0.0.1` (loopback) is reachable only from the same machine; `0.0.0.0` binds every network interface, making the service reachable from the whole LAN/Wi-Fi (and possibly the internet). Internal services should bind to localhost to avoid accidental exposure.
5. They were (a) bound to a reachable interface (`0.0.0.0`) and (b) had default or no authentication. Reachable + unauthenticated = data theft, no exploit required.

</details>

### ✅ Progress Tracker
- [ ] I can recite the 7 OSI layers and give an example protocol at each
- [ ] I memorized 10+ ports and what each is for
- [ ] I scanned my own machine with `nmap` and `lsof` and explained every open port
- [ ] I can tell from `lsof` whether a service is bound to `127.0.0.1` or `0.0.0.0`
- [ ] I can write a Node or Python service that binds to localhost only

### ➡️ Next Step
Module 10 — **Cryptography Essentials**. You can see traffic on the wire now; next we learn the math that makes that traffic trustworthy — symmetric vs. asymmetric encryption, hashing, how HTTPS actually establishes a secure channel, and the common ways crypto is misused.
