## Module 10 — Cryptography Essentials

### 🎯 Mission Briefing
Cryptography is the math that makes the internet trustworthy. You won't design cryptosystems — almost nobody should — but you must deeply understand *how to use them correctly* and *how they fail*. The headline rule of this module: **don't roll your own crypto, and don't use the low-level primitives wrong.** You'll generate keys, inspect a real TLS certificate, and write encryption code the right way in Node and Python.

### 📘 Theory

#### Define the words first

- **Plaintext / ciphertext** — the readable input / the encrypted output.
- **Key** — the secret that controls encryption/decryption. **Confidentiality must rest on the secrecy of the key, never the algorithm** (Kerckhoffs's principle).
- **Symmetric encryption** — *same* key encrypts and decrypts. Fast. AES-256, ChaCha20. The problem it leaves open: how do two parties share the key safely?
- **Asymmetric (public-key) encryption** — a *key pair*: anyone encrypts with your **public** key; only you decrypt with your **private** key. RSA, ECC. ~1000× slower than symmetric.
- **Hash** — one-way, fixed-length fingerprint (SHA-256, SHA-3). For integrity and signatures; for *passwords*, use a slow hash (bcrypt/Argon2 — Module 3).
- **IV / nonce** — a unique, non-secret value fed into a cipher so the *same* plaintext+key produces *different* ciphertext each time. Reusing a nonce breaks many schemes catastrophically.
- **AEAD (Authenticated Encryption with Associated Data)** — a cipher mode (e.g., AES-GCM, ChaCha20-Poly1305) that provides both *confidentiality* and *integrity/authenticity* — so an attacker can't silently tamper with ciphertext. This is what you should use.
- **Digital signature** — hash the message, encrypt the hash with your *private* key; anyone with your public key verifies authenticity and integrity.

#### How HTTPS actually works (hybrid encryption)

1. Browser → server: "show me your certificate."
2. Server → browser: a **certificate** signed by a trusted **Certificate Authority (CA)**.
3. Browser verifies the CA signature chain (this is the part a self-signed/expired cert fails).
4. The two sides perform a key exchange to agree on a fresh **symmetric** session key.
5. All further traffic uses fast symmetric encryption.

Asymmetric (slow) is used only to *bootstrap* trust and the key; symmetric (fast) does the bulk work. TLS, PGP, and Signal all work this way — that's **hybrid encryption**.

#### Why the naive approaches fail

> **Naive #1: "I'll invent my own cipher / obfuscation."**
> Fails because security-by-obscurity collapses the moment the scheme is reverse-engineered (it always is). Vetted algorithms (AES, etc.) have survived decades of expert attack; your homemade XOR has not.

> **Naive #2: "I'll use AES, but in the default/ECB mode with a fixed IV."**
> Fails because **ECB mode** encrypts identical plaintext blocks to identical ciphertext blocks — patterns leak straight through (the famous "ECB penguin" image is still recognizable encrypted). And a reused/fixed IV in CBC/CTR/GCM destroys the guarantee. Use an **AEAD** mode (AES-GCM) with a **random per-message nonce**.

> **Naive #3: "Encryption alone keeps my data safe."**
> Fails because plain encryption gives confidentiality but not *integrity* — an attacker can flip ciphertext bits to corrupt or manipulate the decrypted result without knowing the key. AEAD adds an authentication tag that detects any tampering. Always authenticate your ciphertext.

> **The correct rule:** use a high-level, vetted library; pick an AEAD mode (AES-256-GCM or ChaCha20-Poly1305); use a unique random nonce per message; keep keys out of source code; and never invent your own primitive.

### 🛠️ Lab: Generate Keys, Encrypt, and Inspect a Real Certificate (M2 / Apple Silicon)

> ⚖️ **Ethics box.** You generate **your own** keys and encrypt **your own** files. Inspecting a public website's certificate (a read-only `openssl s_client` to a site that serves it to everyone) is fine. Don't use these tools against systems or data you don't own.

```bash
mkdir -p ~/cyber-journal/m10 && cd ~/cyber-journal/m10

# GnuPG ships or installs natively on arm64:
brew install gnupg
gpg --full-generate-key            # ECC (or RSA 4096), 2y expiry, your name/email
gpg --list-keys
echo "secret mission notes" > notes.txt
gpg --encrypt --recipient your@email.com notes.txt   # -> notes.txt.gpg
gpg --decrypt notes.txt.gpg

# Inspect a REAL TLS certificate (read-only; the site serves it publicly):
echo | openssl s_client -connect github.com:443 -servername github.com 2>/dev/null \
  | openssl x509 -noout -subject -issuer -dates
# Note the issuer (the CA), and the notBefore/notAfter validity window.

# See the avalanche/one-wayness of a hash again (ties back to Module 3):
echo -n "hello" | shasum -a 256
```

**Windows:** `winget install GnuPG.GnuPG`; certificate view via `Test-NetConnection` or browser DevTools → Security. **Linux:** `sudo apt install gnupg`.

### 💻 Vulnerable vs. Secure Code — symmetric encryption done wrong vs. right

```javascript
// ❌ VULNERABLE — Node: ECB mode + hard-coded key, no integrity
const crypto = require("crypto");
function encrypt(plain) {
  const key = Buffer.from("0123456789abcdef");          // WHY WRONG: key in source
  const c = crypto.createCipheriv("aes-128-ecb", key, null); // WHY WRONG: ECB leaks patterns
  return Buffer.concat([c.update(plain), c.final()]);    // WHY WRONG: no auth tag -> tamperable
}
```

```javascript
// ✅ SECURE — Node: AES-256-GCM (AEAD), key from env, random nonce per message
const crypto = require("crypto");
const key = Buffer.from(process.env.ENC_KEY_HEX, "hex");  // 32 bytes, NOT in source
function encrypt(plain) {
  const iv = crypto.randomBytes(12);                      // unique random nonce per message
  const c = crypto.createCipheriv("aes-256-gcm", key, iv);
  const ct = Buffer.concat([c.update(plain, "utf8"), c.final()]);
  const tag = c.getAuthTag();                             // WHY: integrity/authenticity
  return Buffer.concat([iv, tag, ct]);                    // store nonce+tag with ciphertext
}
// WHY IT WORKS: GCM gives confidentiality AND tamper-detection; the random IV means
// identical plaintexts encrypt differently; the key never appears in code.
```

```python
# ❌ VULNERABLE — Python: ECB + hard-coded key, no integrity
from Crypto.Cipher import AES
def encrypt(plain):
    key = b"0123456789abcdef"                 # WHY WRONG: key in source
    return AES.new(key, AES.MODE_ECB).encrypt(plain)  # WHY WRONG: ECB leaks; no auth

# ✅ SECURE — Python: AES-256-GCM via the high-level `cryptography` library
import os
from cryptography.hazmat.primitives.ciphers.aead import AESGCM
key = bytes.fromhex(os.environ["ENC_KEY_HEX"])   # 32 bytes, from env, not source
def encrypt(plain: bytes) -> bytes:
    nonce = os.urandom(12)                       # unique random nonce per message
    ct = AESGCM(key).encrypt(nonce, plain, None) # returns ciphertext + auth tag
    return nonce + ct
# WHY IT WORKS: AESGCM is AEAD (confidentiality + integrity); fresh nonce each call;
# key stays out of source. Same guarantees as the Node version — different stdlib.
```

### 🌍 Case Study — Heartbleed (2014)
A bug in **OpenSSL** — the library that handled TLS for a huge fraction of the internet — let an attacker request more data than they sent and read up to **64 KB of server memory per request**. That memory often contained the server's **private keys**, session cookies, and passwords in plaintext, all leaked with no trace. An estimated 17% of "secure" web servers were affected. The lesson cuts to the heart of this module: **strong algorithms don't save you if the implementation is buggy.** Crypto fails at the implementation layer at least as often as the math — which is exactly why you use vetted libraries and keep them patched (Module 7's supply-chain theme returns in Level 3).

### 🧩 Try It Yourself

**Challenge 1:** Why would you almost never use asymmetric encryption to encrypt an entire large file directly?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

Because asymmetric encryption is roughly 1000× slower than symmetric and has small size limits per operation. Real systems use asymmetric only to exchange (or wrap) a symmetric key, then encrypt the bulk data symmetrically — **hybrid encryption**, exactly what TLS, PGP/GPG, and Signal do.

</details>

**Challenge 2:** Two messages were encrypted with AES-CBC using the *same* IV and key. Why is that dangerous?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

An IV must be unique per message. Reusing it leaks relationships between plaintexts: with CBC, identical leading blocks produce identical ciphertext, revealing where two messages start the same; with CTR/GCM, nonce reuse is catastrophic and can leak the keystream or even the authentication key. Always use a fresh random nonce per message — which is why the secure code generates a new IV each call.

</details>

### 🛡️ Pitfalls & False-Confidence Traps
- **Rolling your own crypto.** Use vetted libraries. Your custom scheme will have a flaw experts would have caught.
- **ECB mode / fixed IV.** ECB leaks structure; reused IVs break confidentiality and integrity. Use AES-GCM with a random nonce.
- **Encryption without authentication.** Confidentiality ≠ integrity. Use AEAD so tampering is detected.
- **Hard-coding keys in source.** They leak via git history and bundles (Module 7). Load from a secret store/env.
- **Confusing encoding with encryption.** Base64 and hex are *encoding* (reversible by anyone), not encryption. "It's base64" protects nothing.
- **Assuming the algorithm is the weak point.** Heartbleed shows implementations and config (expired/self-signed certs, old protocol versions) fail more often than the math.

### ❓ Quiz
1. If a website's certificate expires, what actually breaks, and why?
2. Why are bcrypt and Argon2 *intentionally* slow, while AES is intentionally fast?
3. Why is ECB mode unsafe, and what should you use instead?
4. What does an AEAD mode (like AES-GCM) give you that plain AES-CBC does not?
5. Heartbleed used strong algorithms yet leaked private keys. What does that teach about where crypto fails?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Answers</strong></summary>

1. The browser can no longer verify the certificate's validity window, so the CA-signature/trust check fails and the browser shows a security warning / refuses the connection. The encryption math still works, but *trust* (you're talking to the real server) can't be established.
2. Password hashes must be slow to make offline guessing expensive per attempt (Module 3). Bulk encryption must be fast because it runs on every byte of traffic; its security comes from key secrecy, not slowness.
3. ECB encrypts identical plaintext blocks to identical ciphertext blocks, leaking structure/patterns. Use an AEAD mode such as AES-256-GCM with a unique random nonce per message.
4. Integrity and authenticity: an authentication tag that detects any tampering with the ciphertext. Plain CBC gives confidentiality only, so an attacker can manipulate ciphertext undetected.
5. That implementations and configuration fail at least as often as algorithms. Strong math in buggy/unpatched code (or with expired certs, weak protocol versions) is not secure — use vetted, patched libraries.

</details>

### ✅ Progress Tracker
- [ ] Generated a GPG key pair and encrypted/decrypted a file
- [ ] Inspected a real TLS certificate's issuer and validity window
- [ ] I can explain symmetric vs. asymmetric and why HTTPS uses both (hybrid)
- [ ] I can write AES-256-GCM encryption (Node or Python) with a random nonce and key-from-env
- [ ] I can explain why ECB and reused IVs are dangerous

### ➡️ Next Step
Module 11 — **Linux Power User**. Crypto secures the data; now you build fluency on the operating system that runs nearly every server, pentest tool, and CTF — Linux — including a Kali VM on your M2 via VMware Fusion or UTM.

## Module 11 — Linux Power User

### 🎯 Mission Briefing
Nearly every server, pentest tool, and CTF runs on Linux, so you must be at home on it. This module gives you the filesystem map, the user/privilege model, and process/service control — then turns to the security payoff: how a Linux power user *runs other programs safely*, because the moment your code shells out with user input, you've created the command-injection hole you'll exploit in Module 14. You'll also stand up a Kali VM on your M2.

### 📘 Theory

#### Define the words first

- **Filesystem Hierarchy Standard (FHS)** — the conventional Linux directory layout, so tools know where things live.
- **root** — UID 0, unrestricted power. You almost never log in as root directly.
- **`sudo`** — run a single command as another user (usually root) after authenticating; every use is logged to `/var/log/auth.log`. The audited, least-privilege way to escalate.
- **SUID/SGID bit** — a permission that makes an executable run as its *owner* (often root) regardless of who launches it. Powerful and a classic privilege-escalation vector if set on the wrong binary.
- **Process** — a running program (`ps`, `top`). **Service/daemon** — a long-running background process managed by `systemd` (`systemctl`, `journalctl`).
- **Shell metacharacters** — characters the shell treats specially (`;`, `|`, `&&`, `` ` ``, `$()`, `>`). The root cause of command injection when user input reaches a shell.

#### The FHS bits that matter

`/` root · `/home/user` user homes · `/etc` config · `/var/log` logs · `/usr/bin` binaries · `/tmp` temp (world-writable — never trust its contents) · `/proc` live kernel/process info · `/root` root's home.

#### Users, sudo, and the principle that returns everywhere

`root` is all-powerful; daily work happens as an unprivileged user; `sudo` grants momentary, *audited* escalation for one command. This is **least privilege** (Module 6) plus **auditability** (you can answer "who ran what as root, when?" from the logs). A misconfigured `sudoers` (e.g., `NOPASSWD: ALL`) or a stray SUID-root binary quietly hands an attacker the keys.

#### Why the naive way to run a subprocess fails

> **Naive: "I'll just build a shell command string with the user's input and run it."**
> Fails because the shell interprets metacharacters. If your code runs `ping <user-input>` by handing a *string* to the shell, an attacker submits `8.8.8.8; rm -rf ~` and the `;` starts a second command. This is **command injection** (OWASP A03, Module 14). The Linux power user's fix is to *never invoke a shell* with untrusted input — pass the program and its arguments as a *list/array*, so the OS executes exactly one program with the input as inert data, not as shell syntax.

> **The correct rule:** prefer argument-array execution (no shell), validate/allow-list inputs, run services as unprivileged users, and audit privileged actions.

### 🛠️ Lab: Kali on Apple Silicon + Tamper-Hunting (M2 / ARM64)

> ⚖️ **Ethics box.** The VM and all commands here are **your own systems**. A personal Kali VM is a sandbox you own — the right place to practice offensive tooling. Never point those tools at machines you don't own or have written authorization to test.

> **M2 Pro / Apple Silicon — ARM64.** Kali ships native ARM64 images. **Do not** download the x86 `...-installer-amd64.iso` — it won't boot natively and crawls under emulation. Get **`kali-linux-YYYY.x-installer-arm64.iso`** from the official site; the "Apple Silicon (M-series) Mac" bundle is a one-click UTM option.

1. Install **VMware Fusion Pro 13+** (most polished on Apple Silicon) or **UTM** (free/open-source fallback).
2. Download the Kali **ARM64** image from `https://www.kali.org/get-kali/`.
3. Allocate 4 GB RAM, 2 CPUs, 40 GB disk minimum. Default creds `kali`/`kali` — change immediately.
4. First boot:
   ```bash
   sudo apt update && sudo apt full-upgrade -y
   passwd                                  # change your password
   whoami; hostname; uname -a              # who/where am I
   # Defender skills on your own box:
   sudo find /etc -type f -mtime -1        # config files changed in last 24h (tamper hunt)
   sudo find / -perm -4000 -type f 2>/dev/null   # all SUID binaries (audit for surprises)
   sudo tail -n 20 /var/log/auth.log       # who used sudo recently
   systemctl --type=service --state=running # what's running, and should it be?
   ```

### 💻 Vulnerable vs. Secure Code — running a subprocess without inviting command injection

```javascript
// ❌ VULNERABLE — Node: hands a built string to a shell
const { exec } = require("child_process");
function ping(host) {
  // WHY WRONG: exec() runs a SHELL. host="8.8.8.8; rm -rf ~" -> the ';' chains a
  // second command. Untrusted input becomes shell syntax = command injection.
  exec(`ping -c 1 ${host}`, (e, out) => console.log(out));
}
```

```javascript
// ✅ SECURE — Node: execFile runs ONE program, args as an inert array (no shell)
const { execFile } = require("child_process");
function ping(host) {
  if (!/^[a-zA-Z0-9.\-]+$/.test(host)) throw new Error("invalid host");  // allow-list
  // WHY IT WORKS: no shell is spawned; 'host' is passed as a literal argument, so
  // metacharacters like ';' are just text to ping, never interpreted as commands.
  execFile("ping", ["-c", "1", host], (e, out) => console.log(out));
}
```

```python
# ❌ VULNERABLE — Python: shell=True with interpolated input
import subprocess
def ping(host):
    # WHY WRONG: shell=True invokes /bin/sh; "8.8.8.8; rm -rf ~" chains commands.
    subprocess.run(f"ping -c 1 {host}", shell=True)

# ✅ SECURE — Python: argument list, no shell
import subprocess, re
def ping(host):
    if not re.fullmatch(r"[A-Za-z0-9.\-]+", host):   # allow-list valid hostnames
        raise ValueError("invalid host")
    # WHY IT WORKS: shell=False (default) runs ping directly with host as a literal
    # argument; the OS never parses it as shell syntax. Same defense as Node's execFile.
    subprocess.run(["ping", "-c", "1", host])
```

### 🌍 Case Study — SUID & sudo Misconfigurations in Privilege Escalation
In countless CTFs and real breaches, an attacker who lands as a low-privilege user escalates to root not via a kernel exploit but via *configuration*: a stray SUID-root binary (e.g., a misused `find`, `vim`, or custom script), or a `sudoers` rule like `user ALL=(ALL) NOPASSWD: /usr/bin/some-script` where the script can be coerced into running arbitrary commands. The `find / -perm -4000` and `sudo -l` checks in the lab are exactly what attackers run first and what defenders use to audit. The lesson ties the whole module together: on Linux, *privilege boundaries are configuration*, and sloppy config is a vulnerability.

### 🧩 Try It Yourself

**Challenge 1:** Find every file under `/etc` modified in the last 24 hours, and explain why a defender cares.

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

```bash
sudo find /etc -type f -mtime -1
```
`-mtime -1` = modified less than 1 day ago. `/etc` holds system configuration (users, services, auth, cron). Unexpected recent changes there are a strong tamper/persistence signal — an attacker often edits config to add a user, a cron job, or an SSH key. Knowing the normal baseline lets you spot the anomaly.

</details>

**Challenge 2:** Your colleague says using `subprocess.run(cmd, shell=True)` is fine "because we control the command string." Why is that still risky?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

Because "we control the string" is rarely fully true — parts of it often come, directly or indirectly, from user input, a filename, a database value, or an environment variable. With `shell=True`, any metacharacter that slips into the string is interpreted by the shell, enabling injection. The robust default is to pass an argument *list* with `shell=False` so input is data, not code — even when you *think* the string is trusted. Don't rely on the gap between "trusted now" and "trusted after the next refactor."

</details>

### 🛡️ Pitfalls & False-Confidence Traps
- **Living as root in the VM "to avoid sudo prompts."** Reintroduces root-everywhere (Module 6). Use sudo for the moments you need it.
- **`shell=True` / `exec(string)` with any non-constant input.** The single most common command-injection source. Use argument arrays.
- **Trusting `/tmp`.** It's world-writable; never execute or trust files placed there by others, and create your temp files with safe modes (Module 7).
- **Ignoring SUID/sudoers in your own audits.** `find / -perm -4000` and `sudo -l` reveal escalation paths before an attacker finds them.
- **Reading logs only after an incident.** `/var/log/auth.log` and `journalctl` are most useful when you know the baseline beforehand.

### ❓ Quiz
1. What does `sudo` give you over logging in as root, from a security standpoint?
2. What is the SUID bit, and why is it a privilege-escalation concern?
3. Why is `subprocess.run(cmd, shell=True)` (or Node's `exec`) dangerous with user input, and what's the fix?
4. Why do defenders run `find /etc -mtime -1` and `find / -perm -4000`?
5. Why should you never trust or execute files in `/tmp`?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Answers</strong></summary>

1. Least privilege plus auditability: you operate unprivileged by default and escalate only for one command, and every escalation is authenticated and logged (`/var/log/auth.log`), so privileged actions are attributable.
2. SUID makes a binary run with its *owner's* privileges (often root) no matter who runs it. A vulnerable or misused SUID-root binary lets a low-privilege user execute actions as root — a classic escalation path.
3. `shell=True`/`exec` send the string through a shell, which interprets metacharacters (`;`, `|`, `$()`), so injected input runs as commands. Fix: pass the program and arguments as a list with no shell (`execFile` / `subprocess.run([...])`), plus input validation.
4. To detect tampering and escalation paths: recent changes under `/etc` often indicate attacker persistence (new users, cron, keys); SUID binaries are common privilege-escalation vectors that should be inventoried and justified.
5. `/tmp` is world-writable, so any user/process can plant files there. Trusting or executing them can let an attacker substitute malicious content; treat its contents as untrusted and create your own temp files securely.

</details>

### ✅ Progress Tracker
- [ ] Kali ARM64 VM running on VMware Fusion or UTM
- [ ] I can navigate the FHS and explain `/etc`, `/var/log`, `/proc`, `/tmp`
- [ ] I understand `sudo` vs. root and where escalation is logged
- [ ] I audited my VM for SUID binaries and recent `/etc` changes
- [ ] I can run a subprocess safely (Node `execFile` / Python list-args, no shell)

## Module 12 — Network Scanning & Enumeration (Nmap)

### 🎯 Mission Briefing
Before you attack or defend, you *map*. Nmap has been the field's most important reconnaissance tool for 25+ years. This module teaches you how port scanning actually works at the TCP level, how to read scan results, and — because you're a developer — how to write a tiny scanner yourself so you truly understand what a scan *is*, plus how this same recon is what an attacker runs against *your* deployed services first.

> ⚖️ **Ethics box.** Scan **only** machines you own or have explicit written permission to test. Unauthorized scanning is detectable and often illegal. This module uses two safe targets: **`scanme.nmap.org`** (the Nmap project explicitly authorizes scanning it) and **your own localhost / your own lab VM**. Never scan anything else.

### 📘 Theory

#### Define the words first

- **Port scan** — sending probe packets to a range of ports and inferring, from the replies, which services are listening.
- **TCP three-way handshake** — how a TCP connection opens: client sends **SYN**, server replies **SYN-ACK**, client sends **ACK**. A scanner uses the *response* to a SYN to learn a port's state.
- **Port states (Nmap):** **open** (a service is listening), **closed** (host is up but nothing listening — the host sent a RST), **filtered** (a firewall silently dropped the probe, so no reply came).
- **SYN ("stealth") scan (`-sS`)** — sends SYN, reads the reply, then sends RST to *not* complete the handshake. Faster and historically less logged. Needs root.
- **Connect scan (`-sT`)** — completes the full handshake via the OS. No root needed, but more visible/logged.
- **Service/version detection (`-sV`)** — probes open ports to identify the software *and version*, which reveals known-vulnerable versions.

#### Why scanning matters to both sides

> **Naive: "My service is online but obscure, so no one will find it."**
> Fails because the entire public internet is scanned continuously by automated tools (Shodan, Censys, mass scanners) within *minutes* of a host coming online. "Security through obscurity" of an exposed port is not security. The defensive takeaway: assume every exposed port is discovered immediately, so each one must be intended, patched (`-sV` reveals outdated versions to attackers too), authenticated, and firewalled. Recon is step one of the kill chain (Module 18) — knowing what an attacker sees lets you shrink it first.

#### How a scan reads a handshake

When the scanner sends a SYN to an **open** port, the OS replies SYN-ACK (a service is there). To a **closed** port, the OS replies RST (host up, nothing listening). To a **filtered** port, a firewall drops the SYN, so *nothing* comes back and the scanner times out. Three different behaviors → three states. That's the whole core idea; Nmap just does it fast across 65,535 ports and adds version/OS fingerprinting on top.

### 🛠️ Lab: Nmap + Write Your Own Scanner (M2 / Apple Silicon)

> ⚖️ Targets: `scanme.nmap.org` (authorized) and `127.0.0.1` / your lab VM (owned). Nothing else.

```bash
mkdir -p ~/cyber-journal/m12 && cd ~/cyber-journal/m12
brew install nmap                       # ARM64-native via Homebrew

nmap scanme.nmap.org                    # top-1000 TCP ports
nmap -sV scanme.nmap.org                # + service/version detection
sudo nmap -sS -p- 127.0.0.1             # SYN scan, ALL ports, on your OWN host
nmap -sV -p 5432,3306,8080 127.0.0.1    # version-check your own services
# Your lab VM (optional — you build this VM in Module 15; use scanme.nmap.org / 127.0.0.1 until then):
#   nmap -sV 192.168.64.10
```

> **Heads-up on "your lab VM."** This module (and Modules 13–14) mention *"your lab VM,"* but you don't build it until **Module 15**. Nothing here is blocked by that: every step above uses an authorized public target (`scanme.nmap.org`) or your own `127.0.0.1`. Treat every "lab VM" line as *optional until Module 15* — or, if you'd rather have the VM now, you can do Module 15 early (it also gives Windows learners a single environment where every later lab runs unchanged).

**Flags every operator memorizes:** `-sS` SYN stealth · `-sT` connect · `-sU` UDP · `-sV` version · `-O` OS fingerprint · `-A` aggressive (all) · `-p-` all 65535 ports · `-T4` faster timing · `--script vuln` vuln scripts.

### 💻 Vulnerable vs. Secure Code — a tiny scanner, and what it teaches about exposure

This shows *how* a scan works (and runs only against your own host). The "vulnerable vs secure" lesson is the **bind/firewall posture** the scan reveals.

```javascript
// LEARNING TOOL — Node: minimal connect-scan of YOUR OWN host (owned data only)
const net = require("net");
function scan(host, ports) {
  ports.forEach(port => {
    const s = net.connect({ host, port });
    s.setTimeout(400);
    s.on("connect", () => { console.log(`open   ${port}`); s.destroy(); });   // SYN-ACK got through
    s.on("timeout", () => { console.log(`filtered ${port}`); s.destroy(); }); // no reply (firewalled)
    s.on("error",   () => {});                                                 // RST = closed
  });
}
scan("127.0.0.1", [22, 80, 443, 5432, 8080]);
// SECURITY POINT: any port that prints "open" AND is bound to 0.0.0.0 is reachable
// from your network (Module 9). The fix is not to hide it — it's to bind to 127.0.0.1,
// authenticate it, and firewall it. Hiding loses to automated scanners every time.
```

```python
# LEARNING TOOL — Python: same minimal connect-scan of YOUR OWN host
import socket
def scan(host, ports):
    for port in ports:
        with socket.socket() as s:
            s.settimeout(0.4)
            try:
                s.connect((host, port)); print(f"open   {port}")   # handshake completed
            except socket.timeout:   print(f"filtered {port}")     # dropped by a firewall
            except ConnectionRefusedError: pass                    # RST = closed
scan("127.0.0.1", [22, 80, 443, 5432, 8080])
# SECURITY POINT (same as Node): "open" + bound to 0.0.0.0 = exposed. Obscurity is not a
# control; bind to localhost, require auth, and put a firewall in front.
```

### 🌍 Case Study — Shodan and the Always-On Scan
Shodan is a search engine that continuously port-scans the entire IPv4 internet and indexes what it finds — open databases, webcams, industrial controllers, dev servers with debug modes on. Researchers routinely find tens of thousands of exposed, unauthenticated systems through it. The point for this module: scanning is not a rare, targeted event you might dodge — it is *constant and global*. The instant you expose a port, it is known. That reframes defense from "stay hidden" to "every exposed service must independently survive being found," which is the bind-localhost + authenticate + patch + firewall posture from Module 9.

### 🧩 Try It Yourself

**Challenge 1:** Run `nmap -sV scanme.nmap.org`. Identify the SSH version, note its era, and reason about whether it might be missing patches.

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

`-sV` prints the service banner and best-guess version (e.g., `OpenSSH 6.x`). Compare it to current OpenSSH releases: an old major version likely lacks years of security fixes and is a candidate for known CVEs. This is exactly the reasoning an attacker does with your services — and why version detection cuts both ways: keep your software current so `-sV` reveals nothing exploitable.

</details>

**Challenge 2:** A port shows as **filtered** rather than **closed**. What's the difference, and what does "filtered" tell you?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

**Closed** = the host is up and actively replied with a RST (nothing is listening on that port). **Filtered** = no reply came at all because a firewall silently dropped the probe. "Filtered" tells you a filtering device sits in the path — useful recon, since it reveals the presence (and sometimes the policy) of a firewall even though you can't reach the port.

</details>

### 🛡️ Pitfalls & False-Confidence Traps
- **Scanning without authorization.** Even a "quick" scan of someone else's host can be illegal and is logged. Stick to `scanme.nmap.org` and your own assets.
- **Believing obscurity protects an open port.** Continuous global scanning finds it within minutes. Each exposed service must stand on its own defenses.
- **Reading "open" without reading the bind address.** An open port on `127.0.0.1` is local-only; on `0.0.0.0` it's network-exposed (Module 9). The scan plus `lsof` together tell the full story.
- **Ignoring `-sV` output on your own gear.** Outdated versions are the easiest target; version-scan your own services and patch what's stale (Module 14 case study: Equifax).
- **Trusting a single scan.** UDP services, rate-limiting, and `-T` timing all affect results; combine TCP and UDP scans and confirm with `lsof`/`ss`.

### ❓ Quiz
1. Walk through the TCP three-way handshake and explain how a scanner uses it to detect an open port.
2. What's the difference between a SYN (`-sS`) scan and a connect (`-sT`) scan, and why might you choose each?
3. What do the three Nmap states open, closed, and filtered each tell you?
4. Why is "my service is obscure, so it won't be found" a false sense of security?
5. Why does `-sV` (version detection) matter to both attackers and defenders?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Answers</strong></summary>

1. Client→SYN, server→SYN-ACK, client→ACK opens a TCP connection. A scanner sends a SYN; a **SYN-ACK** reply means a service is listening (open). It can then RST instead of completing (SYN scan) to be faster/quieter.
2. `-sS` sends SYN and resets before completing the handshake — faster and historically less logged, but needs root. `-sT` completes the full handshake via the OS — no root required but more visible in logs. Choose `-sS` for speed/stealth with privileges, `-sT` when unprivileged.
3. **open** = a service is listening and accepting; **closed** = host is up but nothing is listening (got a RST); **filtered** = a firewall dropped the probe so no reply returned.
4. Because the whole internet is scanned continuously and automatically (e.g., Shodan), so any exposed port is discovered within minutes. Obscurity buys no protection; each service must survive being found.
5. It reveals the exact software and version of listening services. Attackers use it to find known-vulnerable versions to exploit; defenders use it to inventory their own stack and patch outdated, exposed software before attackers do.

</details>

### ✅ Progress Tracker
- [ ] Scanned `scanme.nmap.org` and my own `127.0.0.1` successfully
- [ ] I can explain the three-way handshake and SYN vs. connect scans
- [ ] I can explain open vs. closed vs. filtered
- [ ] I wrote a tiny scanner (Node or Python) against my own host and understood the output
- [ ] I version-scanned my own services and noted anything outdated

### ➡️ Next Step
Module 13 — **Packet Analysis with Wireshark**. Scanning tells you *what's listening*; Wireshark shows you the *actual bytes* on the wire — every header and payload — so you can see plaintext leaks, handshake details, and attack traffic with your own eyes.

## Module 13 — Packet Analysis with Wireshark

### 🎯 Mission Briefing
Wireshark is the microscope of cybersecurity: it shows you raw network traffic — every packet, header, and byte. In this module you'll *see with your own eyes* that HTTP sends passwords in plaintext while HTTPS doesn't, watch the TCP handshake you learned in Module 12, and then write the server-side code that forces every connection onto TLS so the leak you witnessed can never happen in your app.

### 📘 Theory

#### Define the words first

- **Packet** — a unit of network data. Wireshark dissects each one layer by layer: **Ethernet frame → IP header → TCP/UDP header → application data** (the OSI layers from Module 9, made concrete).
- **Capture** — recording packets seen by a network interface. On *your own* machine/network this is a diagnostic and learning tool.
- **Display filter** — Wireshark's query language to show only matching packets (e.g., `http`, `tls`, `tcp.port == 443`).
- **Follow Stream** — reassembles all packets of one conversation into the readable request/response, so you see the *whole* exchange, not isolated packets.
- **Plaintext vs. encrypted** — HTTP, FTP, Telnet, and DNS (classically) travel as readable text; HTTPS/TLS encrypts the application data so a capture shows only the handshake metadata, not the contents.
- **HSTS (HTTP Strict Transport Security)** — a response header that tells browsers "only ever talk to me over HTTPS," closing the gap where the first request goes out as plain HTTP.

#### Useful display filters

`ip.addr == 192.168.1.1` · `tcp.port == 443` · `http` (all HTTP) · `dns` · `tls` · `tcp.flags.syn == 1 and tcp.flags.ack == 0` (SYN-only — scanning behavior, ties to Module 12).

#### Why the naive assumption fails

> **Naive: "It's my login form, the data is safe in transit."**
> Fails if the form is served or submitted over **HTTP**. On HTTP, the username and password travel as readable text across every device between you and the server — your router, the coffee-shop access point, the ISP, any attacker doing a man-in-the-middle on the LAN. Wireshark makes this visceral: you will literally read the credentials in Follow Stream. Even an HTTPS site is vulnerable if it *also* answers on HTTP and merely "redirects," because that first plaintext request (and any cookie sent with it) is already exposed. The fix is not "mostly HTTPS" — it's **HTTPS only, enforced, with HSTS** so the browser never sends plaintext in the first place.

> **The correct rule:** encrypt everything in transit (TLS), redirect HTTP→HTTPS at the edge, set HSTS, and mark cookies `Secure` (Module 5) so they're never attached to a plaintext request.

### 🛠️ Lab: See Plaintext vs. TLS With Your Own Eyes (M2 / Apple Silicon)

> ⚖️ **Ethics box.** Capture **only on your own machine/network**, viewing **your own traffic**. Capturing other people's traffic — on shared/work/public Wi-Fi — can be illegal wiretapping. The site `neverssl.com` exists specifically so you can safely observe plaintext HTTP that you yourself requested.

```bash
# Wireshark installs native on Apple Silicon via Homebrew:
brew install --cask wireshark
mkdir -p ~/cyber-journal/m13
```

1. Open Wireshark → select your active interface (usually Wi-Fi) → start capture.
2. In a browser, visit `http://neverssl.com` (intentionally unencrypted). Stop capture.
3. Apply filter `http`, find the `GET` request, right-click → **Follow → HTTP Stream**. **You can read everything in plaintext.**
4. Now visit `https://www.google.com`, filter `tls`. You see the handshake (SNI, cert exchange) but the application data is **encrypted** — Follow Stream shows gibberish. That's TLS working.
5. Filter `tcp.flags.syn == 1 and tcp.flags.ack == 0` to watch the SYN packets that begin each connection (the handshake from Module 12).

CLI alternative (terminal capture of your own loopback test):
```bash
sudo tcpdump -i lo0 -A 'tcp port 8080'   # capture+print ASCII for a localhost app you run
```

### 💻 Vulnerable vs. Secure Code — forcing TLS so nothing leaks in plaintext

```javascript
// ❌ VULNERABLE — Node/Express: serves over plain HTTP (everything is readable on the wire)
require("http").createServer(app).listen(80);
// WHY WRONG: credentials, cookies, and data travel as plaintext — exactly what you
// just read in Wireshark. Any on-path device can capture them.
```

```javascript
// ✅ SECURE — Node/Express: redirect HTTP→HTTPS and enforce HSTS
const helmet = require("helmet");
app.use(helmet.hsts({ maxAge: 31536000, includeSubDomains: true, preload: true }));
app.use((req, res, next) => {
  // WHY IT WORKS: any plain-HTTP request is bounced to HTTPS before data is sent,
  // and HSTS tells the browser to NEVER use HTTP again for this site — so future
  // requests skip the vulnerable first plaintext hop entirely.
  if (req.secure) return next();
  res.redirect(308, "https://" + req.headers.host + req.url);
});
// Terminate TLS here or at a reverse proxy (nginx) with a valid certificate.
```

```python
# ❌ VULNERABLE — Python/Flask: plain HTTP
app.run(host="127.0.0.1", port=80)   # WHY WRONG: same plaintext-on-the-wire exposure

# ✅ SECURE — Python/Flask: enforce HTTPS + HSTS (e.g., via Flask-Talisman)
from flask_talisman import Talisman
Talisman(app, force_https=True, strict_transport_security=True,
         strict_transport_security_max_age=31536000)
# WHY IT WORKS: force_https redirects HTTP->HTTPS; HSTS makes the browser refuse
# plaintext to this site thereafter. Same defense as the Node version — an HTTP standard.
```

### 🌍 Case Study — Firesheep (2010)
A browser extension called **Firesheep** let anyone on the same open Wi-Fi capture other users' *session cookies* for sites (Facebook, Twitter, others) that used HTTPS only for the login page and **plain HTTP afterward**. Because the post-login cookies rode over HTTP, a bystander running Firesheep could copy them and hijack accounts with one click — no password needed. The industry response was exactly this module's lesson: **HTTPS everywhere, all the time**, plus `Secure` cookies and HSTS so session tokens are never sent in the clear. Firesheep is why "HTTPS only on the login form" is a known, named failure.

### 🧩 Try It Yourself

**Challenge 1:** In Wireshark you visit an HTTPS site but can still see the domain name you went to. How, if the content is encrypted?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

Two leaks reveal the destination even under TLS: the **DNS query** (classically plaintext, before DoH/DoT) shows the hostname you looked up, and the TLS **SNI (Server Name Indication)** field in the ClientHello sends the target hostname *in the clear* so the server knows which certificate to present. So TLS protects the *content* but historically not *which site* you contacted. (Encrypted DNS and ECH/Encrypted Client Hello are the evolving fixes.)

</details>

**Challenge 2:** A site serves its login page over HTTPS but the rest of the site over HTTP. Why is that still dangerous, and what's the fix?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

After login, the browser keeps sending the **session cookie** with every request. If those requests go over HTTP, the cookie travels in plaintext and an on-path attacker (Firesheep-style) can capture and reuse it to hijack the session — without ever needing the password. The fix: serve the *entire* site over HTTPS, mark the session cookie `Secure` (Module 5) so it's never attached to a plaintext request, redirect HTTP→HTTPS, and set HSTS.

</details>

### 🛡️ Pitfalls & False-Confidence Traps
- **Capturing traffic you don't own.** On shared/public Wi-Fi this can be illegal. Capture only your own machine/network.
- **"HTTPS on login is enough."** Firesheep proved otherwise. Encrypt the whole site and mark cookies `Secure`.
- **Redirect-only HTTPS without HSTS.** The first plaintext request still leaks. HSTS removes that hop for return visitors.
- **Assuming TLS hides everything.** SNI and (classic) DNS still reveal the hostname. TLS protects content, not necessarily metadata.
- **Trusting a green padlock as "this site is safe."** The padlock means *encrypted transport to whoever holds the cert* — a phishing site can have a valid cert too. Encryption ≠ trustworthiness (Module 4).

### ❓ Quiz
1. What does the TCP three-way handshake look like in Wireshark, and which filter isolates the SYN packets?
2. Why can you often see *which* HTTPS site someone visited even though the content is encrypted?
3. When would capturing on a Wi-Fi network be illegal or unethical?
4. Why is "HTTPS only on the login page" a named, historical failure, and what fixes it?
5. As a developer, what do an HTTP→HTTPS redirect, HSTS, and a `Secure` cookie each contribute?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Answers</strong></summary>

1. Three packets: SYN, SYN-ACK, ACK. Filter `tcp.flags.syn == 1 and tcp.flags.ack == 0` shows the SYN-only packets that begin connections (and reveal scanning).
2. The DNS lookup (classically plaintext) and the TLS SNI field both carry the hostname in the clear, so the destination is visible even when the payload is encrypted (until encrypted DNS and ECH).
3. When it's not your own traffic/network — e.g., on public, work, or shared Wi-Fi — capturing others' packets can be unlawful interception/wiretapping. Capture only what you own.
4. Because post-login session cookies sent over HTTP travel in plaintext and can be stolen and replayed (Firesheep). Fix: HTTPS everywhere, `Secure` cookies, HTTP→HTTPS redirect, and HSTS.
5. The redirect moves any plaintext request to HTTPS; HSTS makes the browser refuse HTTP to the site thereafter (removing the first-hop leak); `Secure` ensures the cookie is never attached to a non-HTTPS request. Together they guarantee credentials/cookies never traverse plaintext.

</details>

### ✅ Progress Tracker
- [ ] Captured and followed an HTTP stream and *read* the plaintext
- [ ] Compared it to a TLS capture and saw encrypted application data
- [ ] Used 3+ display filters including the SYN-only filter
- [ ] I understand why "HTTPS on login only" fails (Firesheep)
- [ ] I can write a Node or Python app that forces HTTPS and sets HSTS

## Module 14 — Web Security & OWASP Top 10

### 🎯 Mission Briefing
Most real-world attacks target web applications, and as a full-stack developer this is *your* home turf — which means these bugs are yours to introduce and yours to prevent. OWASP (the Open Worldwide Application Security Project) publishes the **Top 10**, the field's reference list of the most critical web risks. You'll learn each one from first principles, exploit the big three (SQL injection, XSS, command injection) in a deliberately vulnerable app you run locally, and write the secure version of each in both Node.js and Python.

### 📘 Theory

#### Define the words first

- **Injection** — when untrusted input is interpreted as *code/commands* instead of *data*. The single root cause behind SQLi, XSS, command injection, and more. The universal fix is *separating code from data* so input can never change the structure of a query/page/command.
- **SQL injection (SQLi)** — user input alters a database query's structure (e.g., turning a `WHERE` clause into "always true").
- **Cross-site scripting (XSS)** — attacker-controlled input is rendered as live script in another user's browser. **Reflected** (echoed from the request), **Stored** (saved and served to others), **DOM-based** (introduced client-side by JS).
- **Command injection** — user input reaches a system shell and runs OS commands (Module 11's lesson, as a web bug).
- **IDOR (Insecure Direct Object Reference)** — a form of broken access control where changing an identifier (`/account/123`→`124`) exposes another user's object because the server didn't check ownership.
- **SSRF (Server-Side Request Forgery)** — the server is tricked into making a request to an attacker-chosen URL, often an internal one.
- **Parameterized query / prepared statement** — sending the SQL *structure* and the *data* separately so the database never parses user input as SQL. The correct SQLi defense.

#### OWASP Top 10 (2025 edition — the current release): description → example → defense

> **If you learned the 2021 list, re-anchor now.** Supply-chain attacks got their own category (**A03**), replacing the old "Vulnerable and Outdated Components" entry. A brand-new **A10** covers code that fails *open* when something goes wrong. **SSRF is no longer its own number** — it moved under A01 as an access-control failure. Four categories kept their meaning but changed number: Misconfiguration 05→02, Cryptographic Failures 02→04, Injection 03→05, Insecure Design 04→06.

| ID | Risk | Example | Core defense |
|---|---|---|---|
| A01 | Broken Access Control (now includes SSRF) | IDOR: `/account/124` shows another user; app fetches `http://169.254.169.254/` → cloud creds | Server-side authZ on every request; deny by default; allow-list outbound requests |
| A02 | Security Misconfiguration | Default passwords, verbose errors, debug on | Hardening, minimal config, automated checks |
| A03 | Software Supply Chain Failures | Unpatched Struts (Equifax, below); malicious npm package pulled in at build time | SCA tools, patch fast, SBOM, pin and verify dependencies |
| A04 | Cryptographic Failures | Passwords in MD5; login over HTTP | TLS everywhere (Module 13); Argon2/bcrypt (Module 3); AES-GCM (Module 10) |
| A05 | Injection | `admin' OR '1'='1` in a login field | Parameterized queries; argument-array exec; output encoding |
| A06 | Insecure Design | Reset token that never expires | Threat modeling in design; secure-by-design patterns |
| A07 | Authentication Failures | No lockout, weak/guessable sessions | MFA, rate limiting, strong session tokens (Module 3) |
| A08 | Software or Data Integrity Failures | Unsigned updates; CI trusts unverified artifacts | Signed artifacts, verified provenance |
| A09 | Security Logging & Alerting Failures | Attacker dwells 200 days unseen; alert fires but pages no one | Centralized logging, alerting, SIEM |
| A10 | Mishandling of Exceptional Conditions | A `catch` block that grants access when the permission check throws | Fail **closed** on every security-relevant exception |

#### Why the naive defenses fail (the through-line)

> **Naive #1: "I'll escape/blacklist the dangerous characters."**
> Fails because blacklists are always incomplete — encodings, alternate syntaxes, and edge cases slip through (`'`, `'`, `%27`, double-encoding…). The robust fix is not to *clean* code-laced input but to *never let input be code*: parameterize queries, encode on output, and pass arguments as arrays.

> **Naive #2: "I validate the input in the browser/JavaScript."**
> Fails because the client is fully attacker-controlled — they bypass your form entirely with `curl` or DevTools. Client-side validation is a UX nicety; **every** security check must happen server-side.

> **Naive #3: "The page doesn't show an admin link to normal users, so they can't reach admin functions."**
> Fails because hiding a link is not access control — the attacker just requests the URL directly. Authorization must be enforced on the *server* for *every* request (deny by default), not implied by the UI.

> **The universal principle:** *separate code from data, validate server-side, deny by default, and keep components patched.* Almost the entire Top 10 reduces to these four ideas.

### 🛠️ Lab: Exploit the Big Three Locally (M2 / Apple Silicon)

> ⚖️ **Ethics box.** You attack **only a deliberately vulnerable app running on your own machine** (DVWA or OWASP Juice Shop on localhost). These apps exist to be exploited *by you, on your hardware*. Never use these techniques against any site you don't own or have explicit written authorization to test — SQLi/XSS against a real site is a crime.

**ARM64 note:** **OWASP Juice Shop** has a native `linux/arm64` image and runs great on M2. **DVWA**'s common image is x86; run it with emulation via `--platform linux/amd64` (slower but fine for learning).

```bash
mkdir -p ~/cyber-journal/m14 && cd ~/cyber-journal/m14

# Option A (recommended on M2): Juice Shop, native arm64
docker run --rm -d -p 3000:3000 bkimminich/juice-shop
# Browse http://localhost:3000 and work the challenge board.

# Option B: DVWA via x86 emulation on Apple Silicon
docker run --rm -d -p 8080:80 --platform linux/amd64 vulnerables/web-dvwa
# Browse http://localhost:8080  (login admin/password, set security to "Low")
```

Then practice the big three (DVWA Low, or Juice Shop equivalents):
- **SQL Injection** — User ID field: `1' UNION SELECT user, password FROM users-- ` (note trailing space).
- **Reflected XSS** — search box: `<script>alert(document.domain)</script>`.
- **Command Injection** — the ping form: `8.8.8.8; whoami` (the `;` from Module 11).

Escalate DVWA to **Medium** then **High** for each — same bug, progressively stronger (and finally parameterized) defenses. Watching the *defense* change is how you learn both sides. Clean up: `docker ps` then `docker stop <id>` (frees disk on your 512GB SSD).

### 💻 Vulnerable vs. Secure Code — the three injections, in Node and Python

**SQL Injection**
```javascript
// ❌ VULNERABLE — Node: string-concatenated query
db.query(`SELECT * FROM users WHERE name = '${req.body.name}'`);
// WHY WRONG: name = "x' OR '1'='1" makes the WHERE always true -> dumps all rows.
// ✅ SECURE — Node: parameterized query (structure and data sent separately)
db.query("SELECT * FROM users WHERE name = $1", [req.body.name]);
// WHY IT WORKS: the driver sends the SQL and the value apart; input can NEVER become SQL.
```
```python
# ❌ VULNERABLE — Python: f-string into SQL
cur.execute(f"SELECT * FROM users WHERE name = '{name}'")   # same "OR '1'='1" break
# ✅ SECURE — Python: parameterized (placeholder + params tuple)
cur.execute("SELECT * FROM users WHERE name = %s", (name,))
# WHY IT WORKS: the DB driver binds 'name' as data, not as query structure.
```

**Cross-Site Scripting (XSS)**
```javascript
// ❌ VULNERABLE — Node/Express + template: raw interpolation into HTML
res.send(`<h1>Hello ${req.query.name}</h1>`);
// WHY WRONG: name = "<script>steal()</script>" runs in the victim's browser.
// ✅ SECURE — Node: contextual output encoding (let the template engine escape)
res.render("hello", { name: req.query.name });   // e.g., EJS <%= name %> auto-escapes
// Plus a Content-Security-Policy header to blunt any script that slips through.
```
```python
# ❌ VULNERABLE — Python/Flask: marking user input "safe"
return f"<h1>Hello {name}</h1>"        # or render_template_string with |safe -> XSS
# ✅ SECURE — Python/Flask: Jinja2 auto-escapes by default; never bypass it
return render_template("hello.html", name=name)   # {{ name }} is auto-escaped
# WHY IT WORKS: output is encoded for the HTML context, so input renders as text, not script.
```

**Command Injection** (recap of Module 11, as a web handler)
```javascript
// ❌ VULNERABLE — Node: shell string with user input
require("child_process").exec(`host ${req.query.domain}`);   // "x; rm -rf /" chains commands
// ✅ SECURE — Node: argument array, no shell, with validation
const { execFile } = require("child_process");
if (!/^[a-z0-9.\-]+$/i.test(req.query.domain)) throw new Error("bad domain");
execFile("host", [req.query.domain]);   // input is an inert argument
```
```python
# ❌ VULNERABLE — Python: shell=True
subprocess.run(f"host {domain}", shell=True)      # "x; rm -rf /" chains commands
# ✅ SECURE — Python: list args, no shell, with validation
import re, subprocess
if not re.fullmatch(r"[A-Za-z0-9.\-]+", domain): raise ValueError("bad domain")
subprocess.run(["host", domain])
```

**Broken Access Control / IDOR**
```javascript
// ❌ VULNERABLE — Node: returns any account by id with no ownership check
app.get("/account/:id", (req, res) => res.json(db.getAccount(req.params.id)));
// ✅ SECURE — Node: enforce that the requester OWNS the object (deny by default)
app.get("/account/:id", requireAuth, (req, res) => {
  const acct = db.getAccount(req.params.id);
  if (!acct || acct.ownerId !== req.user.id) return res.sendStatus(403);   // authZ check
  res.json(acct);
});
```
```python
# ❌ VULNERABLE — Python/Flask: no ownership check
@app.get("/account/<int:id>")
def account(id): return jsonify(db.get_account(id))
# ✅ SECURE — Python/Flask: verify ownership server-side, deny by default
@app.get("/account/<int:id>")
@login_required
def account(id):
    acct = db.get_account(id)
    if not acct or acct.owner_id != current_user.id:
        abort(403)
    return jsonify(acct)
```

### 🌍 Case Study — Equifax 2017
A known vulnerability in Apache Struts (**CVE-2017-5638**) had a public patch for **two months**. Equifax didn't apply it. Attackers exploited the unpatched component and exfiltrated **147 million** people's SSNs, birthdates, and addresses; the settlement reached **~$700M**. This maps squarely to **A06 — Vulnerable and Outdated Components**: the most damaging breaches are often not novel exploits but *unpatched known bugs*. Patching is a security control (Module 6), and component scanning (Module 7's supply-chain theme) is how you find these before attackers do.

### 🧩 Try It Yourself

**Challenge 1:** In DVWA low-security SQL Injection, retrieve all usernames and password hashes. Document the exact payload, then explain why the High-security version defeats it.

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

Payload in the User ID field:
```
1' UNION SELECT user, password FROM users-- 
```
The `UNION SELECT` appends the `users` table's columns to the result; the `-- ` comments out the rest of the original query. **Medium** difficulty adds escaping (`mysql_real_escape_string`) that you can sometimes still bypass; **High** uses a **parameterized query**, so the input is bound as data and can never alter the SQL structure — the injection simply becomes a literal (failed) username lookup. That's the whole lesson: escaping is a patch, parameterization is a cure.

</details>

**Challenge 2:** Why is client-side input validation worthless as a *security* control (though useful for UX)?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

Because the client is entirely under the attacker's control. They can disable JavaScript, edit the page in DevTools, or skip the browser entirely and send a crafted request with `curl`/Burp. Any check that runs only in the browser can be bypassed in seconds. Client validation improves UX (instant feedback) but the authoritative security checks — validation, authentication, authorization, output encoding, parameterization — must all run on the **server**, which the attacker cannot edit.

</details>

### 🛡️ Pitfalls & False-Confidence Traps
- **Escaping/blacklisting instead of parameterizing.** Blacklists are incomplete; parameterized queries remove the class of bug entirely.
- **`dangerouslySetInnerHTML` / `|safe` / `v-html` with user data.** These bypass the framework's auto-escaping and reintroduce XSS. Don't.
- **Trusting client-side validation.** Always re-validate and authorize on the server.
- **Hiding UI as "access control."** The attacker requests the URL directly. Enforce authZ on every server request, deny by default.
- **WAF as a substitute for fixing the bug.** A Web Application Firewall buys time and blocks noise but is bypassable; it's a layer, not the fix.
- **Skipping patches because "it works."** Equifax "worked" too. Unpatched known CVEs are the most common breach cause.

### ❓ Quiz
1. What single root cause unites SQLi, XSS, and command injection, and what is the universal fix?
2. What's the difference between Reflected, Stored, and DOM-based XSS?
3. Why is parameterizing a query strictly better than escaping user input?
4. Why is client-side validation never sufficient as a security control?
5. How does an IDOR happen, and what server-side check prevents it?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Answers</strong></summary>

1. All three are **injection**: untrusted input is interpreted as code (SQL, HTML/JS, or shell) rather than data. The universal fix is to **separate code from data** — parameterized queries, contextual output encoding, and argument-array execution — so input can never change the structure.
2. **Reflected** XSS echoes attacker input straight back in the response (e.g., a search term). **Stored** XSS is saved server-side and served to other users (e.g., a malicious comment). **DOM-based** XSS occurs entirely client-side when JavaScript writes untrusted data into the DOM unsafely.
3. Escaping is a blacklist-style patch that can be bypassed via encodings/edge cases and must be applied perfectly everywhere. Parameterization sends SQL structure and data on separate channels, so user input is *never parsed as SQL* — it removes the vulnerability class instead of filtering it.
4. Because the client is attacker-controlled: validation in the browser can be disabled or skipped with tools like `curl`/Burp. Only server-side checks are authoritative.
5. An IDOR happens when the server returns/acts on an object identified by a user-supplied id **without verifying the requester is authorized to access that object**. Prevent it by enforcing ownership/permission checks server-side on every request (deny by default).

</details>

### ✅ Progress Tracker
- [ ] I can name all 10 OWASP categories and give an example of each
- [ ] I exploited SQLi, XSS, and command injection in a local DVWA/Juice Shop
- [ ] I escalated DVWA Low→Medium→High and watched the defense improve
- [ ] I can write the secure version of each (parameterized query, output encoding, arg-array exec, ownership check) in Node and Python
- [ ] I can explain why escaping < parameterization and why client validation isn't security

## Module 15 — Building a Virtual Pentest Lab

### 🎯 Mission Briefing
A personal home lab is your dojo: you must never practice attacks on systems you don't own, so you build your own legal, isolated playground. This module shows you the classic attacker-plus-target topology, *why network isolation is non-negotiable*, how to make it work on Apple Silicon (where the traditional x86 target VMs don't), and how to stand up a disposable vulnerable target in seconds — so your practice is fully self-contained and offline.

> ⚖️ **Ethics box.** The entire point of a lab is to give you a place where attacking is *authorized* — because you own every machine in it and it's cut off from the internet. This is the dividing line between learning and crime (Rule #1 of the Cyber Guardian's Code). Everything in this module stays inside that isolated boundary.

> 🖥️ **This VM makes host OS irrelevant.** Once this lab is built, **every lab from here on can be run inside this VM regardless of your host OS** — Windows, macOS, and Linux learners all get the same Linux command line, so the "M2 / Apple Silicon" labels on later modules stop mattering. If you're on Windows and haven't been using WSL2, this is the moment your environment fully matches everyone else's. (You can also build this lab *earlier* — right after Module 11 — if you'd like that shared environment sooner; the Nmap/Wireshark modules reference it but don't require it.)

### 📘 Theory

#### Define the words first

- **Hypervisor** — software that runs virtual machines (VMware Fusion, UTM/QEMU, VirtualBox). On Apple Silicon you want an **ARM64-aware** hypervisor.
- **Host-only network** — a virtual network connecting your VMs *to each other and the host*, but **not to the internet**. The safe default for a vulnerable lab.
- **NAT network** — lets VMs reach the internet through the host. Convenient, but **wrong for a vulnerable target** because it (and any attacker who pivots through it) can reach out.
- **Snapshot** — a saved VM state you can instantly revert to. Essential: you'll deliberately break targets and roll back.
- **Vulnerable-by-design target** — an app/VM intentionally full of holes (DVWA, Juice Shop, Metasploitable) that exists *to be attacked in a lab*.

#### Why the naive setup is dangerous

> **Naive: "I'll just run the vulnerable VM on my normal network so it's easy to reach."**
> Fails because an intentionally vulnerable machine is, by definition, trivially compromised — and on a NAT/bridged network it can be reached from (and reach out to) your LAN and the internet. Mass scanners (Module 12's Shodan lesson) find exposed services in minutes; a vulnerable target on a routable network can be hijacked by a *real* attacker and used as a foothold into your home network, or enlisted into a botnet. **The vulnerability is the point — so the isolation must be absolute.** Use a host-only network, snapshot before you start, and never bridge a vulnerable target to the internet.

#### The classic lab topology

```
  ┌─────────────────┐                ┌─────────────────────┐
  │  Kali Linux VM  │  host-only net  │  Vulnerable target  │
  │   (attacker)    │ ──────────────  │  (DVWA / MS3 / ...) │
  └─────────────────┘   no internet   └─────────────────────┘
```

### 🛠️ Lab: Stand Up an Isolated Lab on Apple Silicon (M2 / ARM64)

1. In VMware Fusion or UTM, create a **host-only** (isolated) network. Attach both VMs to it.
2. Assign static IPs (e.g., Kali `192.168.56.10`, target `192.168.56.20`).
3. **Snapshot** both VMs while clean.
4. Verify connectivity *and* isolation:
   ```bash
   # From Kali: you SHOULD reach the target...
   ping -c 2 192.168.56.20
   nmap -sV 192.168.56.20            # enumerate the target's services (Module 12)
   # ...and you should NOT reach the internet from the vulnerable target:
   #   (on the target)  ping -c 2 8.8.8.8   -> must FAIL. If it succeeds, your net isn't isolated.
   ```

> **M2 Pro / ARM64 — Metasploitable 2 warning.** Metasploitable 2 is x86-only and unstable under emulation on Apple Silicon. **Don't burn a weekend on it.** ARM-friendly alternatives:
> - **Metasploitable 3** — community ARM builds exist; check `rapid7/metasploitable3` releases.
> - **OWASP Juice Shop** — native ARM64 Docker: `docker run --rm -p 3000:3000 bkimminich/juice-shop`.
> - **DVWA** — x86 image; emulate: `docker run --rm --platform linux/amd64 -p 8080:80 vulnerables/web-dvwa`.
> - **VulnHub** — search for "arm64"/"Raspberry Pi" images.
> - **TryHackMe / HackTheBox** — cloud-hosted targets over VPN; often the smoothest path on M2 and far more targets than one local VM.

### 💻 A Disposable Vulnerable Target You Can Run in Seconds

Sometimes you want a tiny target to practice a *specific* bug without a whole VM. Here's a deliberately vulnerable mini-app (Node and Python) — **run it bound to localhost in your lab only**, attack it, then kill it. It reuses the SQLi/path-traversal bugs from Module 14 so you can drill them anywhere.

```javascript
// ⚠️ DELIBERATELY VULNERABLE practice target — Node. Run ONLY on localhost in your lab.
const http = require("http"), { URL } = require("url");
http.createServer((req, res) => {
  const u = new URL(req.url, "http://localhost");
  const name = u.searchParams.get("name") || "";
  // VULN ON PURPOSE: reflected XSS (no encoding) — practice the Module 14 payload here.
  res.end("<h1>Hello " + name + "<\/h1>");
}).listen(8081, "127.0.0.1");   // 127.0.0.1 = your machine only; never 0.0.0.0 for a vuln app
// Attack it:  open http://localhost:8081/?name=<img src=x onerror=alert(1)>
// Then fix it as the Module 14 secure version and confirm the payload renders as text.
```

```python
# ⚠️ DELIBERATELY VULNERABLE practice target — Python. Run ONLY on localhost in your lab.
from http.server import BaseHTTPRequestHandler, HTTPServer
from urllib.parse import urlparse, parse_qs
class H(BaseHTTPRequestHandler):
    def do_GET(self):
        name = parse_qs(urlparse(self.path).query).get("name", [""])[0]
        self.send_response(200); self.end_headers()
        # VULN ON PURPOSE: reflected XSS (no encoding). Practice, then fix.
        self.wfile.write(("<h1>Hello " + name + "</h1>").encode())
HTTPServer(("127.0.0.1", 8081), H).serve_forever()   # localhost only — never expose a vuln app
# WHY 127.0.0.1 MATTERS: even a practice target must not be network-reachable (Module 9).
```

### 🌍 Case Study — When "Vulnerable Lab" Hits the Real Internet
Security trainers and students have repeatedly, by accident, exposed intentionally vulnerable VMs (and even production-like test boxes) to the public internet via a misconfigured bridged adapter or a cloud instance with an open security group. Automated scanners found and compromised them within *minutes*, in some cases turning the "lab" into a cryptominer or a botnet node. The lesson is the same as Module 9 and Module 12: a vulnerable service plus a routable network equals near-instant compromise. Isolation isn't a nicety for a pentest lab — it's the only thing standing between "safe practice" and "you just hosted malware."

### 🧩 Try It Yourself

**Challenge 1:** Why is a host-only network, not a NAT network, the correct choice for a vulnerable target VM?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

A **host-only** network connects the VMs to each other and the host but provides **no route to the internet**, so a vulnerable target can be attacked by your Kali VM yet cannot be reached by, or reach out to, the outside world. A **NAT** network gives the VM outbound internet access (and, if misconfigured, can expose it), which means a deliberately broken machine could be compromised by real attackers or used to phone home. The vulnerability is intentional, so the isolation must be absolute.

</details>

**Challenge 2:** You start the disposable target above and want to confirm it's *not* reachable from another device on your Wi-Fi. How do you verify, and what binding makes it safe?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

It's bound to `127.0.0.1` (loopback), so only your own machine can connect — another device on the Wi-Fi cannot reach `localhost:8081` at all. Verify from your phone/another laptop by trying `http://<your-mac-ip>:8081` (it should fail/refuse), and locally with `lsof -iTCP:8081 -sTCP:LISTEN` which should show `127.0.0.1:8081`, not `*:8081`/`0.0.0.0:8081` (Module 9). Binding to localhost is what keeps a vulnerable practice app off the network.

</details>

### 🛡️ Pitfalls & False-Confidence Traps
- **Bridged/NAT for a vulnerable target.** Routable + vulnerable = compromised. Host-only, always.
- **No snapshots.** You'll break targets on purpose; without a clean snapshot you'll rebuild from scratch repeatedly.
- **Binding a practice app to `0.0.0.0`.** Even a toy target must be `127.0.0.1`-only, or anyone on your network can hit it (Module 9).
- **Fighting x86-only VMs on M2.** Metasploitable 2 wastes hours under emulation; pick ARM-native targets or cloud ranges.
- **Forgetting the lab is *authorized* precisely because it's yours and isolated.** The instant a target is reachable by others, you've left the authorized zone.

### ✅ Progress Tracker
- [ ] Attacker (Kali) and a target running on a host-only, internet-isolated network
- [ ] Verified the target is reachable from Kali but cannot reach the internet
- [ ] Took clean snapshots of both VMs
- [ ] Ran an Nmap scan from Kali revealing the target's services
- [ ] Stood up the disposable localhost target (Node or Python) and attacked it, then fixed it

### ➡️ Next Step
Module 16 — **Blue Team Basics: Monitoring & Defense**. You've built the attacker's side; now you build the *defender's* — logging, monitoring, and detection — so the Level 2 capstone can pit your red-team skills against your own blue-team instrumentation.
