## Module 16 — Blue Team Basics: Monitoring & Defense

### 🎯 Mission Briefing
Offense gets the press; defense (Blue Team) gets the jobs — for every pentester there are roughly ten defenders. This module covers how defenders *see* attacks: the tooling (SIEM/IDS/EDR), the mental models (the Pyramid of Pain, dwell time), the difference between signature and anomaly detection — and crucially, the security logging *you* must write into your own apps, because an attack you don't log is an attack you can't detect.

### 📘 Theory

#### Define the words first

- **Blue Team** — the defenders: detect, respond, harden. (Red Team = attackers; Purple = both collaborating.)
- **SIEM (Security Information and Event Management)** — collects logs from everywhere (endpoints, firewalls, servers, apps), correlates them, and alerts on suspicious patterns. Splunk, Elastic Security, **Wazuh** (free), Microsoft Sentinel.
- **IDS / IPS** — Intrusion **Detection** / **Prevention** System. Both inspect network traffic for known-bad signatures and anomalies; an IDS *alerts*, an IPS also *blocks* inline. Snort, Suricata, Zeek.
- **EDR (Endpoint Detection and Response)** — watches individual machines for malicious behavior and enables response. CrowdStrike, SentinelOne, Defender for Endpoint; open-source Wazuh/OSSEC agents.
- **Signature-based detection** — matches known-bad patterns (a specific hash, payload, or rule). Precise but blind to novel attacks.
- **Anomaly-based detection** — flags deviations from a learned baseline. Catches the unknown but produces false positives.
- **Dwell time** — how long an attacker is in the environment before detection. The headline blue-team metric.

#### The Pyramid of Pain (David Bianco)

The higher up this pyramid you force attackers, the costlier it is for them to adapt:
```
        TTPs (tactics/techniques — hardest to change)
      Tools
    Network/Host artifacts
  Domain names
 IP addresses
Hash values (easiest to change)
```
Blocking a hash or IP barely inconveniences an attacker (they rotate it instantly); detecting their *behavior* (TTPs) forces them to rebuild their playbook.

#### Why the naive posture fails

> **Naive #1: "We have a SIEM/EDR, so we're covered."**
> Fails because tooling that no one tunes, watches, or *acts on* is theater. The Target breach (below) had an alert — and nobody responded. **Detection without response is decoration.** A blue team is people and process around the tools, not the tools alone.

> **Naive #2: "My app logs errors, so I have security logging."**
> Fails because generic error logs rarely capture the *security-relevant* events a defender needs: who logged in, from where, failed-login bursts, privilege changes, access-control denials. And many apps log the *wrong* things — dumping passwords, tokens, or full card numbers into logs, turning the log store into a breach target itself. Security logging is a deliberate design choice: log the right events, with structure, and **never** log secrets.

> **The correct posture:** instrument the security-relevant events, ship them to a central store, correlate and alert on patterns (e.g., many failed logins from one IP), tune to cut false positives, and pair every alert with a response plan. As a developer, your job is to *emit* clean, structured, secret-free security events that the SIEM can act on.

### 🛠️ Lab: Read Real Logs & Hunt Failed Logins (M2 / Apple Silicon)

> ⚖️ **Ethics box.** You analyze **your own machine's logs** and **your own lab VM's logs**. Reading your own auth logs is exactly what a defender does daily and is completely safe.

```bash
mkdir -p ~/cyber-journal/m16 && cd ~/cyber-journal/m16

# macOS: recent authentication/login events
log show --predicate 'process == "loginwindow"' --last 24h | head -40

# Linux/Kali: failed SSH attempts (classic brute-force signal)
sudo grep "Failed password" /var/log/auth.log | tail -20
# Top offending IPs (credential-stuffing/brute-force detection in one pipeline):
sudo grep "Failed password" /var/log/auth.log | awk '{print $(NF-3)}' | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head
```

**Stand up a free SIEM — Wazuh (ARM64-capable via Docker):** follow the Wazuh Docker quickstart at `https://documentation.wazuh.com`, deploy an agent on your Kali/Ubuntu VM, and watch events populate the dashboard. (Mind your 16GB RAM — run the single-node Wazuh and stop it when done to free memory.)

### 💻 Vulnerable vs. Secure Code — security logging that a SIEM can actually use

```javascript
// ❌ VULNERABLE — Node: no security logging, and a dangerous attempt at it
function login(req, res) {
  const user = authenticate(req.body.username, req.body.password);
  if (!user) return res.sendStatus(401);   // WHY WRONG: a failed login leaves NO trace,
                                            // so brute force is invisible to any SIEM.
  // (Worse pattern seen in the wild:)
  // console.log("login attempt", req.body);  // WHY WRONG: logs the PASSWORD into the log store.
  res.json(user);
}
```

```javascript
// ✅ SECURE — Node: structured, secret-free security events a SIEM can correlate
const logger = require("pino")();   // structured JSON logs
function login(req, res) {
  const username = req.body.username;
  const ok = authenticate(username, req.body.password);
  // WHY IT WORKS: one structured event per outcome, with the fields a defender needs
  // (event, username, source IP, time) and NO password/token. The SIEM can now alert on
  // "N failed logins from one IP in 1 min" = credential stuffing.
  logger.info({ event: ok ? "login_success" : "login_failure",
                username, ip: req.ip, ts: Date.now() });
  if (!ok) return res.sendStatus(401);
  res.json({ id: ok.id });   // also: don't return more than needed
}
```

```python
# ❌ VULNERABLE — Python: silent failures and/or secrets in logs
import logging
def login(req):
    user = authenticate(req["username"], req["password"])
    if not user:
        return 401                       # WHY WRONG: failed login leaves no auditable trace
    # logging.info("login %s", req)      # WHY WRONG: would log the password
    return user

# ✅ SECURE — Python: structured security event, no secrets
import logging, json, time
log = logging.getLogger("security")
def login(req, source_ip):
    username = req["username"]
    user = authenticate(username, req["password"])
    # WHY IT WORKS: emits a structured, secret-free event per outcome with the fields the
    # SIEM needs to detect brute force / credential stuffing across many requests.
    log.info(json.dumps({"event": "login_success" if user else "login_failure",
                         "username": username, "ip": source_ip, "ts": time.time()}))
    return user if user else 401
```

### 🌍 Case Study — Target 2013
Attackers compromised an **HVAC vendor**, used those credentials to reach Target's network, and deployed malware on point-of-sale systems, stealing **40 million** payment cards. Target's FireEye monitoring **did fire an alert** — and no one acted on it in time. This is **OWASP A09 — Logging & Monitoring Failures** at industry scale, and it cuts two ways for this module: (1) detection without *response* is theater (the alert existed and was ignored); (2) attackers pivot through the weakest connected party (third-party/supply-chain access), so your monitoring must cover lateral movement, not just the perimeter. The blue team is people and process, not just a dashboard.

### 🧩 Try It Yourself

**Challenge 1:** Signature-based vs. anomaly-based detection — give one strength and one weakness of each.

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

**Signature-based:** *Strength* — precise, low false positives for *known* threats (a specific malware hash or exploit pattern). *Weakness* — blind to anything new or modified (zero-days, polymorphic malware), since there's no signature yet. **Anomaly-based:** *Strength* — can catch novel attacks by flagging deviations from a learned baseline. *Weakness* — noisy (false positives) and dependent on a good baseline; attackers can also "boil the frog" by shifting the baseline slowly. Mature defenses use both.

</details>

**Challenge 2:** Why is logging the username and source IP of *failed* logins valuable, and why must you never log the password attempt?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

Failed-login events with username + source IP + timestamp let a SIEM correlate *patterns* — many failures from one IP (brute force) or one username failing across many IPs (password spraying / credential stuffing) — which is how you detect an attack in progress and trigger rate-limiting or blocking. You must never log the attempted password because (a) it's a secret that turns your log store into a high-value breach target, and (b) failed attempts often contain users' *real* passwords mistyped into the wrong field or with a transposed character — logging them leaks live credentials.

</details>

### 🛡️ Pitfalls & False-Confidence Traps
- **Buying tools, not running them.** An untuned, unwatched SIEM/EDR is theater. Detection needs response.
- **Logging secrets.** Passwords, tokens, full PANs, and session IDs in logs make the log store a breach target. Redact by design.
- **Only watching the perimeter.** Target was breached via a vendor and moved laterally. Monitor internal movement too.
- **Alert fatigue.** Too many low-value alerts train responders to ignore the one that matters (the Target lesson). Tune ruthlessly.
- **Chasing hashes/IPs alone.** They rotate instantly (bottom of the Pyramid of Pain). Detect behavior/TTPs to actually hurt attackers.

### ❓ Quiz
1. What are the strengths and weaknesses of signature-based vs. anomaly-based detection?
2. Why is dwell time the metric blue teams obsess over?
3. What's the difference between an IDS and an IPS?
4. As a developer, which security events should you log, and what must you never put in logs?
5. Target had an alert and still got breached — what does that teach about detection vs. response?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Answers</strong></summary>

1. Signature: precise and low-noise for known threats but blind to novel/modified ones. Anomaly: can catch unknown attacks via baseline deviation but is noisier and baseline-dependent. Use both.
2. Because the longer an attacker dwells undetected, the more data they exfiltrate and the deeper they entrench. Reducing dwell time directly limits breach impact, so it's the clearest measure of detection effectiveness.
3. An IDS *detects and alerts* on suspicious traffic; an IPS sits inline and can *block/drop* it automatically. IPS prevents but risks blocking legitimate traffic on false positives.
4. Log security-relevant events: logins (success/failure) with username + source IP + timestamp, privilege/role changes, access-control denials, password resets, and admin actions — as structured records. Never log passwords, tokens, session IDs, full card numbers, or other secrets.
5. That detection without response is decoration. The control existed and fired; the failure was organizational — no one triaged/acted in time. Tooling must be paired with monitored alerts, tuned thresholds, and an incident-response process.

</details>

### ✅ Progress Tracker
- [ ] Analyzed my own auth logs and built a top-offending-IP pipeline
- [ ] Deployed at least one monitoring tool (Wazuh or similar) on a lab VM
- [ ] I can explain signature vs. anomaly detection and IDS vs. IPS
- [ ] I understand the Pyramid of Pain and dwell time
- [ ] I can write security logging (Node or Python) that emits structured, secret-free events a SIEM can correlate

## Module 17 — Level 2 Capstone: Attack & Defend Your Lab

### 🎯 The Mission
Run a complete **red-team-then-blue-team** exercise entirely inside the isolated lab you built in Module 15: attack a vulnerable target you own, then detect that same attack with monitoring you deploy. The deliverable is a professional engagement report — the artifact that turns "I did some hacking" into "I can document an assessment like a consultant."

> ⚖️ **Ethics & scope box.** Every target is **your own VM on your own host-only network** (Module 15). This is what makes the attacking authorized. Confirm before you start: your target **cannot reach the internet** (`ping 8.8.8.8` from the target must fail). Never run any of this against a machine you don't own — the lab boundary *is* the authorization.

#### Why this format
You're now combining everything from Level 2: recon (Module 12), traffic analysis (13), the lab (15), and detection (16). A real engagement always produces a written report with a fixed skeleton — **scope → methodology → findings (severity/evidence/remediation) → detection → recommendations**. Practicing both sides on the same event teaches the most valuable lesson in the field: *how an attack looks from the defender's chair.*

### Part A — Red Team (attack)
1. From Kali, perform full reconnaissance on your target (use an ARM-friendly target from Module 15 — Metasploitable 3, a VulnHub ARM box, Juice Shop, or a TryHackMe range; **not** x86 Metasploitable 2 on M2).
2. Document every open port, service, and **version** (`nmap -sV`).
3. Research CVEs affecting those versions (note the CVE IDs and what they allow).
4. Exploit at least **three** distinct issues (e.g., a vulnerable service version, weak/default credentials, an exposed share, a web vuln from Module 14).
5. Capture **evidence** for each step (commands + screenshots). This is your engagement record.

### Part B — Blue Team (detect & recommend)
1. Deploy Wazuh (or similar) on a monitoring VM in the same isolated network.
2. **Replay** your attack and record what the monitoring saw (and missed).
3. Write **three detection rules** that would have caught your own actions (e.g., the port-scan signature from Module 13, a burst of failed logins from Module 16, an exploit's process behavior).
4. Write **three hardening recommendations** for the target — argue what *should* change (patch the vulnerable version, disable the exposed service, enforce strong auth), even where you can't actually patch a deliberately vulnerable box.

### Deliverable & Rubric
A 5–8 page report in your Cyber Journal, written as if presenting to a client.

| Area | Points |
|---|---|
| Scope + confirmed isolation stated up front | 10 |
| Recon: every service/version documented | 15 |
| 3 exploits, each with steps + evidence | 25 |
| Findings written with severity + impact + remediation | 15 |
| Blue-team: what monitoring saw vs. missed | 15 |
| 3 detection rules that would catch the attack | 10 |
| 3 hardening recommendations | 10 |
| **Total** | **100** |

> 🎖️ **Operator Badge Earned.** You can now attack a target *and* detect the attack *and* write it up — technically dangerous, and defensible.

### ➡️ Next Step
Level 3 — **Specialist**, beginning with Module 18 — **The Ethical Hacking Methodology**. You've done the moves; now you learn the formal framework (PTES, the Cyber Kill Chain) that organizes them into a repeatable, professional engagement.

Level 3 isn't just more tools. It's the mindset shift from "learner" to "practitioner." Each module here could easily be its own course. We go deep enough for real competence and point you at where to go further.

> ### 🗺️ Level 3 Roadmap — Specialist · Weeks 21–36
> **The promise:** Level 3 makes you a specialist: ethical-hacking methodology, threat hunting, forensics, incident response, malware analysis, and cloud/mobile/AI threats — all through a defender's lens.
>

> - Run a full ethical-hacking methodology and map activity to MITRE ATT&CK
> - Perform digital forensics and lead an incident response

## Module 18 — The Ethical Hacking Methodology (PTES / Cyber Kill Chain)

### 🎯 Mission Briefing
In Level 2 you *did* the moves; now you learn the *framework* that turns ad-hoc hacking into a professional, repeatable, and legal engagement. Methodology is what separates a security professional from someone who just owns Kali. This module covers the three reference models every practitioner knows — PTES, the Cyber Kill Chain, and MITRE ATT&CK — and the non-negotiable first phase that makes all of it lawful: written authorization and scope.

> ⚖️ **Ethics box.** The defining feature of *ethical* hacking is **authorization in writing, before you touch anything**, plus a defined scope. Everything in Level 3 assumes you only act inside that authorization — your own lab, or a client engagement with a signed contract and rules of engagement. The methodology literally begins with this; skipping it is not "pentesting," it's a crime.

### 📘 Theory

#### Define the words first

- **Methodology** — a repeatable, documented process for an engagement, so results are consistent, complete, and defensible (not "I poked around until something broke").
- **Scope** — the explicit list of in-bounds targets, techniques, and time windows. Anything not in scope is off-limits.
- **Rules of Engagement (RoE)** — the agreed constraints: what you may test, when, how aggressively, who to contact, and what to do if you find something critical.
- **PTES (Penetration Testing Execution Standard)** — a 7-phase engagement model (below).
- **Cyber Kill Chain (Lockheed Martin)** — the attacker's 7-stage lifecycle; defenders aim to break any one link.
- **MITRE ATT&CK** — a continuously updated knowledge base of *real* attacker tactics (the "why") and techniques (the "how"), used by both red and blue teams to plan and detect.

#### PTES — the 7 phases

1. **Pre-engagement** — scope, **rules of engagement, written authorization**. (Legally the most important phase.)
2. **Intelligence Gathering** — OSINT and recon (Module 12).
3. **Threat Modeling** — what's valuable, to whom, and how it might be attacked.
4. **Vulnerability Analysis** — what's weak (Module 19).
5. **Exploitation** — prove the risk is real.
6. **Post-Exploitation** — measure the blast radius (what could an attacker reach next?).
7. **Reporting** — the only deliverable clients actually pay for; a finding without a clear, prioritized remediation is worthless.

#### Cyber Kill Chain (attacker's view)

Reconnaissance → Weaponization → Delivery → Exploitation → Installation → Command & Control → Actions on Objectives. The defensive value: you only need to **break one link** to stop the attack — block delivery (email filtering), prevent installation (EDR), or sever C2 (egress filtering, Module 25).

#### MITRE ATT&CK

The industry-standard taxonomy of attacker behavior, organized by **tactic** (Initial Access, Execution, Persistence, Privilege Escalation, Defense Evasion, …) and the **techniques** under each. Both sides use it: red teams plan realistic attack paths; blue teams map their detections to techniques to find coverage gaps. Bookmark `https://attack.mitre.org` — you'll reference it for your whole career.

#### Why the naive approach fails

> **Naive #1: "I'll just start scanning and exploiting — methodology is bureaucracy."**
> Fails two ways. **Legally:** without written authorization and scope you may be committing a crime regardless of intent. **Practically:** ad-hoc testing is inconsistent and incomplete — you miss whole classes of issues, can't reproduce findings, and can't write a credible report. The methodology *is* the value.

> **Naive #2: "The exploit is the deliverable — popping the box is the point."**
> Fails because clients pay for **risk reduction**, which lives in the report: clear findings, business impact, severity, and prioritized remediation. A spectacular exploit with no actionable write-up changes nothing. Post-exploitation and reporting are where the engagement earns its keep.

> **The correct posture:** authorize and scope in writing first; follow a documented methodology; map findings to a shared language (ATT&CK) so attacker and defender can communicate; and treat the report as the product.

### 🛠️ Lab: Map Your Own Engagement to the Frameworks (M2 / Apple Silicon)

> ⚖️ Use the attack you ran in the **Module 17 capstone** (your owned, isolated lab). You're classifying *your own authorized* actions — no new attacks.

1. Bookmark and explore `https://attack.mitre.org`. Pick the tactic **Initial Access** and read three techniques.
2. Take your Module 17 attack and **map each step to a Kill Chain stage and an ATT&CK technique** (e.g., your `nmap` = Reconnaissance / T1046 Network Service Discovery; your exploit = Exploitation / a relevant technique).
3. Write a one-page **Rules of Engagement template** in your journal: in-scope targets, test window, allowed techniques, emergency contact, handling of critical findings.
4. Read one real public pentest report (search "NCC Group public report" or a vendor's published assessment) and note how it follows the PTES skeleton.

```bash
mkdir -p ~/cyber-journal/m18 && cd ~/cyber-journal/m18
# Capture your framework mapping as a table for the report:
printf 'Step | Kill Chain | ATT&CK\nnmap -sV | Reconnaissance | T1046\n...\n' > mapping.md
code mapping.md roe-template.md
```

### 💻 Vulnerable vs. Secure Practice — encode your scope so you can't go out of bounds

Rules of engagement shouldn't live only in a PDF. When you script any testing, *encode the scope* so a typo can't point your tools at an unauthorized target — the automation equivalent of the authorization phase.

```javascript
// ❌ VULNERABLE PRACTICE — Node: a test script with the target hard-coded ad hoc
const target = process.argv[2];
runScan(target);   // WHY WRONG: no scope check — a fat-fingered arg scans a real, out-of-scope host.

// ✅ SECURE PRACTICE — Node: enforce the authorized scope before acting
const SCOPE = new Set(["192.168.56.20", "127.0.0.1"]);   // your signed-off targets only
const target = process.argv[2];
if (!SCOPE.has(target)) throw new Error(`refusing: ${target} is OUT OF SCOPE`);
runScan(target);   // WHY IT WORKS: the RoE is code; the tool physically cannot touch a non-target.
```

```python
# ❌ VULNERABLE PRACTICE — Python: no scope guard
import sys; run_scan(sys.argv[1])   # WHY WRONG: any IP passed gets scanned, authorized or not

# ✅ SECURE PRACTICE — Python: scope allow-list gate
import sys
SCOPE = {"192.168.56.20", "127.0.0.1"}   # signed-off targets only
target = sys.argv[1]
if target not in SCOPE:
    raise SystemExit(f"refusing: {target} is OUT OF SCOPE")
run_scan(target)   # WHY IT WORKS: scope becomes an enforced precondition, not a hope.
```

### 🌍 Case Study — The Coalfire Iowa Courthouse Arrest (2019)
Two professional penetration testers from Coalfire were **arrested and jailed overnight** while physically testing an Iowa courthouse — work their client (the state judicial branch) had hired them to do. The problem was a **scope and authorization gap**: the contract's wording and the chain of who could authorize physical entry were unclear, and local authorities hadn't been informed. Charges were eventually dropped, but the incident became the field's canonical lesson: **even authorized testers can face real legal jeopardy when scope, authorization, and communication aren't airtight in writing.** The pre-engagement phase isn't paperwork — it's the thing that keeps you out of a cell.

### 🧩 Try It Yourself

**Challenge 1:** Why is "Reporting" listed as the most valuable PTES phase, even though "Exploitation" is the exciting one?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

Because clients buy **risk reduction**, not exploits. The report is where the engagement's value is delivered: documented findings with business impact, severity, evidence, and *prioritized, actionable remediation*. A brilliant exploit with no clear write-up leaves the organization exactly as vulnerable as before. The exploit proves the risk; the report drives the fix. (This is the same "find *and* fix" discipline as the capstones.)

</details>

**Challenge 2:** A defender wants to "break the kill chain." Why is that often easier than preventing the initial compromise outright?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

Because the kill chain has *seven* sequential links and the attacker must succeed at *all* of them, while the defender only has to break *one*. You may not stop the initial phishing email, but you can block the macro from executing (Installation), detect the beacon to the C2 server and sever it (Command & Control), or catch the exfiltration (Actions on Objectives). Defense in depth means stacking controls across stages so a single failure doesn't equal a breach.

</details>

### 🛡️ Pitfalls & False-Confidence Traps
- **Acting before written authorization.** Verbal "go ahead" is not authorization. Get scope and RoE signed (Coalfire).
- **Treating the exploit as the deliverable.** The report and remediation are the product; document as you go.
- **Scope creep mid-engagement.** "While I was in there I also tested…" can be unauthorized access. Stay in scope; amend in writing if needed.
- **Skipping threat modeling.** Without knowing what's valuable, you test everything shallowly and the crown jewels insufficiently.
- **Not mapping to ATT&CK.** Without a shared taxonomy, red and blue can't compare attack paths to detection coverage.

### ❓ Quiz
1. What are the 7 PTES phases, and which is legally the most important?
2. Why does breaking a single link disrupt the entire Cyber Kill Chain?
3. What is MITRE ATT&CK, and how do red and blue teams each use it?
4. Why is the report — not the exploit — the deliverable clients pay for?
5. What does the Coalfire case teach about authorization and scope?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Answers</strong></summary>

1. Pre-engagement, Intelligence Gathering, Threat Modeling, Vulnerability Analysis, Exploitation, Post-Exploitation, Reporting. **Pre-engagement** (scope + written authorization) is legally most important — it's what makes the rest lawful.
2. The chain is a sequence the attacker must complete end to end; the defender only needs to break one link (block delivery, prevent installation, sever C2, or stop exfiltration) to disrupt the whole operation.
3. A continuously updated knowledge base of real attacker tactics and techniques. Red teams use it to plan realistic attack paths; blue teams map detections to techniques to find and close coverage gaps — a shared language for both.
4. Because clients buy risk reduction, which is delivered through documented, prioritized, actionable findings and remediation. The exploit only proves the risk; the report enables the fix.
5. Authorization and scope must be explicit, written, and communicated to all relevant parties (including local authorities for physical tests). Even contracted, well-intentioned testers can be arrested when those are ambiguous — the pre-engagement phase is a legal safeguard, not paperwork.

</details>

## Module 19 — Vulnerability Assessment

### 🎯 Mission Briefing
This is PTES phase 4 (Module 18). A vulnerability assessment is *not* "run a scanner and email the PDF" — it's the disciplined work of finding weaknesses, **separating real issues from false positives**, and **prioritizing** by what actually matters. As a developer, the most valuable version of this skill is scanning your *own* code's dependencies and triaging the results — turning a wall of CVEs into the two you must fix this sprint.

> ⚖️ **Ethics box.** Scan **only your own lab target and your own code/dependencies**. A vulnerability scan is noisy and intrusive; running one against a system you don't own is unauthorized activity. Everything here targets `192.168.56.20` (your Module 15 lab) and your own projects.

### 📘 Theory

#### Define the words first

- **Vulnerability** — a weakness that could be exploited to violate confidentiality, integrity, or availability.
- **CVE (Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures)** — a unique public ID for a specific known vulnerability (e.g., `CVE-2021-44228`, Log4Shell).
- **CVSS (Common Vulnerability Scoring System)** — a 0.0–10.0 severity score from factors like Attack Vector, Complexity, Privileges Required, User Interaction, Scope, and CIA impact. It measures *intrinsic severity*, not *your* risk.
- **EPSS (Exploit Prediction Scoring System)** — the probability a CVE will be exploited in the wild soon. Pairs with CVSS to prioritize.
- **False positive** — a scanner finding that isn't actually exploitable in context. **Triage** — validating and prioritizing findings.
- **Vulnerability scan vs. penetration test** — a scan *finds* potential issues (often automated, breadth); a pentest *proves* exploitability and chains issues (manual, depth).

#### Why the naive approach fails

> **Naive #1: "The scanner found 400 vulnerabilities; we have 400 problems."**
> Fails because raw scanner output is full of **false positives**, duplicates, and findings that don't apply to your configuration. An un-triaged dump overwhelms teams, who then ignore it entirely. The work is *triage*: validate, de-duplicate, and rank.

> **Naive #2: "Fix the highest CVSS scores first."**
> Fails because CVSS measures *intrinsic* severity in the abstract, not *your* risk. A CVSS 9.8 in a component you don't expose, behind authentication, on an internal host may matter far less than a CVSS 6.5 on your public login page. Real prioritization combines **severity (CVSS) × exploit likelihood (EPSS/known-exploited) × exposure (is it reachable?) × asset value**.

> **Naive #3: "We scanned the servers, so we're covered."**
> Fails because most of your attack surface is *your dependencies* (Module 14's Equifax/A03). A modern app pulls in hundreds of third-party packages, any of which can carry a known CVE. Scanning the running host misses the vulnerable library compiled into your app — you must scan the software bill of materials too.

> **The correct posture:** scan broadly (hosts *and* dependencies), then *triage*: validate findings, prioritize by combined risk (severity × exploitability × exposure × value), and feed the top items into remediation with owners and deadlines.

### 🛠️ Lab: Scan a Target and Your Own Dependencies (M2 / Apple Silicon)

```bash
mkdir -p ~/cyber-journal/m19 && cd ~/cyber-journal/m19

# Network/web scanner: Nuclei (ARM64-native via Homebrew), template-driven & fast
brew install nuclei
nuclei -update-templates
nuclei -u http://192.168.56.20            # YOUR lab target only (Module 15)

# Dependency scanning of YOUR OWN code (this is the dev's daily vuln assessment):
npm audit --audit-level=high              # Node project: known CVEs in your packages
pipx run pip-audit                        # Python project: same idea for pip deps
trivy fs .                                # brew install trivy — scans deps, IaC, secrets

# Full standalone scanners (heavier; mind 16GB RAM / 512GB SSD):
#  - Nessus Essentials (free up to 16 IPs)   - OpenVAS/Greenbone (fully open source)
```

> **M2 note:** `nuclei`, `trivy`, and `pip-audit` are ARM64-native (Homebrew/pipx). OpenVAS is heaviest — run it in a VM/Docker and stop it when done to reclaim RAM/disk.

### 💻 Vulnerable vs. Secure Practice — triage by *risk*, not just CVSS

```javascript
// ❌ VULNERABLE PRACTICE — Node: sort findings by CVSS alone, fix top-down
findings.sort((a, b) => b.cvss - a.cvss);
fixFirst(findings[0]);   // WHY WRONG: ignores whether it's exposed/exploitable in YOUR context.

// ✅ SECURE PRACTICE — Node: rank by combined risk before choosing what to fix
function riskScore(f) {
  // WHY IT WORKS: severity is necessary but not sufficient. Multiply by real-world
  // exploit likelihood (EPSS / known-exploited), reachability, and asset value, so an
  // exposed internet-facing 6.5 outranks an unreachable internal 9.8.
  return f.cvss * f.epss * (f.internetExposed ? 1.0 : 0.3) * f.assetValue;
}
const prioritized = findings.sort((a, b) => riskScore(b) - riskScore(a));
fixFirst(prioritized[0]);
```

```python
# ❌ VULNERABLE PRACTICE — Python: CVSS-only triage
findings.sort(key=lambda f: f["cvss"], reverse=True)
fix_first(findings[0])   # WHY WRONG: severity != your risk

# ✅ SECURE PRACTICE — Python: combined-risk triage
def risk_score(f):
    # severity x exploit-likelihood x exposure x asset value
    return f["cvss"] * f["epss"] * (1.0 if f["internet_exposed"] else 0.3) * f["asset_value"]
prioritized = sorted(findings, key=risk_score, reverse=True)
fix_first(prioritized[0])
# WHY IT WORKS: prioritizes what an attacker can actually reach and is likely to exploit.
```

### 🌍 Case Study — Log4Shell (CVE-2021-44228, 2021)
A CVSS **10.0** vulnerability in the ubiquitous Java logging library **Log4j** let attackers achieve remote code execution by getting a single crafted string logged (e.g., in a username or User-Agent). It was catastrophic precisely because Log4j was a *transitive dependency* buried inside countless applications — most teams **didn't know they were running it**. The scramble that followed taught the whole industry this module's core lessons: (1) you can't assess what you can't see, so you need a **software bill of materials** and dependency scanning, not just host scans; and (2) prioritization must weigh **exposure and exploitability**, because Log4Shell was both internet-reachable and trivially exploitable everywhere it appeared. Dependency scanning (`npm audit`, `pip-audit`, `trivy`) is the direct, practical descendant of this lesson.

### 🧩 Try It Yourself

**Challenge 1:** Your scanner reports a CVSS 9.8 RCE in a library, but it's only used by an internal batch job not reachable from the internet, behind authentication. A CVSS 6.1 reflected XSS sits on your public login page. Which do you fix first, and why?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

Likely the **6.1 XSS on the public login page first** — though the *correct* answer is "compute combined risk, then decide." CVSS measures intrinsic severity, not your risk. The 9.8 is unreachable from outside and gated by auth (low exposure/likelihood right now); the 6.1 is internet-facing on a high-value page (high exposure, targets credentials). Risk = severity × exploitability × exposure × asset value, so the lower-CVSS but exposed issue can outrank the higher-CVSS but unreachable one. (You still schedule the 9.8 — exposure can change.)

</details>

**Challenge 2:** Why does `npm audit` / `pip-audit` catch things an `nmap` scan of your running server never will?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

`nmap` sees *network services and their versions* from the outside; it can't see the third-party libraries *compiled or bundled into* your application. A vulnerable logging or parsing library (Log4Shell-style) exposes no distinct port — it's reachable through your normal app traffic. Dependency scanners read your lockfile/SBOM and match every package version against known-CVE databases, surfacing the vulnerable component inside your build that a network scan is blind to.

</details>

### 🛡️ Pitfalls & False-Confidence Traps
- **Emailing the raw scanner PDF.** Un-triaged output is noise; validate, de-duplicate, and prioritize first.
- **CVSS-only prioritization.** Combine severity with exploitability (EPSS/known-exploited), exposure, and asset value.
- **Scanning hosts but not dependencies.** Most surface is third-party code (Log4Shell). Scan your SBOM.
- **Ignoring false positives until you ignore everything.** High noise trains teams to dismiss the whole report — tune and validate.
- **Treating a scan as a pentest.** Scans find *potential* issues; only validation/exploitation proves real, chained risk.

### ❓ Quiz
1. What's the difference between a vulnerability scan and a penetration test?
2. What does CVSS measure, and why is it insufficient for prioritization on its own?
3. What four factors should real risk-based prioritization combine?
4. Why must you scan dependencies, not just running hosts (cite Log4Shell)?
5. What is triage, and why is an un-triaged scanner dump worse than useless?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Answers</strong></summary>

1. A scan automatically *finds* potential weaknesses across breadth; a pentest *manually validates and chains* them to prove real exploitability and impact (depth). A scan is an input to, not a substitute for, a pentest.
2. CVSS measures a vulnerability's *intrinsic* severity (attack vector, complexity, impact, etc.), abstractly. It ignores whether the issue is exposed/reachable in your environment and how likely it is to be exploited, so it can't rank *your* risk alone.
3. Severity (CVSS) × exploit likelihood (EPSS / known-exploited) × exposure (reachable from where?) × asset value. An exposed, likely-exploited issue on a valuable asset outranks an unreachable high-CVSS one.
4. Because most of your attack surface is third-party code bundled into your app, invisible to a network scan. Log4Shell lived inside countless apps as a transitive dependency; only an SBOM/dependency scan reveals such components.
5. Triage is validating, de-duplicating, and prioritizing findings. An un-triaged dump is full of false positives and irrelevant items that overwhelm teams, who then ignore the entire report — so the real, important issue gets buried with the noise.

</details>

## Module 20 — Web App Pentesting with Burp Suite

### 🎯 Mission Briefing
Automated scanners (Module 19) find the easy, known issues; the *interesting* bugs — broken access control, business-logic flaws, IDOR — are found by a human watching and *modifying* real traffic. Burp Suite is the field's standard tool for exactly that: an intercepting proxy that sits between your browser and the server so you can see and change every request. As a developer, Burp is also the fastest way to *prove* that your client-side checks are bypassable — and therefore why every check must be re-enforced server-side.

> ⚖️ **Ethics box.** Point Burp **only** at applications you own (your Module 15 lab, your own dev app on localhost) or at **PortSwigger's Web Security Academy**, which is purpose-built and authorized for this practice. Intercepting and modifying traffic to a site you don't own is an attack. Burp will happily tamper with *any* site — the authorization boundary is on you.

### 📘 Theory

#### Define the words first

- **Intercepting proxy** — a tool that sits between your browser and the server, so every request/response passes through it and can be *inspected and modified* in flight. (You are deliberately man-in-the-middling *your own* traffic.)
- **Burp CA certificate** — a certificate you install in your browser so Burp can decrypt your *own* HTTPS traffic for inspection. (Only works because you chose to trust it on your own machine.)
- **Proxy / Intercept** — pause a request, edit it, then forward it. **Repeater** — resend and tweak a single request endlessly. **Intruder** — automate sending many variations (fuzzing). **Decoder** — encode/decode Base64/URL/hex.
- **Client-side control** — any restriction enforced in the browser (hidden fields, disabled buttons, JS validation). Burp exists partly to demonstrate these are *suggestions*, not security.

#### Why the naive assumptions fail

> **Naive #1: "The form only lets users submit valid values, so the server is safe."**
> Fails because Burp (or `curl`) lets the attacker send *any* value directly, bypassing the form entirely. A `<select>` with three options, a hidden `price` field, a disabled "admin" checkbox — all are editable in the proxy. This is the visceral proof of Module 14's rule: **the client is attacker-controlled; every check must be re-done server-side.**

> **Naive #2: "Scanners already test my app, so manual testing adds nothing."**
> Fails because scanners can't understand your *business logic* or *authorization model*. They won't know that order #1002 belongs to a different user, or that the "apply coupon" step can be replayed for infinite discounts. Manual testing with Repeater — changing one ID, replaying one request — finds the IDOR and logic flaws that automated tools structurally miss.

> **The correct posture:** use Burp to *observe and manipulate* requests like an attacker, find what scanners miss (access control, logic), and then fix it where it belongs — on the server, re-validating and re-authorizing every request regardless of what the UI allowed.

#### A worked example: finding IDOR with Repeater

You're logged in and view your order at `GET /api/orders/1002`. You send that request to **Repeater**, change `1002` to `1001`, and resend. If you get back *someone else's* order, that's an **IDOR / broken access control** (Module 14, A01) — the server returned an object without checking you own it. One changed digit, one resend; no scanner needed.

### 🛠️ Lab: Burp + PortSwigger Academy (M2 / Apple Silicon)

> **M2 note:** Burp Suite Community Edition runs **natively on Apple Silicon** (download the macOS Apple Silicon build, or `brew install --cask burp-suite`). It's free; the Pro scanner is paid but unnecessary for learning.

1. Install Burp Suite Community. Configure Firefox to proxy through `127.0.0.1:8080` (use a separate Firefox profile or FoxyProxy so you can toggle it).
2. Install Burp's CA certificate in that Firefox profile so your *own* HTTPS decrypts.
3. Master the core tools: **Proxy** (intercept/modify), **Repeater** (replay/tweak), **Intruder** (fuzz), **Decoder**.
4. Work the free **PortSwigger Web Security Academy** labs in order: Authentication → Access Control → SQL Injection → XSS → CSRF/SSRF/XXE.

```bash
mkdir -p ~/cyber-journal/m20 && cd ~/cyber-journal/m20
brew install --cask burp-suite        # ARM64-native macOS build
# Practice the IDOR drill against YOUR OWN localhost app (or a PortSwigger lab):
#  1. Log in, request GET /api/orders/<your-id>
#  2. Send to Repeater, change the id, resend
#  3. If you see another user's data -> IDOR. Document it, then write the server-side fix below.
```

### 💻 Vulnerable vs. Secure Code — the IDOR you found in Repeater, fixed server-side

```javascript
// ❌ VULNERABLE — Node: returns the order by id, trusting the request (Burp changes the id freely)
app.get("/api/orders/:id", requireAuth, (req, res) => {
  // WHY WRONG: any logged-in user can request ANY order id. The UI only showed THEIR orders,
  // but Burp/Repeater sends arbitrary ids. No ownership check = IDOR.
  res.json(db.getOrder(req.params.id));
});
```

```javascript
// ✅ SECURE — Node: re-authorize on the server for every request
app.get("/api/orders/:id", requireAuth, (req, res) => {
  const order = db.getOrder(req.params.id);
  // WHY IT WORKS: the server independently verifies the authenticated user owns this object,
  // so a tampered id from Burp returns 403/404 instead of someone else's data.
  if (!order || order.userId !== req.user.id) return res.sendStatus(404);
  res.json(order);
});
```

```python
# ❌ VULNERABLE — Python/Flask: no ownership check (Burp swaps the id)
@app.get("/api/orders/<int:id>")
@login_required
def get_order(id):
    return jsonify(db.get_order(id))   # WHY WRONG: any id returns any user's order = IDOR

# ✅ SECURE — Python/Flask: verify ownership server-side
@app.get("/api/orders/<int:id>")
@login_required
def get_order(id):
    order = db.get_order(id)
    if not order or order.user_id != current_user.id:
        abort(404)                     # WHY IT WORKS: tampered id can't reach others' data
    return jsonify(order)
```

### 🌍 Case Study — IDOR / BOLA, the Quiet Epidemic
Broken Object Level Authorization (BOLA/IDOR) is consistently the **#1 risk on the OWASP API Security Top 10** and the root cause of a long line of real breaches: mobile apps and APIs that returned account, message, or document objects by ID **without checking the caller owned them**. In multiple disclosed cases, simply incrementing an ID in a request — exactly the Repeater drill above — exposed other users' personal data, ride histories, or medical records. It's quiet because it produces no error and no crash; the server cheerfully returns the data. The only defense is the server-side ownership check in the secure code — and Burp is how you discover whether you have it.

### 🧩 Try It Yourself

**Challenge 1:** A shopping site sends `POST /checkout` with a hidden field `price=49.99`. Using Burp, what would you try, and what does it reveal about where trust belongs?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

Intercept the request in Burp Proxy and change `price` to `0.01` (or `0`), then forward it. If the order completes at the tampered price, the server **trusted a client-supplied price** — a classic business-logic flaw. The lesson: never trust *any* value from the client, especially prices, quantities, user IDs, or roles. The server must recompute the price from the catalog by product ID, ignoring whatever the client sent. Hidden fields are not secret and not trustworthy.

</details>

**Challenge 2:** Why can Burp find an IDOR that a vulnerability scanner usually can't?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

A scanner doesn't understand your *authorization model* — it doesn't know that order 1001 belongs to a different user than the one whose session it's using, so it can't tell that receiving 1001's data is a *violation* rather than a normal response (HTTP 200, valid JSON). A human in Burp logs in as a known user, requests a known-foreign ID in Repeater, and *recognizes* that seeing someone else's data is wrong. IDOR/BOLA is a context- and logic-dependent flaw, which is precisely the category automated tools miss and manual testing catches.

</details>

### 🛡️ Pitfalls & False-Confidence Traps
- **Trusting hidden/disabled fields and client validation.** Burp edits them freely. Re-validate and re-authorize on the server.
- **Pointing Burp at sites you don't own.** Intercept/modify against others is an attack; use your lab or PortSwigger.
- **Leaving the Burp proxy/CA configured in your daily browser.** Use a dedicated profile; a trusted Burp CA in everyday browsing weakens your own TLS. Remove it when done.
- **Relying on scanners alone.** They miss access-control and business-logic bugs. Manual Repeater testing is where IDOR/BOLA is found.
- **Filtering by HTTP status to judge success.** IDOR returns a normal 200; you must inspect the *content* to see you got someone else's data.

### ❓ Quiz
1. What does an intercepting proxy like Burp let you do that the browser alone does not?
2. Why are hidden form fields and disabled buttons not a security control?
3. Walk through how you'd find an IDOR using Burp Repeater.
4. Why does manual web testing find bugs that automated scanners miss?
5. What is the single correct server-side fix for the IDOR you find, and why does it work?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Answers</strong></summary>

1. It sits between browser and server so you can intercept, inspect, and *modify* every request/response (and replay them in Repeater, fuzz them in Intruder) — sending values the UI would never let you submit.
2. Because everything in the browser is attacker-controlled. Hidden fields are visible and editable in the proxy/DevTools; disabled buttons can be re-enabled or their request crafted directly. They affect UX, not security.
3. Log in as a known user, capture a request for one of *your* objects (e.g., `GET /api/orders/1002`), send it to Repeater, change the ID to one you don't own (`1001`), resend, and check whether you receive another user's data — if so, it's an IDOR/broken access control.
4. Scanners don't understand business logic or your authorization model, so they can't tell that a valid-looking 200 response actually leaked another user's data or that a step can be abused. Humans recognize context-dependent violations.
5. Enforce an **ownership/authorization check on the server for every request** — verify the authenticated user is permitted to access the specific object, returning 403/404 otherwise. It works because it no longer trusts the client-supplied identifier; tampering in Burp can't bypass a server-side authorization decision.

</details>

## Module 21 — Intro to Exploitation: Buffer Overflows (Conceptual + Safe Practice)

### 🎯 Mission Briefing
You don't need to write zero-days to be an excellent security professional, but you must *understand* memory-level exploitation: the stack, the heap, why a missing bounds check becomes code execution, and the modern mitigations. As a full-stack developer, the most important takeaway is *why this whole bug class barely exists in the languages you use* — and where it can still bite you (native add-ons, `unsafe` blocks, and the size/format checks you still owe even in memory-safe code).

> ⚖️ **Ethics box.** Practice memory exploitation only in **authorized teaching environments** — PicoCTF, OverTheWire, or your own lab binaries. These platforms exist to be exploited. Never run exploit code you don't understand, and never against systems you don't own.

### 📘 Theory

#### Define the words first

- **Buffer** — a fixed-size region of memory holding data (e.g., a 64-byte array for a username).
- **Stack** — the region holding function-local variables and, crucially, the **saved return address** (where execution resumes when a function returns). It grows downward.
- **Buffer overflow** — writing more data into a buffer than it can hold, so the excess overwrites *adjacent* memory.
- **Return address overwrite** — when the overflow reaches the saved return address, an attacker can redirect execution to code of their choosing — turning a memory bug into arbitrary code execution (RCE).
- **Memory-safe language** — one whose runtime checks bounds and manages memory (JavaScript/Node, Python, Java, Go, Rust) so classic overflows are prevented automatically. **Memory-unsafe**: C/C++, where the programmer must enforce bounds manually.

#### Process memory layout

```
+-------------------+  High memory
|      Stack        |  local vars, saved return addresses  (grows down)
+-------------------+
|       ...         |
|      Heap         |  dynamically allocated memory        (grows up)
+-------------------+
|  BSS / Data       |  globals
+-------------------+
|      Text         |  the program's machine code
+-------------------+  Low memory
```

#### The classic stack overflow, step by step

A C function copies attacker input into a fixed buffer *without checking the length*. The attacker supplies more bytes than the buffer holds. The extra bytes spill past the buffer and overwrite the saved **return address** on the stack. When the function returns, the CPU jumps to the attacker-controlled address — into injected code or a reused gadget. One missing length check became full control of execution.

#### Why modern systems resist this (defense in depth at the memory layer)

- **ASLR** — randomizes memory addresses so the attacker can't predict where to jump.
- **DEP / NX** — marks the stack/heap non-executable, so injected data can't run as code.
- **Stack canaries** — a random value placed before the return address; if an overflow overwrites it, the program detects the change and aborts before returning.
- **CFI (Control-Flow Integrity)** — verifies that return/jump targets are legitimate.

#### Why the naive assumptions fail

> **Naive #1: "I write in JavaScript/Python, so memory bugs aren't my problem."**
> Mostly true — and that's the headline: **memory-safe languages prevent classic overflows by checking bounds for you**, which is a *huge* security win and a real reason to prefer them. But it's not absolute. You can still hit memory bugs through **native add-ons** (Node C++ addons, Python C extensions), through a vulnerable **dependency** written in C (think image/parser libraries), and through `unsafe` blocks in otherwise-safe languages. And memory safety does **nothing** for the *logical* input bugs (injection, IDOR) you've spent Modules 14/20 on. Safety from overflows ≠ safety from all input.

> **Naive #2: "Mitigations like ASLR/DEP mean overflows are solved."**
> Fails because mitigations *raise the cost*, they don't eliminate the bug. Attackers developed ROP (return-oriented programming) to defeat NX, info leaks to defeat ASLR, and so on. Mitigations are defense in depth; the real fix is not having the bug — bounds-checked code and memory-safe languages.

> **The correct posture:** prefer memory-safe languages; keep native dependencies patched (Module 19); validate input *length and format* even in safe languages; and understand the mitigations so you can reason about why an old C service is dangerous.

### 🛠️ Lab: Understand the Bug Safely (M2 / Apple Silicon)

> ⚖️ Authorized practice only: PicoCTF (browser-based), OverTheWire, or your own compiled binary.

- **PicoCTF** binary-exploitation challenges at `https://picoctf.org` — free, runs in the browser, no local risk.
- **OverTheWire `Narnia`** wargame — classic, authorized buffer-overflow practice over SSH.
- Local debugging on ARM64: `brew install gdb` (note: low-level stack-smashing tutorials are usually written for x86; the *concepts* are identical on Apple Silicon's ARM64, but exact offsets/registers differ — the browser CTFs sidestep this nicely on M2).

```bash
mkdir -p ~/cyber-journal/m21 && cd ~/cyber-journal/m21
# Observe a memory-safe language REFUSING the overflow (run these — they're safe):
node -e 'const b = Buffer.alloc(8); b.write("A".repeat(100)); console.log(b.length)'   # writes only 8; no corruption
python3 -c 'b = bytearray(8); 
try:
    b[0:100] = b"A"*100
except Exception as e:
    print("blocked:", e)'   # the runtime enforces bounds instead of smashing the stack
```

### 💻 Vulnerable vs. Secure Code — the overflow, and why your languages dodge it

```c
// ❌ VULNERABLE — C (memory-UNSAFE): no bounds check, classic stack smash
void greet(char *input) {
  char name[64];
  strcpy(name, input);   // WHY WRONG: copies ALL of input; >64 bytes overflows 'name' and
                         // overwrites the saved return address -> attacker controls execution.
  printf("Hello %s\n", name);
}
// ✅ SECURE — C: bound the copy to the buffer size
void greet_safe(const char *input) {
  char name[64];
  snprintf(name, sizeof name, "%s", input);   // WHY IT WORKS: writes at most 63 bytes + NUL,
  printf("Hello %s\n", name);                 // so input can never spill past the buffer.
}
```

```javascript
// ✅ Node (memory-SAFE by design): the same mistake simply cannot smash the stack
function greet(input) {
  // WHY IT WORKS: JS strings/Buffers are bounds-checked and managed by the runtime.
  // Over-long input is just a long string — no adjacent memory is overwritten, no RCE.
  // Your job here is LOGICAL validation, not memory safety:
  if (input.length > 64) throw new Error("name too long");   // policy/UX, not memory protection
  return `Hello ${input}`;
}
// CAVEAT: native addons (require('some-c-addon')) CAN reintroduce memory bugs — keep deps patched.
```

```python
# ✅ Python (memory-SAFE by design): no manual bounds management, no classic overflow
def greet(name: str) -> str:
    # WHY IT WORKS: Python manages memory and bounds; an over-long string can't overwrite
    # the return address. You still validate length/format as POLICY, not memory safety:
    if len(name) > 64:
        raise ValueError("name too long")
    return f"Hello {name}"
# CAVEAT: C extensions / ctypes can reintroduce memory unsafety — patch native deps (Module 19).
```

### 🌍 Case Study — The Morris Worm (1988) and Its Modern Echoes
The **Morris Worm**, one of the first internet worms, spread in part by exploiting a **stack buffer overflow** in the Unix `fingerd` service — the exact `strcpy`-without-bounds bug above. It infected a large fraction of the early internet and led to the first conviction under the U.S. Computer Fraud and Abuse Act. Decades later the same bug class still produced **Heartbleed** (Module 10, an out-of-bounds *read*) and **EternalBlue** (the SMB overflow behind WannaCry, Module 4). The throughline: **missing bounds checks in memory-unsafe code have caused the largest incidents in computing history** — which is precisely why the industry is migrating safety-critical code to memory-safe languages, and why "I write in a memory-safe language" is a genuine security advantage worth understanding.

### 🧩 Try It Yourself

**Challenge 1:** Your teammate says "we use Rust/Go/Node, so buffer overflows are impossible for us — we can ignore them." Where is that right, and where is it dangerously incomplete?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

**Right:** memory-safe languages bounds-check and manage memory, so the *classic* stack/heap overflow in your own application code is effectively prevented — a real and large security benefit. **Incomplete/dangerous:** (1) **native code** still in the picture — Node C++ addons, Python C extensions, Rust `unsafe` blocks, and any C/C++ *dependency* (image/parsing libraries) can still overflow; (2) memory safety does nothing for **logical** vulnerabilities — injection, IDOR, auth flaws, SSRF — which are most web bugs; (3) you still owe **input length/format validation** as policy. So: celebrate the memory-safety win, but keep native deps patched (Module 19) and keep doing everything from Modules 14/20.

</details>

**Challenge 2:** ASLR and DEP are enabled, yet exploit developers still write working exploits. How, and what does that say about relying on mitigations?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

Attackers defeat **DEP/NX** with **ROP (return-oriented programming)** — chaining existing executable code snippets ("gadgets") instead of injecting new code — and defeat **ASLR** with **information leaks** that reveal a real address to de-randomize the layout. Mitigations *raise the cost and complexity* of exploitation but don't remove the underlying bug. The lesson: mitigations are valuable defense in depth, but the only true fix is **not having the vulnerability** — bounds-checked code, memory-safe languages, and patched dependencies.

</details>

### 🛡️ Pitfalls & False-Confidence Traps
- **"Memory-safe language = no memory bugs ever."** Native addons, C dependencies, and `unsafe` blocks reintroduce risk. Patch and audit them.
- **Confusing memory safety with input safety.** Safe languages stop overflows, not injection/IDOR/SSRF. Keep validating and authorizing.
- **Trusting mitigations as the fix.** ASLR/DEP/canaries raise cost; ROP and info leaks bypass them. Eliminate the bug.
- **Running unknown exploit code.** It can be backdoored or destructive. Only run code you understand, in authorized labs.
- **Ignoring length/format validation in safe languages.** Over-long or malformed input is still a logic/DoS/abuse concern even without memory corruption.

### ❓ Quiz
1. In a classic stack overflow, what gets overwritten to achieve code execution, and why is that powerful?
2. Why do memory-safe languages (Node, Python, Go, Rust) largely prevent this bug class?
3. Name two ways a memory bug can still affect a "memory-safe" application.
4. What do ASLR, DEP/NX, and stack canaries each do, and why aren't they a complete fix?
5. Why is the industry migrating safety-critical software to memory-safe languages (cite the historical impact)?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Answers</strong></summary>

1. The saved **return address** on the stack. Overwriting it lets the attacker redirect execution when the function returns — to injected code or reused gadgets — converting a memory write into arbitrary code execution (full control).
2. Their runtimes perform automatic **bounds checking** and manage memory, so writing past the end of a buffer raises an error or simply can't reach adjacent memory like the return address — the classic overflow is prevented by construction.
3. Through **native code**: C/C++ extensions and addons (Node N-API addons, Python C extensions), `unsafe` blocks (Rust), or a vulnerable C/C++ **dependency** (e.g., an image/parser library) compiled into the app.
4. ASLR randomizes addresses (attacker can't predict targets); DEP/NX marks data memory non-executable (injected code can't run); stack canaries detect return-address overwrites and abort. They raise exploitation cost but are bypassable (ROP, info leaks), so they're defense in depth, not the cure.
5. Because missing bounds checks in memory-unsafe code caused the largest incidents in computing history (Morris Worm, Heartbleed, EternalBlue/WannaCry). Memory-safe languages remove that entire bug class by construction, which is why governments and major vendors now push for them in safety-critical software.

</details>

### ✅ Progress Tracker
- [ ] I can explain how overwriting the return address yields code execution
- [ ] I can explain why Node/Python/Go/Rust largely prevent classic overflows
- [ ] I can name two ways memory bugs still reach a memory-safe app (native deps, `unsafe`)
- [ ] I can explain ASLR, DEP/NX, and canaries and why they aren't a complete fix
- [ ] I completed at least one PicoCTF or OverTheWire binary challenge

## Module 22 — Digital Forensics Fundamentals

### 🎯 Mission Briefing
When the attack already happened, forensics is how you get *answers*: what did the attacker touch, what did they take, and can you prove it? This module covers the discipline of investigating digital events after the fact — for incident response (Module 23) and, sometimes, for court. The non-negotiable theme is **evidence integrity**: do everything in a way that proves you didn't alter what you found. As a developer, that same principle — *preserve before you touch, and hash to prove integrity* — shapes how you build audit logs and handle incidents in your own systems.

> ⚖️ **Ethics box.** Forensics tools read disk and memory images that may contain *anyone's* private data. Practice **only on the public sample images** linked below or on **images of your own systems**. Examining someone else's device/data without authorization is a serious privacy and legal violation, even with good intentions.

### 📘 Theory

#### Define the words first

- **Digital forensics** — methodically collecting, preserving, and analyzing digital evidence so conclusions are accurate and defensible.
- **Chain of custody** — a documented record of everyone who handled the evidence, when, and what they did. Breaks in it can make evidence inadmissible.
- **Write blocker** — hardware/software that allows *reading* a drive but physically prevents *writing* to it, so analysis can't alter the original.
- **Forensic image** — a bit-for-bit copy of a disk/memory; you analyze the *copy*, never the original.
- **Hashing for integrity** — compute a hash (e.g., SHA-256, Module 3) of the original and the copy; matching hashes prove the copy is identical and nothing changed during handling.
- **Order of volatility** — collect the most ephemeral evidence first (RAM, network connections, running processes) before the durable stuff (disk), because volatile data vanishes on power-off.

#### Artifact types

Disk (files, *deleted* files, filesystem metadata), memory/RAM (passwords, keys, running processes, injected code), network (PCAPs from Module 13, logs from Module 16), mobile, and cloud (API/audit logs, often with limited access).

#### Why the naive approach fails

> **Naive #1: "I'll just poke around the live machine to see what happened."**
> Fails because *touching the evidence changes it*. Browsing files updates access timestamps, running tools writes to disk and memory, and a reboot destroys all RAM-resident evidence (keys, processes, network state). You can also tip off attacker malware to self-destruct. The discipline: capture volatile data first (per order of volatility), image the disk with a write blocker, hash everything, and analyze the *copy*.

> **Naive #2: "Deleting a file removes it, so there's nothing to recover."**
> Fails because deletion usually just unlinks the file's directory entry; the data blocks remain on disk until overwritten. Forensic tools recover deleted files, slack space, and filesystem journals routinely. (The flip side for *defenders*: truly sensitive data needs secure erasure/crypto-shredding, not just "delete.")

> **Naive #3: "Evidence is evidence — how I collected it doesn't matter."**
> Fails because without chain of custody and integrity hashes, you can't prove the evidence wasn't altered, so it's worthless in any dispute (and untrustworthy even internally). Process *is* the evidence's credibility.

> **The correct posture:** preserve before you analyze; collect in order of volatility; never write to the original (write blocker + work on a hashed image); maintain chain of custody; and document relentlessly as if you'll testify.

### 🛠️ Lab: Disk & Memory Forensics on Sample Images (M2 / Apple Silicon)

> ⚖️ Use **public sample images** or images of **your own** systems only.

- **Autopsy** (free disk-forensics GUI): open a sample disk image; explore deleted files, browser history, and USB-device history. Sample images: `https://dfir.science` and CTF sources.
- **Volatility 3** (memory forensics) — ARM64-native via pipx:
```bash
mkdir -p ~/cyber-journal/m22 && cd ~/cyber-journal/m22
pipx install volatility3            # or: pip install volatility3
# First, hash the evidence so you can prove integrity (and re-verify later):
shasum -a 256 memory.raw | tee memory.raw.sha256
# Analyze the COPY (these are read-only against the image):
vol -f memory.raw windows.pslist    # running processes
vol -f memory.raw windows.netscan   # network connections at capture time
vol -f memory.raw windows.malfind   # injected/hidden code
# Re-hash afterward and confirm it MATCHES memory.raw.sha256 (you didn't alter the evidence).
shasum -a 256 -c memory.raw.sha256
```

### 💻 Vulnerable vs. Secure Practice — evidence integrity, the developer's version

The forensic principle "preserve the original, prove integrity with a hash" is exactly how you should handle an incident artifact (a leaked log, a suspicious upload) in your own systems.

```javascript
// ❌ VULNERABLE PRACTICE — Node: analyze a suspect file in place, mutating it
const fs = require("fs");
const data = fs.readFileSync("/var/incidents/suspect.bin");
fs.appendFileSync("/var/incidents/suspect.bin", "\n# analyst note");  // WHY WRONG: you just
// modified the evidence. Its hash changes; chain of custody is broken; it's now untrustworthy.

// ✅ SECURE PRACTICE — Node: hash, copy, work on the copy, never touch the original
const crypto = require("crypto");
const original = fs.readFileSync("/var/incidents/suspect.bin");
const digest = crypto.createHash("sha256").update(original).digest("hex");
fs.writeFileSync("/var/incidents/suspect.bin.sha256", digest);   // record integrity
fs.copyFileSync("/var/incidents/suspect.bin", "/tmp/working-copy.bin");  // analyze THIS
// WHY IT WORKS: the original is read once and never written; the hash lets you prove at any
// later time that nothing changed; all analysis happens on an isolated copy.
```

```python
# ❌ VULNERABLE PRACTICE — Python: opening evidence read-write (or in place)
with open("/var/incidents/suspect.bin", "r+b") as f:   # WHY WRONG: r+b can modify the original;
    data = f.read()                                     # even metadata/atime changes hurt custody

# ✅ SECURE PRACTICE — Python: hash, copy, analyze the copy
import hashlib, shutil
with open("/var/incidents/suspect.bin", "rb") as f:     # read-only
    digest = hashlib.sha256(f.read()).hexdigest()
open("/var/incidents/suspect.bin.sha256", "w").write(digest)   # record integrity
shutil.copy2("/var/incidents/suspect.bin", "/tmp/working-copy.bin")  # analyze the copy
# WHY IT WORKS: original untouched + recorded hash = provable integrity; analysis on the copy.
```

### 🌍 Case Study — BTK Killer Caught by Metadata (2005)
After decades evading capture, the "BTK" serial killer sent police a floppy disk. Forensic examination of a deleted Word document on it recovered **metadata** — an embedded author name ("Dennis") and a reference to a church — that led directly to his arrest and conviction. The case is a forensics classic for two reasons relevant here: (1) **"deleted" is not gone** — recoverable artifacts and embedded metadata persist far beyond what users assume; and (2) rigorous, documented examination of a faithfully preserved copy turned a tiny artifact into court-grade evidence. For developers, it's also a privacy lesson: the files your app generates carry metadata (authors, GPS, timestamps) you may be leaking — strip it.

### 🧩 Try It Yourself

**Challenge 1:** A server is actively compromised. Why might powering it off "to be safe" destroy your most valuable evidence, and what should you collect first?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

Powering off wipes **RAM**, which holds the most volatile and often most valuable evidence: running malicious processes, network connections to the attacker's C2, decryption keys, and in-memory-only ("fileless") malware (Module 4). Per the **order of volatility**, you should first capture a **memory image** and current network/process state, *then* image the disk. Only after volatile evidence is preserved (and hashed) do you consider isolation/shutdown — and isolation (pull from network) is usually preferable to a hard power-off precisely to preserve RAM.

</details>

**Challenge 2:** Why do investigators compute and record a hash of evidence *before* analysis and verify it *after*?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Reveal</strong></summary>

The hash is a tamper-evidence seal. Recording the SHA-256 of the original (or image) at collection time, then re-hashing after handling, proves the evidence was **not altered** during analysis — a matching hash demonstrates integrity, which is essential for both court admissibility and internal trust. A mismatch reveals that something changed, invalidating the evidence. It's the same one-way-hash property from Module 3 used as an integrity seal rather than a password defense.

</details>

### 🛡️ Pitfalls & False-Confidence Traps
- **Investigating on the live original.** Every action changes timestamps/disk/RAM. Image first, analyze the copy.
- **Powering off before capturing RAM.** Destroys volatile evidence. Follow order of volatility.
- **Skipping hashes / chain of custody.** Without them, evidence can't be proven unaltered and is worthless in disputes.
- **Believing "deleted" means gone.** Files, slack space, and metadata are routinely recoverable; sensitive data needs secure erasure.
- **Leaking metadata in your own app's files.** Documents/images carry authors, GPS, and timestamps — strip them server-side before serving user uploads.

### ❓ Quiz
1. What is the order of volatility, and why does it dictate collection sequence?
2. Why must you analyze a forensic *image*, not the original, and what role does a write blocker play?
3. How do hashes establish evidence integrity?
4. Why is "I deleted the file" not the same as "the data is gone"?
5. What is chain of custody and why does it matter even for internal investigations?

<details>
<summary><strong>🔓 Answers</strong></summary>

1. The principle of collecting the most ephemeral evidence first — RAM, running processes, network connections — before durable evidence like disk, because volatile data is lost on power-off or over time. It dictates capturing memory/state before imaging disks.
2. Because analyzing the original alters it (timestamps, writes, possible malware triggers), destroying its evidentiary value. You image it bit-for-bit and work on the copy; a write blocker physically prevents any write to the original during imaging.
3. By computing a cryptographic hash (e.g., SHA-256) of the original/image at collection and re-computing it later: a matching hash proves the data is byte-identical and unaltered; a mismatch proves tampering. It's a one-way integrity seal.
4. Deletion typically just removes the directory pointer; the data blocks persist until overwritten, and metadata/journals remain. Forensic tools recover deleted files and artifacts routinely — true removal requires secure erasure or crypto-shredding.
5. Chain of custody is the documented record of who handled the evidence, when, and how. It matters because it proves the evidence wasn't tampered with or substituted; without it, conclusions are untrustworthy in court and internally, regardless of what the analysis found.

</details>

- [ ] Ran Volatility 3 on a sample memory image (pslist/netscan/malfind)
- [ ] Hashed evidence before and after and verified it was unchanged
- [ ] I can explain order of volatility and why RAM is captured first

Module 23 — **Incident Response**. Forensics answers "what happened"; incident response is the *process* wrapped around it — detect, contain, eradicate, recover, learn — so an organization survives a breach with minimal damage and improves afterward.
